Lectures on stochastic sewing with applications
Oleg Butkovsky

Abstract
These are lecture notes for a mini-course on stochastic sewing, taught at the University of Edinburgh and Beijing Institute of Technology in Spring/Summer 2025. The aim is to introduce the reader to stochastic sewing techniques and to show how they can be successfully applied to study various problems in stochastic analysis, including: regularization by noise for stochastic differential equations driven by Brownian motion or fractional Brownian motion, well-posedness of stochastic PDEs with irregular drift, the study of averaging operators and local times, and the analysis of numerical algorithms.



1
Introduction

The stochastic sewing lemma (SSL) is a tool invented by Khoa Lê
[
Lê20
]
in 2018 for analyzing random perturbations of differential equations.
It is a stochastic generalization of the classical sewing lemma (Gubinelli
[
Gub04
]
, Feyel and De La Pradelle
[
FdLP06
]
). Since then, this research area has developed rapidly, and stochastic sewing (and its further extensions) has found numerous applications in stochastic PDEs, rough stochastic differential equations, rough path theory, stochastic numerics, the study of fast–slow systems, and related topics. The goal of these lecture notes is to provide the first systematic study of stochastic sewing techniques and their applications, combining recent developments and highlighting the main ideas.

For the convenience of the reader, we present the key techniques and methods in the simplest possible yet nontrivial setting. This way the technical calculations are greatly reduced, while the reader can still fully grasp the main ideas and arguments. Further extensions are left as exercises.

It turns out that the problems described above have the following common feature: they rely on accurate bounds of integrals of the form

\int f(\text{noise}(t)+\text{perturbation}(t))\,dt,\qquad\int f(\text{noise}(t)+\text{perturbation}(t))\,d\,\text{noise}(t),

(1.1)

where
f
is a measurable, non-smooth function or even a Schwartz distribution, the “noise” is a given highly irregular process with a known law, and the “perturbation” is a process whose law is unknown but is more regular (in some sense) than the noise. Such bounds are sometimes called Krylov-type bounds. The key point is that although
f
itself may be quite rough, its integral along the noise is much more regular, thanks to the averaging effect of the noise.

When the noise is a finite-dimensional Brownian motion, such bounds can be obtained using the so-called Veretennikov–Zvonkin transformation method, as beautifully explained in
[
Fla11
]
. However, the method strongly relies on a good Itô’s formula. Therefore, its application is very difficult or even impossible if the noise is a fractional Brownian motion or in the context of stochastic PDEs

Stochastic sewing has emerged as a very efficient alternative to the Veretennikov–Zvonkin transformation method, as it does not rely on the Markov or semimartingale property of the noise and is applicable to a broad range of processes.

Let us stress that the stochastic sewing lemma is a very flexible technique that can be adapted to the specific problem at hand. This will be the main guiding principle for these lecture notes: to show the reader that even when the original SSL is not enough to obtain a certain result, a suitable modification may work. We begin with SSL and apply it to an initial problem where it suffices. We then proceed to more challenging problems, and whenever the current set of tools is no longer sufficient, we introduce a new tool and use it together with the previous ones to overcome the next difficulty. In this way the collection of methods gradually grows, and we will also point out open problems for which the present set of tools still falls short.

Thus, we start with the strong well-posedness of SDEs driven by Brownian or fractional Brownian motion with Hölder drift (
Section

3
), where the original SSL is sufficient. We then move to weak well-posedness (uniqueness in law) for SDEs with distributional drift, where SSL alone is no longer enough, and we introduce generalized coupling techniques (
Section

4
) to handle this challenge. Next, we consider the rate of convergence of the Euler scheme for SDEs with irregular drift. When the noise is Brownian and the drift is Hölder continuous, SSL still applies directly (
Section

5.1
). We then study SDEs driven by a Lévy process: to establish well-posedness, we introduce the shifted stochastic sewing lemma (
Section

5.6
), and to analyze the rate of convergence of the Euler scheme, we additionally rely on the John-Nirenberg inequality (
Section

5.7
). Finally, we look at SDEs with Sobolev drifts, for which we introduce the taming singularities lemma (
Section

5.8
).

Each chapter is accompanied by exercises. Some of them provide a path to the proof of classical results which we use in the lecture note, while others focus on further applications of stochastic sewing.

Comments on literature.

Section

2
is based on
[
Gub04
,
FdLP06
,
Lê20
]
,
Section

3
follows the approach of
[
Lê20
]
,
Section

4
builds on
[
BM24
]
, and
Section

5
draws on
[
BDG21
,
BDG22
,
Ger22
,
DGL23
]
.

Further directions
. These lecture notes are necessarily limited in scope, and it is not possible to present all the interesting problems where stochastic sewing ideas have found effective applications. To give the reader at least a glimpse of this broader landscape, we briefly mention a few further directions here.

First, one can use stochastic sewing to study well-posedness and qualitative properties of a variety of models. Examples include rough and Young differential equations
[
MP24
,
MM23
]
, stochastic McKean–Vlasov equations
[
GHM23
]
, and stochastic PDEs
[
ABLM24a
,
ABLM24b
,
DHL24
,
DGK24
]
. Further applications to SDEs, such as the existence of a flow of solutions and Malliavin differentiability, can be found in
[
GG25
,
HP21
,
BG23
]
. Equations with multiplicative noise are treated in
[
DGLL24
]
.

Second,
[
HL20
]
uses the stochastic sewing lemma to analyze slow–fast systems in which the slow component is driven by a fractional Brownian motion.

Third,
[
FHL21
,
BCN24
,
BFS24
]
introduce and study an entirely new class of equations, called
Rough SDEs
, where stochastic sewing is a key ingredient of the proofs.

Additional applications include the study of local times of stochastic processes
[
BLM25
]
and the analysis of densities of solutions
[
AGRT25
]
, where stochastic sewing is combined with Romito’s lemma
[
Rom18
]
.

Convention on constants
. Throughout the paper,
C
denotes a positive constant whose value may change from line to line; its dependence is always specified in the corresponding statement.

The lecture notes are based upon work supported by the National Science Foundation under Grant No. DMS-2424139, while the author was in residence at the Simons Laufer Mathematical Sciences Institute in Berkeley, California, during the Fall 2025 semester.

1.1
Example 1: Regularization by noise for SDEs

It has been known for a long time that an ill-posed deterministic system can become well-posed when randomly perturbed. Indeed,
consider a differential equation

dX_{t}=\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\sign(X_{t})|X_{t}|^{\alpha}dt,\quad X_{0}=0,

(1.2)

where
\alpha\in(0,1)
. It is easy to see that it has infinitely many solutions: for example one can fix any
t_{0}>0
and take
X_{t}=\mathbbm{1}_{t\geq t_{0}}(t-t_{0})^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}}
,
t\geq 0
. It is also clear that the equation

dX_{t}=-\sign(X_{t})dt,\quad X_{0}=0

has no solutions. On the other hand, if we perturb these equations by a random Brownian forcing, that is if we consider an SDE

dX_{t}=b(X_{t})\,dt+dW_{t},\quad X_{0}=x,

(1.3)

where
d\in\mathbb{N}
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
W
is a
d
-dimensional standard Brownian motion, then this equation has a unique solution when the drift
b
is merely bounded measurable.
This phenomenon is called
regularization by noise
; we refer the
reader to monograph
[
Fla11
]
and a short note
[
DER24
]
for many interesting examples and further discussion.

The intuition behind regularization by noise is that the stochastic perturbation prevents the solution from getting stuck at points where the deterministic dynamics are singular. For example, the dynamics of equation (
1.2
) allow the solution to remain at zero for an arbitrary amount of time, move up, or move down. Adding a noise term creates fluctuations that immediately push the process away from these unstable points, effectively selecting a unique trajectory.

Let us be a bit more precise and recall the standard definitions. Fix
d\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
X\colon\Omega\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a continuous process. We say that a couple
(X,W)
on a complete filtered probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,1]},\mathsf{P}
) is a
weak solution
to SDE (
1.3
), if
W
is an
(\mathcal{F}_{t})
-Brownian motion,
X
is adapted to
(\mathcal{F}_{t})
, and

X_{t}=x+\int_{0}^{t}b(X_{r})\,dr+W_{t},\quad\text{a.s. for $t\in[0,1]$}.

A weak solution
(X,W)
is called a
strong solution
if
X
is adapted to
(\mathcal{F}^{W}_{t})
, that is the completion of the filtration generated by
W
. We say that
strong (pathwise) uniqueness
holds for (
1.3
) if for any two weak solutions of (
1.3
)
(X,W)
and

(Y,W)
with common noise
W
on a common probability space one has
\mathsf{P}(X_{t}=Y_{t}\text{ for all $t\in[0,1]$})=1
.

A seminal result of Veretennikov and Zvonkin states that regularization by noise happens for any bounded measurable drift. We refer to
[
KR05
]
for further extensions.

Theorem 1.1

(
[
Ver80
,
Zvo74
]
)
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
b\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded measurable function. Then SDE (
1.3
)
has a unique strong solution.

Establishing this result using stochastic sewing will be our primary goal in the first part of the course. Note that
[
Ver80
,
Zvo74
]
employed a very different proof strategy, namely a suitable transformation of the equation. Although their method is very powerful, it relies heavily on Itô’s calculus and PDE techniques, which are largely inapplicable beyond the semimartingale setting, for instance when the noise is a fractional Brownian motion or space–time white noise. In contrast, stochastic sewing is much more general and requires minimal assumptions on the driving noise.

Let us understand the main difficulty in proving
Theorem

1.1
and see what we need to overcome it.

Fix
d\in\mathbb{N}
. For a function
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,
\gamma\in(0,1]
define Hölder norms and seminorms:

\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}:=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}|f(x)|;\qquad[f]_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}:=\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|f(x)-f(y)|}{|x-y|^{\gamma}};\qquad\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}:=\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}+[f]_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}.

Denote by
\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}
the space of all bounded continuous functions
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

{\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}<\infty}
.

Let
X=:W+\varphi
,
Y=:W+\psi
be two solutions to the SDE (
1.3
) with the initial condition
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We would like to show that
X=Y
. A naive attempt to prove this is to write

|X_{t}-Y_{t}|=|\varphi_{t}-\psi_{t}|=\Bigl|\int_{0}^{t}(b(W_{r}+\varphi_{r})-b(W_{r}+\psi_{r}))\,dr\Bigr|\leq[b]_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\int_{0}^{t}|\varphi_{r}-\psi_{r}|^{\gamma}\,dr,\quad t\in[0,1].

(1.4)

However, this does not give much: if
\gamma=1
, Gronwall’s lemma implies that
X_{t}-Y_{t}\equiv 0
, but for
\gamma<1
(our main case of interest), we obtain nothing. Instead, we aim to replace the bound in (
1.4
) with the following:

\Bigl\|\int_{0}^{t}(b(W_{r}+\varphi_{r})-b(W_{r}+\psi_{r}))\,dr\Bigr\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}\leq C[b]_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}t^{\rho}\sup_{r\leq t}\|\varphi_{r}-\psi_{r}\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)},\quad t\in[0,T]

(1.5)

for some
\rho>0
. This is exactly a bound of the type (
1.1
)! At the very least, as a starting point, we would like to show that for
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\Bigl\|\int_{0}^{t}(b(W_{r}+x)-b(W_{r}+y))\,dr\Bigr\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle???}}{{\leq}}C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}t^{\rho}|x-y|,\quad t\in[0,T]

(1.6)

We establish bounds (
1.5
) and (
1.6
) in
Section

3
and their extension to the fractional Brownian case will be done in
3.13
,
3.14
and
3.15
.

Section

4
is devoted to
weak
regularization by noise, that is, uniqueness in law of solutions to (
1.3
) with even less regular drifts: we assume that
b
is a Schwartz distribution. Here a different stochastic sewing-type argument will be needed as for such irregular
b
bounds
(
1.5
) and (
1.6
) do not hold. We will prove the following result.

Theorem 1.2

(
[
ZZ17
,
FIR17
]
)
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
b\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\gamma>-\frac{1}{2}
. Then SDE (
1.3
)
has a weak solution
(X,W)
. If
(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{W})
is another weak solution to (
1.3
) belonging to a certain natural class, then
\law(X)=\law(\widetilde{X})
.

We refer to
Section

4.1
for the precise definitions, including the definition of the space
\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}
for negative
\gamma
. Let us note here that the technique of
[
ZZ17
,
FIR17
]
was again the Zvonkin transformation method and thus relies on the Markov properties of the noise. We will show how this result can be established using stochastic sewing, which has to be combined with some ideas from ergodic theory. Since stochastic sewing does not require a “good” Itô formula to be applicable, the same ideas also lead to weak uniqueness for SDEs driven by a fractional Brownian motion and for stochastic PDEs.

Finally, in
Section

5
we see how stochastic sewing works for SDEs driven by a discontinuous driver. Let
\alpha\in(0,2)
. Recall that
L\colon\Omega\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a symmetric
\alpha
-stable process if it is càdlàg, has independent stationary increments and

\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt\exp(i\lambda L_{t})=\exp(-c_{\alpha,d}|\lambda|^{\alpha}t),\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\,t\in[0,1]

(1.7)

for some
c_{\alpha,d}>0
. We will prove the following result.

Theorem 1.3

(
[
Pri12
,
CZZ17
,
CSZ18
]
)
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\alpha\in(0,2)
. Suppose that

\gamma>1-\frac{\alpha}{2}.

(1.8)

Let
b\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then SDE

X_{t}=x+\int_{0}^{t}b(X_{r})\,dr+L_{t}

(1.9)

has a unique strong solution.

We note again that the above-mentioned papers
[
Pri12
,
CZZ17
,
CSZ18
]
established this result using a Zvonkin transformation method, whereas in
Section

5
we prove it directly using stochastic sewing combined with a number of new tools.

1.2
Example 2: Numerical methods

Next, once the well-posedness of the SDE (
1.3
) is established, let us discuss how this equation can be solved numerically. It turns out that stochastic sewing techniques are also very useful for analyzing numerical algorithms.

We consider the SDE (
1.3
) and study the convergence of the standard Euler scheme. That is, we fix
n\in\mathbb{N}
, consider a partition
\{0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\dots<t_{n}=1\}
of the interval
[0,1]
and define recursively the process

X_{t_{i+1}}^{n}=X_{t_{i}}^{n}+(t_{i+1}-t_{i})b(X^{n}_{t_{i}})+(W_{t_{i+1}}-W_{t_{i}}),\quad i=0,\ldots,n-1.

(1.10)

To simplify the notation we work only on uniform grids, so
t_{i}=i/n
. For an integer
n\geq 1
denote

\kappa_{n}(t):=\frac{\lfloor nt\rfloor}{n},\quad t\in[0,1],

(1.11)

that is the largest point of the grid
\{0,\frac{1}{n},\frac{2}{n},\ldots\}
which is smaller or equal than
t
. Then we can rewrite the scheme (
1.10
) as

dX^{n}_{t}=b(X^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(t)})\,dt+\,dW_{t},\qquad X_{0}=x_{0},\quad t\in[0,1].

(1.12)

Let
b\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}
,
\gamma\in(0,1]
. It is clear that for any
t\in[0,1]
we have the following naive bound:

\displaystyle\Bigl|X^{n}_{t}-X_{t}\Bigr|

\displaystyle=\Bigl|\int_{0}^{t}b(X^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r)})-b(X_{r})\,dr\Bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\Bigl|\int_{0}^{t}b(X^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r)})-b(X^{n}_{r})\,dr\Bigr|+\Bigl|\int_{0}^{t}b(X^{n}_{r})-b(X_{r})\,dr\Bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\int_{0}^{t}|X^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r)}-X^{n}_{r}|^{\gamma}\,dr+\Bigl|\int_{0}^{t}b(X^{n}_{r})-b(X_{r})\,dr\Bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}n^{-\gamma}+\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\int_{0}^{t}|W_{\kappa_{n}(r)}-W_{r}|^{\gamma}\,dr+\Bigl|\int_{0}^{t}b(X^{n}_{r})-b(X_{r})\,dr\Bigr|.

Since
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt|W_{\kappa_{n}(r)}-W_{r}|^{\gamma}=Cn^{-\gamma/2}
, we see that the best rate one can obtain with the naive approach (even ignoring all other terms) is
n^{-\gamma/2}
. In
Section

5.1
, we will use stochastic sewing to improve this rate by
1/2
. More precisely, we will show the following result.

Theorem 1.4

(
[
BDG21
]
)
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\geq 1
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\gamma,\varepsilon>0
,
b\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Let
X
solve (
1.3
) and
X^{n}
be its Euler scheme (
1.12
). Then there exists a constant
C=C(\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}},\gamma,\varepsilon,d,m)>0
such that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\|\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|X_{t}-X^{n}_{t}|\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq Cn^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{2}+\varepsilon}.

(1.13)

Comparing this result with
Theorem

1.1
, we see that
Theorem

1.4
establishes convergence in the entire regime (up to a loss of an arbitrary
\varepsilon>0
) of
\gamma
for which uniqueness of a strong solution is known. Furthermore, the new convergence rate does not deteriorate to
0
as the regularity of the drift approaches the threshold
\gamma=0
, and it is at least
n^{-\frac{1}{2}}
. Moreover, it is known that this rate is optimal
[
EMGY25
]
; however, showing the optimality is out of the scope of these lecture notes.

In
Section

5.2
–
Section

5.7
, we extend this result to SDEs driven by a jump process. This requires a significant extension of the stochastic sewing arguments and the introduction of several new tools. In the end, we will prove the following bound on the convergence rate.

Theorem 1.5

(
[
BDG22
]
)
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\geq 1
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\alpha\in(1,2)
,
\varepsilon>0
. Let
b\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
, where
\gamma
satisfies condition (
1.8
). Let
X
be a solution to (
1.9
), and let
X^{n}
be the corresponding Euler scheme

dX^{n}_{t}=b(X^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(t)})\,dt+\,dL_{t},\qquad X_{0}=x_{0},\quad t\geq 0.

Then there exists a constant
C=C(\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}},\alpha,\gamma,\varepsilon,d,m)>0
such that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\|\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|X_{t}-X^{n}_{t}|\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq Cn^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}+\varepsilon}.

We see again that for
\alpha\in(1,2)
,
Theorem

1.5
provides a convergence rate in the entire range of
\gamma
where strong well-posedness is known.

Finally, in
5.23
–
5.30
we will study numerics for SDEs with Sobolev rather than Hölder drifts. This will require further generalization of stochastic sewing.

2
Sewing and stochastic sewing

We begin with the presentation of the sewing lemma and its stochastic analogue. For
0\leq S<T
, consider the simplices

\Delta_{[S,T]}:=\{(s,t)\in[S,T]^{2}\colon s<t\};\quad\Delta^{3}_{[S,T]}:=\{(s,u,t)\in[S,T]^{3}\colon s<u<t\}.

If
A_{\cdot,\cdot}
is a function
\Delta_{[S,T]}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then we put

\delta A_{s,u,t}:=A_{s,t}-A_{s,u}-A_{u,t},\quad(s,u,t)\in\Delta^{3}_{[S,T]}.

(2.1)

The next statement is the celebrated sewing lemma, obtained independently
by Feyel and De La Pradelle
[
FdLP06
]
and Gubinelli
[
Gub04
]
.

Theorem 2.1

(Sewing lemma,
[
FdLP06
,
Gub04
]
)
.

Let
E
be a Banach space,
0\leq S\leq T
. Suppose that there exist functions
\mathcal{A}\colon[S,T]\to E
,
A\colon\Delta_{[S,T]}\to E
, such that the following holds:

1.

for any sequence of partitions
\Pi_{N}:=\{S=t^{N}_{0},...,t^{N}_{k(N)}=T\}
of
[S,T]
with
|\Pi_{N}|\to 0
one has

\mathcal{A}_{T}-\mathcal{A}_{S}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\sum_{i=0}^{k(N)-1}A_{t^{N}_{i},t_{i+1}^{N}};

(2.2)

2.

there exist
\gamma>1
,
\Gamma>0
such that for every
(s,u,t)\in\Delta^{3}_{[S,T]}
we have

\|\delta A_{s,u,t}\|_{E}\leq\Gamma|t-s|^{\gamma}.

(2.3)

Then there exists a constant
C=C(\gamma)>0
independent of
S
,
T
,
\Gamma
such that

\|\mathcal{A}_{T}-\mathcal{A}_{S}\|_{E}\leq\|A_{S,T}\|_{E}+C\Gamma|T-S|^{\gamma}.

(2.4)

Remark 2.2
.

Actually, condition (
2.2
) can be omitted. One can show that condition (ii) of the lemma alone implies that the Riemann sum
\sum A_{t^{N}_{i},t_{i+1}^{N}}
always converges and that its limit satisfies (
2.4
), see, e.g.,
[
FH20
, Lemma 4.2]
. However, to significantly simplify the proof, and since we will only apply the sewing lemma in this form, we keep condition (
2.2
).

Proof of
Theorem

2.1
.

Fix
0\leq S\leq T
. For
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, consider a dyadic partition
t^{n}_{i}=S+\frac{i}{2^{n}}(T-S)
,
i=0,1,\ldots,2^{n}
, of the interval
[S,T]
. Denote the
n
th Riemann sum by

A^{(n)}:=\sum_{i=0}^{2^{n}-1}A_{t_{i}^{n},t_{i+1}^{n}}.

(2.5)

Then, by (
2.2
) we have

\|\mathcal{A}_{T}-\mathcal{A}_{S}\|_{E}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\|A^{(n)}\|_{E}\leq\|A_{S,T}\|_{E}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\|A^{(n+1)}-A^{(n)}\|_{E}.

(2.6)

Using condition (
2.3
), it is easy to see that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}

\|A^{(n+1)}-A^{(n)}\|_{E}=\Bigl\|\sum_{i=0}^{2^{n}-1}\delta A_{t_{i}^{n},t_{2i+1}^{n+1},t_{i+1}^{n}}\Bigr\|_{E}\leq\sum_{i=0}^{2^{n}-1}\bigl\|\delta A_{t_{i}^{n},t_{2i+1}^{n+1},t_{i+1}^{n}}\bigr\|_{E}\leq\Gamma|T-S|^{\gamma}2^{-n(\gamma-1)}.

Summing this over
n\in\mathbb{N}
(recall that
\gamma>1
by assumption) and substituting into (
2.6
), we finally get

\|\mathcal{A}_{T}-\mathcal{A}_{S}\|_{E}\leq\|A_{S,T}\|_{E}+C\Gamma|T-S|^{\gamma},

for
C=C(\gamma)
which is the desired bound (
2.4
).
∎

The sewing lemma is crucial in the context of rough path theory, see, e.g.,
[
FH20
]
. We can also try to apply it for our purposes. By taking
E=L_{m}(\Omega)
,
m\geq 1
, in
Theorem

2.1
, we see that we need to verify the following condition on increments

\|\delta A_{s,u,t}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq\Gamma|t-s|^{\gamma}

(2.7)

for
\gamma>1
. However, this condition can be significantly improved if we switch to the stochastic setting. As an analogy, recall that to define the deterministic integral
\int f_{s}dg_{s}
, where
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}
,
g\in\mathcal{C}^{\beta}
one generally requires
{\alpha+\beta>1}
, whereas the stochastic integral
\int f_{s}dW_{s}
can be defined under much weaker regularity assumptions due to stochastic cancellations.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathsf{P})
be a probability space,
T>0
. If
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
is a complete filtration, then we write

\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{t}[\cdot]:=\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt[\cdot|\mathcal{F}_{t}],\quad t\in[S,T].

We will also frequently use the following inequality, which follows directly from Jensen’s inequality: if
m\geq 1
,
X\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a random variable, and
\mathcal{G}\subset\mathcal{F}
is a
\sigma
-algebra, then

\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt[X|\mathcal{G}]\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq\|X\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}.

(2.8)

We are now ready to discover the central tool of these lecture notes: the stochastic sewing lemma of Lê.

Theorem 2.3

(Stochastic sewing lemma,
[
Lê20
]
)
.

Let
m\in[2,\infty)
,
0\leq S\leq T
.
Suppose that there exist measurable functions
{\mathcal{A}\colon\Omega\times[S,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}}
,
A\colon\Omega\times\Delta_{[S,T]}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and a complete filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[S,T]}
such that the following holds:

1.

for any sequence of partitions
\Pi_{N}:=\{S=t^{N}_{0},...,t^{N}_{k(N)}=T\}
of
[S,T]
with
|\Pi_{N}|\to 0
one has

\sum_{i=0}^{k(N)-1}A_{t^{N}_{i},t_{i+1}^{N}}\to\mathcal{A}_{T}-\mathcal{A}_{S}\quad\text{in probability as $N\to\infty$};

(2.9)

2.

for any
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[S,T]}
, the random variable
A_{s,t}
is
\mathcal{F}_{t}
–measurable;

3.

there exists
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}\geq 0
,
\gamma_{1}>\frac{1}{2}
,
\gamma_{2}>1
such that for every
(s,u,t)\in\Delta^{3}_{[S,T]}
we have

\displaystyle\|\delta A_{s,u,t}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq\Gamma_{1}|t-s|^{\gamma_{1}},

(2.10)

\displaystyle\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\delta A_{s,u,t}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq\Gamma_{2}|t-s|^{\gamma_{2}}.

(2.11)

Then there exists a constant
C=C(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},d,m)
independent of
S
,
T
,
\Gamma_{1}
,
\Gamma_{2}
such that

\|\mathcal{A}_{T}-\mathcal{A}_{S}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq\|A_{S,T}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}+C\Gamma_{1}|T-S|^{\gamma_{1}}+C\Gamma_{2}|T-S|^{\gamma_{2}}.

(2.12)

Remark 2.4
.

It is interesting to compare the conditions (
2.10
) and (
2.11
) of the stochastic sewing lemma with condition (
2.7
). We observe that assumption (
2.11
), compared to (
2.7
), additionally involves a conditional expectation. As we will see below,
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\delta A_{s,u,t}
is often much smaller than
\delta A_{s,u,t}
. We also note that the exponent
\gamma_{1}
in condition (
2.10
) is assumed to be greater than
1/2
, whereas the corresponding parameter
\gamma
in (
2.7
) was required to be greater than
1
. All this will be crucial in our applications of the stochastic sewing lemma later.

To prove the stochastic sewing lemma, we recall the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality (BDG-inequality).

Proposition 2.5

(BDG-inequality,
[
Bur66
,
BDG72
]
)
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
(\mathcal{F}_{i})_{i=0,\ldots,n}
be a filtration. Let
(X_{i},\mathcal{F}_{i})_{i=1,\ldots,n}
be a sequence of martingale differences, that is each
X_{i}
is
\mathcal{F}_{i}
measurable and
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt(X_{i}|\mathcal{F}_{i-1})=0
. Then for any
m\geq 1
there exists a constant
C=C(m)>0
so that

\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt\Bigl|\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\Bigr|^{m}\leq C\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt\Bigl[\bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}|X_{i}|^{2}\bigr)^{\frac{m}{2}}\Bigr]

It is easy to see that it follows from the BDG-inequality, that if
m\geq 2
, then in the setting of
Proposition

2.5
we have

\Bigl\|\sum_{i=0}^{n}X_{i}\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq C\Bigl\|\sum_{i=0}^{n}|X_{i}|^{2}\Bigr\|_{L_{\frac{m}{2}}(\Omega)}^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq C\Bigl(\sum_{i=0}^{n}\|X_{i}\|^{2}_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}},

(2.13)

where
C=C(m)>0
.

Proof of
Theorem

2.3
.

Fix
m\geq 2
,
0\leq S\leq T
.
As before, we consider the dyadic partition
t^{n}_{i}=S+\frac{i}{2^{n}}(T-S)
,
i=0,1,\ldots,2^{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
of the interval
[S,T]
. We define
A^{(n)}
as in (
2.5
) and note that by Fatou’s lemma

\|\mathcal{A}_{T}-\mathcal{A}_{S}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\|A^{(n)}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq\|A_{S,T}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\|A^{(n+1)}-A^{(n)}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}.

(2.14)

We estimate the difference of
A^{(n+1)}-A^{(n)}
using the BDG-inequality and conditions (
2.10
), (
2.11
). We get for
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}

\displaystyle\|A^{(n+1)}-A^{(n)}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\,\,=\Bigl\|\sum_{i=0}^{2^{n}-1}\delta A_{t_{i}^{n},t_{2i+1}^{n+1},t_{i+1}^{n}}\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\,\,\leq\Bigl\|\sum_{i=0}^{2^{n}-1}(\delta A_{t_{i}^{n},t_{2i+1}^{n+1},t_{i+1}^{n}}-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{t_{i}^{n}}\delta A_{t_{i}^{n},t_{2i+1}^{n+1},t_{i+1}^{n}})\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}+\Bigl\|\sum_{i=0}^{2^{n}-1}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{t_{i}^{n}}\delta A_{t_{i}^{n},t_{2i+1}^{n+1},t_{i+1}^{n}}\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\,\,\leq C\Bigl(\sum_{i=0}^{2^{n}-1}\bigl\|\delta A_{t_{i}^{n},t_{2i+1}^{n+1},t_{i+1}^{n}}\!\!-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{t_{i}^{n}}\delta A_{t_{i}^{n},t_{2i+1}^{n+1},t_{i+1}^{n}}\bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}^{2}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}\!+\!\sum_{i=0}^{2^{n}-1}\bigl\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{t_{i}^{n}}\delta A_{t_{i}^{n},t_{2i+1}^{n+1},t_{i+1}^{n}}\bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

(2.15)

\displaystyle\,\,\leq C\bigl(2^{n}\Gamma_{1}^{2}2^{-2n\gamma_{1}}(T-S)^{2\gamma_{1}}\bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}+C\Gamma_{2}|T-S|^{\gamma_{2}}2^{-n\gamma_{2}}2^{n}

(2.16)

\displaystyle\,\,\leq C\Gamma_{1}2^{-n(\gamma_{1}-\frac{1}{2})}(T-S)^{\gamma_{1}}+C\Gamma_{2}|T-S|^{\gamma_{2}}2^{-n(\gamma_{2}-1)},

(2.17)

for
C=C(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},d,m)>0
. Here in (
2.15
) we used that the sequence

\{\delta A_{t_{i}^{n},t_{2i+1}^{n+1},t_{i+1}^{n}}-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{t_{i}}\delta A_{t_{i}^{n},t_{2i+1}^{n+1},t_{i+1}^{n}},\mathcal{F}_{t_{i+1}^{n}}\}_{i=0,\ldots,2^{n}-1}

is a martingale difference sequence, and we apply the BDG inequality (
2.13
). Inequality (
2.16
) followed from elementary inequality
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2(a^{2}+b^{2})
valid for any
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
and (
2.8
).
Summing (
2.17
) over
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
and substituting into (
2.14
), we finally get

\|\mathcal{A}_{T}-\mathcal{A}_{S}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq\|A_{S,T}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}+C\Gamma_{1}(T-S)^{\gamma_{1}}+C\Gamma_{2}|T-S|^{\gamma_{2}}.

for
C=C(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},d,m)>0
which is (
2.12
).
∎

Remark 2.6
.

To verify condition (
2.10
) it is sufficient to check that there exist constants
\Gamma_{1}\geq 0
,
\gamma_{1}>\frac{1}{2}
so that for any
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[S,T]}
one has

\|A_{s,t}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq\Gamma_{1}|t-s|^{\gamma_{1}}.

2.1
Exercises

We will use the Burkholder–Davis–Gundy inequality (
Proposition

2.5
) only in the regime
m\geq 2
. Let us prove it in this setting. The exercises in this subsection are inspired by
[
ZK24
, Section 1]
. We begin with Doob’s inequality, which reduces the problem of bounding the maximum of a martingale to bounding its terminal value.

Exercise 2.7

(Doob’s inequality)
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
m>1
. Let
\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}
be a filtration. Let
((M_{i})_{i=1,\ldots n},\mathbb{F})
be a martingale. Show that

\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt\max_{k\leq n}|M_{k}|^{m}\leq C\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt|M_{n}|^{m},

where the constant
C=C(m)>0
is independent of
n
.

Hints
: 1) Use the identity
x^{m}=C\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda^{m-1}\mathbbm{1}(\lambda<x)\,d\lambda
, where
C=C(m)>0
.

2) To bound
\mathsf{P}(\max_{k\leq n}|M_{k}|>\lambda)
, use the identity

\{\max_{k\leq n}|M_{k}|>\lambda\}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\{\max_{k\leq i-1}|M_{k}|<\lambda,|M_{i}|>\lambda\}.

and the inequality
\mathbbm{1}(|M_{i}|>\lambda)\leq\lambda^{-1}M_{i}\mathbbm{1}(|M_{i}|>\lambda)
.

3) First assume additionally that
M
is bounded by an arbitrarily large constant, and then apply Fatou’s lemma to remove this assumption.

A key step toward the proof of the BDG inequality is the following nice idea.

Exercise 2.8

(Garsia-Neveu’s lemma)
.

Let
\eta,\xi
be two nonnegative random variables Assume that for any
\lambda\geq 0

\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt(\eta-\lambda)^{2}\mathbbm{1}(\eta>\lambda)\leq\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt\xi^{2}\mathbbm{1}(\eta>\lambda).

Show that this implies
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt\eta^{m}\leq\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt\xi^{m}
for any
m\geq 2
.

Hint
: Use the identity
x^{m}=C\int_{0}^{x}(x-\lambda)^{2}\lambda^{m-3}\,d\lambda
, where
C=C(m)>0
.

Exercise 2.9

(Burkholder–Davis–Gundy inequality)
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_{i})_{0\leq i\leq n}
be a filtration. Let
(X_{i},\mathcal{F}_{i})_{i=1,\ldots,n}
be a sequence of martingale differences, that is each
X_{i}
is
\mathcal{F}_{i}
measurable and
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt(X_{i}|\mathcal{F}_{i-1})=0
.

1.

Show that
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt\bigl|\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\bigr|^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt|X_{i}|^{2}
.

2.

Use Garsia-Neveu’s lemma, part (i) of this exercise, and Doob’s inequality to show that for any
m>2

\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt\Bigl|\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\Bigr|^{m}\leq C\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt\Bigl[\bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}|X_{i}|^{2}\bigr)^{m/2}\Bigr],

where the constant
C=C(m)>0
is independent of
n
.

Hint
: At some point, you may want to use a decomposition similar to the one in Hint 2) of
2.7
.

3
Regularization by noise for SDEs driven by Brownian motion

Now let us see how the stochastic sewing lemma can be used to obtain uniqueness for SDEs with irregular drift. We will follow the plan sketched in
Section

1.1
: first, in
Section

3.1
, we establish the bound (
1.6
), then proceed to the proof of (
1.5
) in
Section

3.2
, and finally, in
Section

3.3
, prove
Theorem

1.1
(up to the loss of an arbitrarily small
\varepsilon>0
in the regularity of the drift).

We would like to stress that the argument will be quite general. For simplicity, and to highlight the main ideas, we illustrate it here for SDEs driven by Brownian motion and leave the extension to SDEs driven by fractional Brownian motion as an exercise to the reader; see
Section

3.4
. For other types of noise, the general proof strategy still applies, although further refinement of the stochastic sewing argument is required. We will identify which steps fail when the driving noise is a Lévy process and fix them in
Sections

5.3
,
5.5
,
5.6
and
5.4
.

In this section we fix a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathsf{P})
, and a complete filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
. Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
and let
W
be a
d
-dimensional standard
(\mathcal{F}_{t})
-Brownian motion. As we discussed above, our proof strategy will be quite flexible, and can be adapted to equations with other driving processes (fractional Brownian motion, Lévy process, space-time white noise and so on). The results in this section are mostly due to
[
Lê20
]
.

3.1
Step 1: Bounding integral functionals of a Brownian motion

We begin with the following technical but crucial lemma. Denote by
p_{t}
the density of a
d
-dimensional Gaussian vector with covariance matrix
t\mathbbm{1}_{d}
, and let
P_{t}
be the corresponding semigroup, that is

P_{t}f(x):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p_{t}(y)f(x-y)\,dy,\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

(3.1)

where
f
is a bounded measurable function
\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
,
\gamma\in[0,1]
. Then there exists
C=C(\gamma,d)>0
such that for any
t>0
we have

\|P_{t}f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}\leq Ct^{\frac{\gamma-1}{2}}\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}};\quad\|\nabla P_{t}f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}\leq Ct^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}.

(3.2)

Proof.

Assume additionally that
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
. Then for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\displaystyle\Bigl|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p_{t}(y)\nabla f(x-y)\,dy\Bigr|

\displaystyle=\Bigl|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla p_{t}(y)f(x-y)\,dy\Bigr|=\Bigl|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla p_{t}(y)(f(x-y)-f(x))\,dy\Bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\nabla p_{t}(y)||y|^{\gamma}\,dy\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}t^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p_{t}(y)|y|^{1+\gamma}\,dy

\displaystyle\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}t^{-1}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt|W_{t}|^{1+\gamma}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}t^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma}{2}},

for
C=C(d)>0
. Therefore for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

|P_{t}f(x)-P_{t}f(y)|=\Bigl|(x-y)\int_{0}^{1}\nabla P_{t}f(\theta x+(1-\theta)y)\,d\theta\Bigr|\leq C|x-y|\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}t^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma}{2}},

which yields the first inequality in (
3.2
) for
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
.

In general case,
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}
, we note that
P_{\varepsilon}f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
for any
\varepsilon>0
and therefore

\|P_{t}f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}=\|P_{t-\varepsilon}(P_{\varepsilon}f)\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}\leq C(t-\varepsilon)^{\frac{\gamma-1}{2}}\|P_{\varepsilon}f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\leq C(t-\varepsilon)^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}},

for
C=C(d)
. By passing to the limit as
\varepsilon\to 0
, we get the first part of (
3.2
).

To get the second part of (
3.2
), we apply the first part with
\gamma=0
. We get

\|\nabla P_{t}f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}=\|P_{\frac{t}{2}}\nabla P_{\frac{t}{2}}f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}\leq Ct^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|\nabla P_{\frac{t}{2}}f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}t^{-1+\frac{\gamma}{2}},

for
C=C(d)>0
, which implies the statement of the lemma.
∎

Consider now a function
b\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\gamma>0
. Recall that we would like to bound the moments of

\mathcal{A}_{t}:=\int_{0}^{t}(b(W_{r}+x)-b(W_{r}+y))\,dr,\quad x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\,\,t\in[0,T],

(3.3)

in terms of
|x-y|
(weaker bounds of the form
|x-y|^{\rho}
for
\rho<1
are not allowed).
We would like to use the SSL —
Theorem

2.3
. But how to do it? What should we choose as a germ
A_{s,t}
? A naive attempt would be to approximate for
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,T]}

\mathcal{A}_{t}-\mathcal{A}_{s}\approx(t-s)(b(W_{s}+x)-b(W_{s}+y)).

Recall, however, that the optimal approximation of
\mathcal{A}_{t}-\mathcal{A}_{s}
in the
L_{2}(\Omega)
sense by an
\mathcal{F}_{s}
-measurable random variable is given by the conditional expectation.

\mathcal{A}_{t}-\mathcal{A}_{s}\approx\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\int_{s}^{t}(b(W_{r}+x)-b(W_{r}+y))\,dr.

We will use this as our germ
A_{s,t}
. Thus, we are now ready to present the very first application of the SSL. Before we begin, let us observe that the following very useful identity holds: for any bounded measurable
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,
0\leq s<t
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}f(W_{t}+x)=P_{t-s}f(W_{s}+x)

(3.4)

Theorem 3.2
.

Let
m\geq 2
,
\gamma\in(0,1]
,
b\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
. Then there exists a constant
C=C(\gamma,d,m)>0
such that for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
(S,T)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
one has

\Bigl\|\int_{S}^{T}(b(W_{r}+x)-b(W_{r}+y))\,dr\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}|x-y||T-S|^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}.

(3.5)

Proof.

Fix
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
0\leq S\leq T
. Motivated by the above discussion, let us apply
Theorem

2.3
to the process
(\mathcal{A}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
defined in (
3.3
) and the germ

A_{s,t}:=\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\int_{s}^{t}(b(W_{r}+x)-b(W_{r}+y))\,dr,\quad(s,t)\in\Delta_{[S,T]}.

(3.6)

Let us verify that all the conditions of the SSL are satisfied.
First, we check (
2.11
) to gain an intuition why the SSL is useful. Recalling the notation (
2.1
), we have for
(s,u,t)\in\Delta^{3}_{S,T}

\delta A_{s,u,t}=(\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{u})\int_{u}^{t}(b(W_{r}+x)-b(W_{r}+y))\,dr.

This implies
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\delta A_{s,u,t}=0
and (
2.11
) holds. Next, using (
3.4
), we get

\displaystyle|A_{s,t}|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{s}^{t}\bigl|P_{r-s}b(W_{s}+x)-P_{r-s}b(W_{s}+y)\bigr|\,dr\leq C|x-y|\int_{s}^{t}\|P_{r-s}b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}\,dr

\displaystyle\leq C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}|x-y|(t-s)^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}.

where
C=C(\gamma,d)>0
and the last inequality follows from
Lemma

3.1
. This obviously implies

\|A_{s,t}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}|x-y|(t-s)^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}

(3.7)

and since
\gamma>0
, then condition (
2.10
) is satisfied (recall
Remark

2.6
).

Condition (ii) of
Theorem

2.3
is satisfied by the definition of
A_{s,t}
in (
3.6
).
Finally, let us check condition (
2.9
). For any partition
\Pi=\{S=t_{0}\leq t_{1}\leq\ldots\leq t_{k}=T\}
of
[S,T]
we apply the BDG inequality to derive

\displaystyle\Bigl\|\mathcal{A}_{T}-\mathcal{A}_{S}-\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}A_{t_{i},t_{i+1}}\Bigr\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}^{2}

\displaystyle=\Bigl\|\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}(\mathcal{A}_{t_{i+1}}-\mathcal{A}_{t_{i}}-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{t_{i}}(\mathcal{A}_{t_{i+1}}-\mathcal{A}_{t_{i}}))\Bigr\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\|\mathcal{A}_{t_{i+1}}-\mathcal{A}_{t_{i}}-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{t_{i}}(\mathcal{A}_{t_{i+1}}-\mathcal{A}_{t_{i}})\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}^{2}(t_{i+1}-t_{i})^{2}\leq\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}^{2}|\Pi||T-S|\to 0,

as
|\Pi|\to 0
. Thus, condition (
2.9
) is also satisfied.

Thus, all the assumptions of the SSL hold, and taking into account (
3.7
), we obtain from (
2.12
)

\Bigl\|\int_{S}^{T}(b(W_{r}+x)-b(W_{r}+y))\,dr\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}|x-y||T-S|^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma}{2}},

where
C=C(\gamma,d,m)>0
, which is (
3.5
).
∎

Remark 3.3
.

Now we see why the stochastic sewing lemma is more effective than the deterministic sewing lemma for bounding integral functionals of Brownian motion. Indeed, (
3.7
) implies
\|A_{s,t}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq C(t-s)^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}
. Therefore, to apply the SSL and verify condition (
2.10
), we only need to require
\gamma>0
. By contrast, the corresponding condition for the deterministic sewing lemma (condition (
2.7
)) fails unless
\gamma>1
, so the usual sewing lemma is absolutely useless here. Note also that the second SSL condition, (
2.11
), is totally harmless here since

\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\delta A_{s,u,t}\equiv 0
.

3.2
Step 2: Bounding integrals along a perturbed Brownian path

Our next step is to allow
x
and
y
in (
3.5
) to be random and time-dependent. However, we cannot simply take arbitrary stochastic processes
(x_{r})_{r\in[0,1]}
,
(y_{r})_{r\in[0,1]}
and expect (
3.5
) to remain valid. For example, setting in (
3.5
)
x_{r}:=-W_{r}+z
and
y_{r}:=-W_{r}
, where
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, would lead to the estimate
|b(z)-b(0)|\leq C|b|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}|z|
, which is clearly false for a generic
b\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}
. We must therefore assume that the perturbation is smoother (in a certain sense) than the noise.

Next question: how do we want to capture the smoothness of the perturbation? Of course, a natural approach would be to require that its
\mathcal{C}^{\tau}([0,1])
norm is a.s. finite for some
\tau>\frac{1}{2}
(recall that
W\in\mathcal{C}^{1/2-\varepsilon}
for any
\varepsilon>0
). However, since we aim to prove strong uniqueness via a Gronwall-type argument, it is much more convenient to first take the
L_{m}(\Omega)
-norm of the increment of the perturbation (
m\geq 2
), and then measure its
\mathcal{C}^{\tau}([0,1])
norm. This motivates the following definitions.

For
m\geq 1
,
(S,T)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
, a measurable function
f\colon\Omega\times[S,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\tau\in(0,1]
put

\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([S,T])}:=\sup_{t\in[S,T]}\|f(t)\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)};\,\,\,\,\,[f]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{m}([S,T])}:=\sup_{(s,t)\in\Delta_{[S,T]}}\frac{\|f(t)-f(s)\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}}{|t-s|^{\tau}}.\\

(3.8)

It follows from the definition that

\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([S,T])}\leq\|f_{S}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}+\sup_{t\in[S,T]}\|f(t)-f(S)\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq\|f_{S}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}+(T-S)^{\tau}[f]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{m}([S,T])}.

(3.9)

The following is a key bound for uniqueness.

Theorem 3.4
.

Let
m\geq 2
,
\gamma,\tau\in(0,1]
. Let
b\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
. Let
\psi,\psi\colon\Omega\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be continuous measurable functions adapted to
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
. Assume that

\frac{\gamma}{2}+\tau>\frac{1}{2}.

(3.10)

Suppose further that
there exists a constant
\Gamma_{0}\geq 0
such that a.s. for all
s,t\in\Delta_{[0,1]}

|\varphi_{t}-\varphi_{s}|\leq\Gamma_{0}|t-s|.

(3.11)

Then there exists a constant
C=C(\gamma,\tau,d,m)>0
such that
for any
(S,T)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
one has

\displaystyle\Bigl\|\int_{S}^{T}\bigl(b(W_{r}+\varphi_{r})-b(W_{r}+\psi_{r})\bigr)\,dr\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\,\,\,\leq C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(T-S)^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}\bigl((1+\Gamma_{0})\|\varphi-\psi\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([S,T])}+[\varphi-\psi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{m}([S,T])}(T-S)^{\tau}\bigr).

(3.12)

Theorem

3.4
brings us back to the discussion in the introduction, where we explained that to bound an integral functional of a stochastic process, we decompose the process into the sum of a very irregular noise about which we know everything (in this case,
W
) and a generic perturbation about which we do not know much, apart from the fact that it is much smoother than the noise, see condition (
3.11
) and note the appearance of the term
[\varphi-\psi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{m}([S,T])}
in the right-hand side of (
3.4
).

Before we proceed to the proof of
Theorem

3.4
, we need to prepare ourselves with the following simple technical lemma.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then

|f(x_{1})-f(x_{2})-f(x_{3})+f(x_{4})|\leq\|\nabla f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}|x_{2}-x_{1}||x_{3}-x_{1}|+\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}|x_{1}-x_{2}-x_{3}-x_{4}|.

Proof.

We have for
x,y,z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle|f(x)-f(x+z)-f(y)+f(y+z)|

\displaystyle=\Bigl|\int_{0}^{1}z(\nabla f(x+\theta z)-\nabla f(y+\theta z))\,d\theta\Bigr|

\displaystyle\leq|z||x-y|\|\nabla f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}.

Therefore

\displaystyle|f(x_{1})-f(x_{2})-f(x_{3})+f(x_{4})|

\displaystyle\leq|f(x_{1})-f(x_{2})-f(x_{3})+f(x_{3}+x_{2}-x_{1})|

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq}+|f(x_{4})-f(x_{3}+x_{2}-x_{1})|

\displaystyle\leq\|\nabla f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}|x_{2}-x_{1}||x_{3}-x_{1}|+\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}|x_{1}-x_{2}-x_{3}-x_{4}|.\qed

Now we can prove
Theorem

3.4
.

Proof of
Theorem

3.4
.

Fix
m\geq 2
,
0\leq S\leq T
. Let us verify that all the conditions of the SSL are satisfied for the germ

A_{s,t}:=\int_{s}^{t}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}(b(W_{r}+\varphi_{s})-b(W_{r}+\psi_{s}))\,dr,\quad(s,t)\in\Delta_{[S,T]}

(3.13)

and the process

\mathcal{A}_{t}:=\int_{S}^{t}\bigl(b(W_{r}+\varphi_{r})-b(W_{r}+\psi_{r})\bigr)\,dr,\quad t\in[S,T].

Note that we used
W_{r}+\varphi_{s}
as the argument of
b
in the germ (
3.13
), rather than
W_{r}+\varphi_{r}
: this choice exactly matches our ideology. We do not know anything about the conditional law
\law(W_{r}+\varphi_{r}\mid\mathcal{F}_{s})
, whereas

\law(W_{r}+\varphi_{s}\mid\mathcal{F}_{s})
is Gaussian.

Another important detail of the proof is that we first verify all the conditions of the SSL for a smooth function
b
, and then use Fatou’s lemma to pass to an arbitrary
b
. This will significantly simplify the verification of condition (i) of the SSL and will also be useful for future purposes, when the drift
b
is assumed to be only bounded.

Step 1
. Assume additionally that
b\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
(but of course we would like to bound the germ only in terms of
\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}
). Arguing exactly as in the proof of
Theorem

3.2
, we get

\displaystyle|A_{s,t}|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{s}^{t}\bigl|P_{r-s}b(W_{s}+\varphi_{s})-P_{r-s}b(W_{s}+\psi_{s})\bigr|\,dr\leq C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}|\varphi_{s}-\psi_{s}|\int_{s}^{t}\|P_{r-s}b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}\,dr

\displaystyle\leq C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}|\varphi_{s}-\psi_{s}|(t-s)^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma}{2}},

for
C=C(\gamma,d)>0
,
where we consecutively used identity (
3.4
) and
Lemma

3.1
.
Recalling the definitions of norms in (
3.8
), we get

\|A_{s,t}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\|\varphi-\psi\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([S,T])}(t-s)^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}

(3.14)

for
C=C(\gamma,d)>0
,
and thus condition (
2.10
) holds.

Next, let us verify condition (
2.11
). This time it is a bit trickier, as
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\delta A_{s,u,t}
is not zero. We have for
(s,u,t)\in\Delta^{3}_{[S,T]}

\delta A_{s,u,t}=\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\int_{u}^{t}(b(W_{r}+\varphi_{s})-b(W_{r}+\psi_{s}))\,dr-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{u}\int_{u}^{t}(b(W_{r}+\varphi_{u})-b(W_{r}+\psi_{u}))\,dr.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\delta A_{s,u,t}

\displaystyle\,\,=\int_{u}^{t}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{u}(b(W_{r}+\varphi_{s})-b(W_{r}+\psi_{s})-b(W_{r}+\varphi_{u})+b(W_{r}+\psi_{u}))\,dr

\displaystyle\,\,=\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\!\!\int_{u}^{t}(P_{r-u}b(W_{u}+\varphi_{s})-P_{r-u}b(W_{u}+\psi_{s})-P_{r-u}b(W_{u}+\varphi_{u})+P_{r-u}b(W_{u}+\psi_{u}))\,dr.

(3.15)

Using
Lemma

3.5
, we get

\displaystyle|P_{r-u}b(W_{u}+\varphi_{s})-P_{r-u}b(W_{u}+\psi_{s})-P_{r-u}b(W_{u}+\varphi_{u})+P_{r-u}b(W_{u}+\psi_{u})|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\|\nabla P_{r-u}b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}|\varphi_{s}-\psi_{s}|\,|\varphi_{u}-\varphi_{s}|+\|P_{r-u}b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}|(\varphi_{u}-\psi_{u})-(\varphi_{s}-\psi_{s})|.

(3.16)

Combining this with (
3.2
) and recalling heat kernel bounds in
Lemma

3.1
, we get

\displaystyle\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\delta A_{s,u,t}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C\Gamma_{0}\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\int_{u}^{t}(r-u)^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}\|\varphi-\psi\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([S,T])}|u-s|\,dr

(3.17)

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq}+C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\int_{u}^{t}(r-u)^{\frac{\gamma}{2}-\frac{1}{2}}[\varphi-\psi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{m}([S,T])}|u-s|^{\tau}\,dr

\displaystyle\leq C\Gamma_{0}\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(t-s)^{\frac{\gamma}{2}+1}\|\varphi-\psi\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([S,T])}

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq}+C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(t-s)^{\frac{\gamma}{2}+\frac{1}{2}+\tau}[\varphi-\psi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{m}([S,T])}.

(3.18)

Since
\gamma>0
and
\frac{\gamma}{2}+\tau>\frac{1}{2}
, we see that condition (
2.11
) is satisfied.

Condition (ii) of
Theorem

2.3
is satisfied by the construction of the germ in (
3.13
). Finally, to verify condition (i) of the SSL, let
\Pi:=\{S=t_{0},t_{1},...,t_{k}=T\}
be an arbitrary partition of
[S,T]
.
Note that for any
i=0,\ldots,k-1

\displaystyle\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{t_{i}}(\mathcal{A}_{t_{i+1}}-\mathcal{A}_{t_{i}})-A_{t_{i},t_{i+1}}\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\|b(W_{r}+\varphi_{r})-b(W_{r}+\varphi_{t_{i}})\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}\,dr

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq}+\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\|b(W_{r}+\psi_{r})-b(W_{r}+\psi_{t_{i}})\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}\,dr

\displaystyle\leq\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}(t_{i+1}-t_{i})^{1+\tau}([\varphi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{2}([S,T])}+[\psi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{2}([S,T])}).

Therefore, using the BDG inequality, we get

\displaystyle\Bigl\|\mathcal{A}_{T}-\mathcal{A}_{S}-\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}A_{t_{i},t_{i+1}}\Bigr\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\Bigl\|\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}(\mathcal{A}_{t_{i+1}}-\mathcal{A}_{t_{i}}-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{t_{i}}(\mathcal{A}_{t_{i+1}}-\mathcal{A}_{t_{i}}))\Bigr\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}^{2}

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq}+C\Bigl\|\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}(\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{t_{i}}(\mathcal{A}_{t_{i+1}}-\mathcal{A}_{t_{i}})-A_{t_{i},t_{i+1}})\Bigr\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\|\mathcal{A}_{t_{i+1}}-\mathcal{A}_{t_{i}}-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{t_{i}}(\mathcal{A}_{t_{i+1}}-\mathcal{A}_{t_{i}})\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}^{2}

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq}+C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}^{2}([\varphi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{2}([S,T])}^{2}+[\psi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{2}([S,T])}^{2})(\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}(t_{i+1}-t_{i})^{1+\tau})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}^{2}|\Pi|^{2\tau\wedge 1}(1+[\varphi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{2}([S,T])}+[\psi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{2}([S,T])}^{2})\to 0.

for
C>0
as
|\Pi|\to 0
. Thus, condition (
2.9
) also holds. Therefore, all the conditions of SSL are satisfied and (
2.12
) together with (
3.14
) and (
3.18
) implies (
3.4
).

Step 2
. For a general
b\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}
we put
b_{n}:=P_{\frac{1}{n}}b
. Then obviously
b_{n}(x)\to b(x)
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\|b_{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\leq\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}
and
b_{n}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
. Thus, Fatou’s lemma and Step 1 of the proof yield

\displaystyle\Bigl\|\int_{S}^{T}(b(W_{r}+\varphi_{r})-b(W_{r}+\psi_{r}))\,dr\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\Bigl\|\int_{S}^{T}(b_{n}(W_{r}+\varphi_{r})-b_{n}(W_{r}+\psi_{r}))\,dr\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\quad\leq C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(t-s)^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}\bigl((1+\Gamma_{0})\|\varphi-\psi\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([S,T])}+[\varphi-\psi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{m}([S,T])}(t-s)^{\tau}\bigr),

which is (
3.4
).
∎

We see that, in general, we have not required much from the driving process
W
; in particular, no Itô’s formula was needed. However, let us point out some parts of the proof that are “fragile” and would need to be replaced in other settings.

Remark 3.6
.

Let us look again at the crucial inequality (
3.2
). When we substitute (
3.2
) into (
3.2
), we need to bound

\Bigl\|\,\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}|\varphi_{s}-\psi_{s}|\,|\varphi_{u}-\varphi_{s}|\,\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

in terms of
\|\varphi_{s}-\psi_{s}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}
. Note that Hölder’s inequality won’t help here, because the resulting bound would involve the
(m+\varepsilon)
-moment of
|\varphi_{s}-\psi_{s}|
, and we would not be able to close the buckling argument; see inequality (
3.19
) below. Therefore, we had to assume an a.s. bound on
|\varphi_{u}-\varphi_{s}|
; see (
3.11
). Of course, if
X=W+\varphi
is a solution to the SDE (
1.3
) with bounded drift, then this condition is automatically satisfied. However, for less regular drifts (not locally bounded, measures, or generalized functions), this becomes a problem. The same issue also appears for rough SDEs, see
[
FHL21
]
.

Remark 3.7
.

Another very dangerous inequality is (
3.17
). Here we were very lucky: we had to ensure that
(r-u)^{\frac{\gamma}{2}-1}|u-s|
is integrable in
r
, and it barely is, since
\gamma>0
. In other situations, however, this is not the case. Note that the factor
(u-s)
does not help to improve integrability. This problem was resolved in
[
Ger22
]
, where a different version of stochastic sewing was developed. We will look at this in more detail in
Section

5.6
, where we consider SDEs driven by
\alpha
-stable processes.

3.3
Proof of strong uniqueness

We are now ready to prove our first main result: strong uniqueness of solutions to the SDE (
1.3
) via the SSL, following the plan outlined in
Section

1.1
.

Let
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
,
(Y_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
be two solutions to SDE (
1.3
) adapted to the filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
and with the same initial condition
X_{0}=Y_{0}=x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Define for
t\in[0,1]

\varphi_{t}:=X_{t}-W_{t};\quad\psi_{t}:=Y_{t}-W_{t}.

The main idea is to bound
\|X-Y\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{2}([0,1])}=\|\varphi-\psi\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{2}([0,1])}
by half of the same quantity, which would immediately imply that
X_{t}=Y_{t}
a.s. for all
t\in[0,1]
, and hence strong uniqueness. However, doing this directly seems to be not possible. Indeed, when we bound
\|\varphi-\psi\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{2}([0,1])}
via
Theorem

3.4
, a higher norm, namely
[\varphi-\psi]_{\mathcal{C}^{1/2}L_{2}([0,1])}
, appears. Therefore, we modify our strategy and aim to show that
[\varphi-\psi]_{\mathcal{C}^{1/2}L_{2}([0,T])}
can be bounded by half of itself, at least for small enough
T>0
. Since
X_{0}=Y_{0}
, this still yields
X_{t}=Y_{t}
a.s. for all
t\in[0,T]
. Iterating the argument then gives strong uniqueness on the whole interval
[0,1]
.

Proof of
Theorem

1.1
: strong uniqueness.

To simplify the argument, we prove the strong uniqueness part of
Theorem

1.1
under slightly more restrictive assumptions: namely, we assume that the drift
b\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}
for some
\gamma>0
. For the stochastic sewing proof of
Theorem

1.1
in full generality, we refer to
[
LL21
]
.

Let us apply
Theorem

3.4
to the processes
\varphi
,
\psi
defined above with
\tau=\frac{1}{2}
,
m=2
. We note that
|\varphi_{t}-\varphi_{s}|\leq\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}|t-s|
for any
s,t\in[0,1]
and thus condition (
3.11
) is satisfied. Condition (
3.10
) also holds since
\tau=\frac{1}{2}
and
\gamma>0
. Then all conditions of
Theorem

3.4
are satisfied and it follows from (
3.4
) that there exists
C=C(\gamma,d)
such that for any
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}

\displaystyle\|X_{t}-X_{s}-(Y_{t}-Y_{s})\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=\Bigl\|\int_{s}^{t}\bigl(b(W_{r}+\varphi_{r})-b(W_{r}+\psi_{r})\bigr)\,dr\Bigr\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(1+\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}})(t-s)^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}\|\varphi-\psi\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{2}([s,t])}

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq}+C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(t-s)^{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}[\varphi-\psi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{2}([s,t])}.

Let
T\in(0,1)
. Dividing both sides of the above inequality by
(t-s)^{\frac{1}{2}}
and taking the supremum over
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,T]}
, we obtain (note that
X-Y=\varphi-\psi
)

[\varphi-\psi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{2}([0,T])}\leq C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(1+\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}})\|\varphi-\psi\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{2}([0,T])}+C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}T^{\frac{1}{2}}[\varphi-\psi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{2}([0,T])}.

(3.19)

Note that by definition

\|\varphi-\psi\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{2}([0,T])}\leq\|\varphi_{0}-\psi_{0}\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}+T^{\frac{1}{2}}[\varphi-\psi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{2}([0,T])}=T^{\frac{1}{2}}[\varphi-\psi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{2}([0,T])}.

(3.20)

Hence we get from (
3.19
)

[\varphi-\psi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{2}([0,T])}\leq C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(1+\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}})T^{\frac{1}{2}}[\varphi-\psi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{2}([0,T])},

for
C=C(\gamma,d)
.
Pick now
T_{0}
small enough so that

C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(1+\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}})T_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\frac{1}{2}.

Then we deduce from (
3.19
)

[\varphi-\psi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{m}([0,T_{0}])}=0

and thus by (
3.20
),
X_{t}=Y_{t}
a.s. for
t\in[0,T_{0}]
. Repeating this argument
\lceil 1/T_{0}\rceil
times we get that
X_{t}=Y_{t}
a.s. for any
t\in[0,1]
and thus strong uniqueness holds.
∎

Strong existence of solutions to (
1.3
) now follows easily. Indeed, weak existence is immediate by the Girsanov theorem. By the Yamada–Watanabe theorem (see, e.g.,
[
KS91
, Corollary 5.3.23]
), weak existence and strong uniqueness for (
1.3
) imply strong existence.

Remark 3.8
.

Note that the proof given above is quite robust. The only properties of
W
we used are the Gaussian bounds (
3.2
) and the identity (
3.4
). Therefore, the same general idea can be applied in many other settings. In particular,
3.15
extends the uniqueness result to SDEs driven by fractional Brownian motion.

3.4
Exercises

Let us extend
Theorem

1.1
to SDEs driven by a fractional Brownian motion (fBM). First, let us recall its definition and basic properties.

Definition 3.9
.

A process
X\colon\Omega\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
is called
Gaussian
if for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and any

t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{n}\in[0,1]^{n}
, the vector
(X_{t_{1}},X_{t_{2}},\dots,X_{t_{n}})
has a Gaussian distribution.

Definition 3.10
.

Let
H\in(0,1)
. A Gaussian process
B^{H}\colon\Omega\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
is called a
fractional Brownian motion with Hurst parameter
H
if it is continuous and for any
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
one has
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057ptB^{H}_{t}=0
,
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057ptB^{H}_{s}B^{H}_{t}=\frac{1}{2}(t^{2H}+s^{2H}-(t-s)^{2H})
.

If
H=\frac{1}{2}
, then an fBM is a Brownian motion. For
H\neq\frac{1}{2}
, an fBM is not a Markov process and not a martingale. For any
\varepsilon>0
, almost all trajectories of fBM
B^{H}
are Hölder continuous functions with exponent
H-\varepsilon
, that is
\mathsf{P}(B^{H}\in\mathcal{C}^{H-\varepsilon}([0,1];\mathbb{R}))=1
.

A
d
-dimensional fBM with Hurst parameter
H\in(0,1)
(
d\in\mathbb{N}
) is simply a vector consisting of
d
independent fBMs with Hurst parameter
H
. We recall from
[
Nua06
, Section 5.1.3, formula (5.8) and Proposition 5.1.3]
that given a
d
-dimensional fBM
B^{H}
one can construct on the same probability space a standard
d
-dimensional BM
W
such that

B^{H}_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}K_{H}(t,s)\,dW_{s},

(3.21)

for a certain function
K_{H}\colon[0,1]^{2}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
satisfying for any
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
(see, e.g.,
[
BLM25
, Proposition B.2(ii)]
)

K_{H}(t,s)\geq C(t-s)^{H-\frac{1}{2}},

(3.22)

for
C=C(H)
.

We fix now
H\in(0,1)
, a
d
-dimensional fBM
B^{H}
, and let
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
be the filtration of the underlying BM
W
from representation (
3.21
).

Exercise 3.11
.

1.

Generalize formula (
3.4
) and show that for any bounded measurable
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,
0\leq s<t
we have

\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}f(B^{H}_{t})=P_{\sigma^{2}(s,t)}f(\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}B^{H}_{t}),

(3.23)

for
\sigma^{2}(s,t):=\int_{s}^{t}K_{H}(t,r)^{2}\,dr
.

2.

Adapt the proof of
Theorem

3.2
to the fractional Brownian case. Namely, given
m\geq 2
,
\gamma\in(0,1]
,
b\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
,
\rho\in[0,1]
,
\rho<\gamma+\frac{1}{2H}
, show that
there exists a constant
C=C(\gamma,\rho,d,H,m)>0
such that for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
(S,T)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
one has

\Bigl\|\int_{S}^{T}(b(B^{H}_{r}+x)-b(B^{H}_{r}+y))\,dr\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}|x-y|^{\rho}|T-S|^{1+(\gamma-\rho)H}.

Hint
: Use identity (
3.23
) and inequality (
3.22
).

To solve the next exercise, we will need Kolmogorov’s continuity theorem. It allows us to exchange the order of taking the supremum and the expectation in norms of the form (
3.8
). That is,
it gives a bound on the moments of the Hölder norm of a stochastic process if the moments of its increments are Hölder-bounded.

Theorem 3.12

(Kolmogorov’s continuity theorem, see, e.g.,
[
FV10
, Theorem A.11]
,

[
Kun90
, Theorem 1.4.1]
)
.

Let
(E,d)
be a Polish space. Let
\gamma\in(0,1]
,
\Gamma\geq 0
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
m>\frac{k}{\gamma}
,
T>0
. Let
X\colon\Omega\times[0,T]^{k}\to E
be a continuous process such that for any
s,t\in[0,T]^{k}

\|d(X_{s},X_{t})\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq\Gamma|t-s|^{\gamma}.

Then for any
\beta\in(0,\gamma-\frac{k}{m})
there exists a constant
C=C(\beta,\gamma,k,m,T)>0

such that

\|[X]_{\mathcal{C}^{\beta}([0,1]^{k};E)}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq C\Gamma.

Exercise 3.13
.

Let
\gamma\in(0,1]
,
b\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
,
H\in(0,1)
.
Use Kolmogorov’s continuity theorem and
3.11
to deduce that the process

x\mapsto\int_{0}^{1}b(B_{r}^{H}+x)\,dr,\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

is a.s. in
\mathcal{C}^{(\gamma+\frac{1}{2H}-\varepsilon)\wedge(1-\varepsilon)}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
for any
\varepsilon>0
.

Next, we extend
Theorem

3.4
to the fractional Brownian case. We consider only the setting where no extensions of stochastic sewing are needed and therefore restrict ourselves to the case
H<\frac{1}{2}
.

Exercise 3.14
.

Let
H\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
,
m\geq 2
,
\tau\in(H,1]
. Let
b
be a bounded measurable function
\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
. Let
\psi,\varphi\colon\Omega\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
be continuous measurable functions adapted to
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
. Suppose that
there exists a constant
\Gamma_{0}\geq 0
such that a.s. for all
s,t\in\Delta_{[0,1]}

|\varphi_{t}-\varphi_{s}|\leq\Gamma_{0}|t-s|.

Show that there exists a constant
C=C(\tau,d,m)>0
such that
for any
(S,T)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
one has

\displaystyle\Bigl\|\int_{S}^{T}\bigl(b(B^{H}_{r}+\varphi_{r})-b(B^{H}_{r}+\psi_{r})\bigr)\,dr\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\quad\leq C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}(t-s)^{1-H}\bigl((1+\Gamma_{0})\|\varphi-\psi\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([S,T])}+[\varphi-\psi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{m}([S,T])}(t-s)^{\tau}\bigr).

Exercise 3.15

(Catellier-Gubinelli theorem)
.

Let
H\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
, let
b
be a bounded measurable function
\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Use
3.14
to show that the solution to SDE

dX_{t}=b(X_{t})dt+dB_{t}^{H}

is unique.

Note that the statement of
3.15
actually holds in much greater generality, namely for
b\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}
,
\gamma>1-\frac{1}{2H}
and
H\in(0,\infty)\setminus\mathbb{N}
; see
[
CG16
,
Ger22
]
(there is a way to define an fBM for
H>1
). The proof for the case
H<\frac{1}{2}
,
\gamma<0
can be carried out using the same stochastic sewing technique, but it requires familiarity with the basic theory of Besov spaces. We will return to this later in
Section

4
.
In contrast, if
H>\frac{1}{2}
, the stochastic sewing lemma in the form of
Theorem

2.3
leads to a suboptimal result (recall
Remark

3.7
). To obtain the optimal condition,
[
Ger22
]
introduced a shifted stochastic sewing lemma. We will look at this extension in
Section

5.5
.

4
Weak well-posedness of SDEs driven by Brownian motion

The main goal of this section is to show how stochastic sewing techniques can be combined with ideas from ergodic theory to obtain
weak
uniqueness (uniqueness in law). In this section we follow
[
BM24
]
. As in the previous section, to ease the understanding of the main ideas, we deliberately concentrate on the simplest model: SDEs driven by a Brownian motion. However, the same strategy can be successfully applied to SDEs driven by a fractional Brownian motion or to SPDEs; see
[
BM24
]
.
Thus, in this section we prove
Theorem

1.2
without relying on the Zvonkin transformation argument.

4.1
SDEs with distributional drifts

First of all, let us recall that the Schwartz space
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the space of all functions
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{C})
such that
such that
f
and all its derivatives are rapidly decreasing at infinity. That is, for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
we have

\|\varphi\|_{\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),k}:=\sup_{\mu\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{+},|\mu|\leq k}\|(1+|\cdot|)^{k}\partial^{\mu}f\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\infty,

where for
\mu=(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{d})
we used the usual convention:
|\mu|:=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mu_{i}
;
\partial^{\mu}:=\partial^{\mu_{1}}_{x_{1}}\ldots\partial^{\mu_{d}}_{x_{d}}
.

The Schwartz space of distributions
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a space of all linear maps
u\colon\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{\mathbb{C}}
such that for some
C>0
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
one has

|\langle u,\varphi\rangle|\leq C\|\varphi\|_{\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),k},\quad\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Now we are ready to define the notion of regularity of a Schwartz distribution.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\alpha<0
. We say that a Schwartz distribution
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
belongs to the Besov space
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
if

\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}:=\sup_{t\in(0,1]}t^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\|P_{t}f\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\infty.

(4.1)

Here
P_{t}f
denotes the convolution of the distribution
f
with the heat kernel
P_{t}
defined in (
3.1
), that is

P_{t}f(x):=\langle f,p_{t}(x-\cdot)\rangle,\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

One can show that
Definition

4.1
is equivalent to the standard definition of the Besov space via Paley–Littlewood blocks (see, e.g.,
[
BCD11
, Theorem 2.34]
), but is more convenient for our analysis. The function
p_{t}
can in fact be replaced by
h_{t}(x):=t^{-\frac{d}{2}}h(x/t)
, where
h\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}

is any smooth integrable function.

It is easy to see that this definition keeps the following natural property: if
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for
\alpha\in(0,1)
, then
\nabla f
belongs to the Besov space of negative regularity
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha-1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Furthermore, it follows from the Besov embedding theorem that integrable functions belongs to a Besov space of certain negative regularity, that is,
L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset\mathcal{C}^{-\frac{d}{p}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
We also note that this definition matches
Lemma

3.1
in the following sense.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
,
\gamma<0
,
\rho\in(0,1]
. Then there exists
C=C(\gamma,\rho,d)>0
such that for any
t>0
we have

\|P_{t}f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\rho}}\leq Ct^{\frac{\gamma-\rho}{2}}\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}.

Proof.

We write
P_{t}f=P_{\frac{t}{2}}P_{\frac{t}{2}}f=:P_{\frac{t}{2}}g
, where we denoted
g:=P_{t/2}f
. It follows from
Definition

4.1
that

\|g\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq Ct^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}.

(4.2)

for
C>0
. Next, using
Lemma

3.1
we deduce for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle|P_{\frac{t}{2}}g(x)-P_{\frac{t}{2}}g(y)|

\displaystyle\leq 2\|g\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{1-\rho}|P_{\frac{t}{2}}g(x)-P_{\frac{t}{2}}g(y)|^{\rho}\leq 2\|g\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{1-\rho}|x-y|^{\rho}\|P_{\frac{t}{2}}g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}^{\rho}

\displaystyle\leq C\|g\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}|x-y|^{\rho}t^{-\frac{\rho}{2}},

for
C=C(d)
. Thus, recalling also (
4.2
), we finally get

\|P_{t}f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\rho}}=\|P_{\frac{t}{2}}g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\rho}}\leq C\|g\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}t^{-\frac{\rho}{2}}\leq Ct^{-\frac{\rho}{2}}t^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}.\qed

Next, let us explain how we can define the notion of a solution to SDE

X_{t}=x+\int_{0}^{t}b(X_{r})\,dr+W_{t},

(4.3)

where the drift
b
is not a function but a distribution from the Besov space
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, with
{\alpha<0}
. Note that in this case the term
b(X_{r})
is not defined: indeed, one cannot evaluate Schwartz distributions at fixed points. Recall, however, that in
Section

3
we observed that for any bounded measurable function
{f\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}}
, the function
\widetilde{f}(x):=\int_{0}^{1}f(W_{r}+x)\,dr
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, is a.s. much smoother than
f
; namely,
\widetilde{f}
is Lipschitz. Therefore, instead of defining
b(X_{r})
directly, we attempt to make sense of the whole integral
\int b(X_{r})\,dr
, hoping that it will be well defined thanks to the averaging effect of
X
. This motivates the following definition.

Definition 4.3
.

We say that a sequence of functions
f^{n}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, converges to a function
f
in
\mathcal{C}^{\beta-}
,
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, if
\sup_{n}\|f_{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\beta}}<\infty
and for any
\beta^{\prime}<\beta
we have
\|f_{n}-f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\beta^{\prime}}}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.

Definition 4.4

(
[
BC01
, Definition 2.1]
)
.

Let
b\in\mathcal{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
. We say that a continuous process
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
taking values in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a solution to (
4.3
) with the initial condition
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, if there exists a continuous process
(\psi_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
taking values in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that:

1.

X_{t}=x+\psi_{t}+W_{t}
,
t\in[0,1]
a.s.;

2.

for
any
sequence
(b^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
functions converging to
b
in
\mathcal{C}^{\beta-}
we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\Big|\int_{0}^{t}b^{n}(X_{r})\,dr-\psi_{t}\Big|=0\,\,\text{in probability}.

Note that in
Definition

4.3
we did not require that
f_{n}
converge to
f
in
\mathcal{C}^{\beta}
, but imposed a slightly weaker condition. This is due to the fact that given
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\beta}
,
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, one can always construct a sequence
(f^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}+}
of smooth functions converging to
f
in
\mathcal{C}^{\beta-}
; for example, one can take
f_{n}:=P_{1/n}f
, see
4.18
. On the other hand, for a general
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\beta}
a sequence of smooth functions
(f^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}+}
converging to
f
in
\mathcal{C}^{\beta}
might not exist.

We also note that if
b\in\mathcal{C}^{\beta}
with
\beta>0
, then
b(X_{t})
is of course well defined, and it is immediate that this notion of a solution coincides with the standard notion of a solution.

As in the classical case, we define a
weak solution
to (
4.3
) to be a couple
(X,W)
on a complete filtered probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]},\mathsf{P})
such that
W
is an
(\mathcal{F}_{t})
-Brownian motion,
X
is adapted to
(\mathcal{F}_{t})
and solves equation (
4.3
) in the sense of
Definition

4.4
. We say that
weak uniqueness
holds for (
4.3
) if whenever
(X,W)
and
(Y,\overline{W})
are two weak solutions of this equation (not necessarily defined on the same probability space), then
\law(X)=\law(Y)
on the path space
\mathcal{C}([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Finally, we introduce some notation related to distances between probability measures and recall some fundamentals. Let
(E,\rho)
be a metric space. The space of all probability measures on
E
equipped with the Borel
\sigma
-algebra
\mathscr{B}(E)
is denoted by
\mathcal{P}(E)
. For two probability measures
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(E)
we define the Wasserstein (Kantorovich-Rubinstein) distance between them as

\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mu,\nu):=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\law(X)=\mu\\
\law(Y)=\nu\end{subarray}}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt\rho(X,Y),

(4.4)

where the infimum is taken over all random variables
X,Y
with
\law(X)=\mu
,
\law(Y)=\nu
. The choice
\rho(x,y)=\mathbbm{1}(x\neq y)
,
x,y\in E
, leads to the total variation distance
d_{TV}
, which is given by

d_{TV}(\mu,\nu):=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\law(X)=\mu\\
\law(Y)=\nu\end{subarray}}\mathsf{P}(X\neq Y)=\sup_{A\in\mathscr{B}(E)}|\mu(A)-\nu(A)|.

(4.5)

It is well known that if
(E,\rho)
is Polish and
\rho
is bounded, then weak convergence of measures is equivalent to convergence in
W_{\rho}
, see, e.g.,
[
Vil09
, Corollary 6.13]
.

A simple yet powerful tool in bounding total variation distance between probability measures is Pinsker’s inequality.

Proposition 4.5

(Pinsker’s inequality)
.

Let
\mathsf{P},\mathsf{Q}
be two probability measures on a measurable space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F})
. Then

d_{TV}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{Q})\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\mathsf{P}}\log\frac{d\mathsf{P}}{d\mathsf{Q}}}.

4.2
Challenges and proof strategy

Before going into the details,let us explain what the challenges are in proving weak uniqueness of SDEs/SPDEs in general and why stochastic sewing alone is not efficient. Then we present a sketch of the proof strategy, skipping over some technicalities which will be treated later. In particular, we omit the arbitrarily small exponents.

Thus, let
(X,W)
and
(Y,\overline{W})
be two solutions to SDE (
4.3
). Our goal is to show that

\law(X)=\law(Y)
.

For the case of SDEs driven by a Brownian motion, a possible proof strategy is to use the Zvonkin transformation method. The idea is to consider an SDE for
\Psi(X)
, where
\Psi\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a certain “nice” function. Using Ito’s formula, one can show that for a carefully chosen
\Psi
, the new equation has a less singular drift and therefore it has a unique weak solution. Then, since
\Psi
is nice, one deduces weak uniqueness for the original SDE, see
[
ZZ17
,
FIR17
]
for more details.

However, this approach cannot be extended to SDEs driven by a more general process, such as fractional Brownian motion (in which case Ito’s formula is not available) or to stochastic PDEs (in this case only a rather restricted Ito-type formula is available which does not allow to implement the above strategy).

Alternatively, weak uniqueness can also be established using Girsanov’s theorem if one can show
that the process
W+\int_{0}^{\cdot}b(X_{r})\,dr
has the law of a Brownian motion under some probability measure
\widetilde{\mathsf{P}}
. However, we are in the range of regularity of the drifts
b
, where this is not the case. Indeed, we have to require that at least
\int_{0}^{1}|b|^{2}(X_{r})\,dr<\infty
, which does not hold if
b
is a true distribution (one cannot square the distributions).

Thus, we aim to develop a general strategy which can show directly that
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt\Phi(X)=\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt\Phi(Y)
for a large class of test functions
\Phi\colon\mathcal{C}([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
without relying on PDE methods or Markov property of the noise.
Assume for a moment that
X
and
Y
are defined on the same probability space and that
W=\overline{W}
. Then a naive direct approach, which fails here, would be simply to write

|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt\Phi(X_{t})-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt\Phi(Y_{t})|\leq\|\Phi\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt|X_{t}-Y_{t}|=\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt\Bigl|\int_{0}^{t}(b(X_{r})-b(Y_{r}))\,dr\Bigr|,

(4.6)

and then use stochastic sewing, arguing as in
Section

3
. Similarly to
3.13
applied with
H=\frac{1}{2}
, one would get for
T\in[0,1]

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|X_{t}-Y_{t}\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}\leq CT^{\frac{1}{2}}\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|X_{t}-Y_{t}\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}^{(\gamma+1)\wedge 1}+\text{good small terms}.

(4.7)

While this bound is definitely better than a direct bound (
1.4
), it is still not sufficient for our purposes. Indeed, if
\gamma>0
, one can remove the bad term
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|X_{t}-Y_{t}\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}
on the right-hand side for small
T>0
, and even get strong uniqueness of solutions to SDE (
4.3
), as shown in
Section

3
. However, we are interested in distributional drifts, so
\gamma
can be negative as well. In this regime, the exponent
\gamma+1
is less than
1
and (
4.7
) does not imply that
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|X_{t}-Y_{t}\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}
is small. Therefore this direct approach does not work.

We also see that already the first inequality in (
4.6
) is too harsh, as we do not expect
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt|X_{t}-Y_{t}|
to be
0
(this would imply strong uniqueness and we are interested in the regime where weak uniqueness holds and strong may fail). On the other hand, it is not clear how to bound
|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt\Phi(X_{t})-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt\Phi(Y_{t})|
with stochastic sewing.

Therefore, we avoid comparing the solutions
X
and
Y
directly. Instead, we combine stochastic sewing with some ideas from ergodic theory: the generalized coupling method of
[
Hai02
,
Mat02
,
KS20
]
. Fix a sequence
(b^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
of smooth functions converging to
b
in
\mathcal{C}^{\gamma-}
. Let
X^{n}
be the strong solution to SDE

X^{n}_{t}=x+\int_{0}^{t}b^{n}(X^{n}_{r})\,dr+W_{t},\quad t\in[0,1]

(4.8)

We will prove that
X^{n}
converges weakly to
X
and similarly that
X^{n}
converges weakly to
Y
. By uniqueness of the limit, this would imply
\law(X)=\law(Y)
.

To do this we consider a bounded separable metric space
\mathcal{C}([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{d})
equipped with the distance

d(x,y):=\|x-y\|_{\mathcal{C}([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{d})}\wedge 1,

and the corresponding Wasserstein distance
\mathbb{W}_{\|\cdot\|\wedge 1}
, recall (
4.4
). As explained in
Section

4.1
, to show that the sequence
(X^{n})
converges weakly to
X
it is sufficient to show that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{W}_{\|\cdot\|\wedge 1}(\law(X^{n}),\law(X))=0.

The main idea of the generalized coupling method is to introduce an auxiliary process
\widetilde{X}^{n}
, which is defined on the same space as
X
and solves SDE

d\widetilde{X}^{n}_{t}=b^{n}(\widetilde{X}^{n}_{t})dt+\lambda(X_{t}-\widetilde{X}^{n}_{t})dt+dW_{t},

(4.9)

where the parameter
\lambda>1
will be fixed later. We see that the additional term
{\lambda(X_{t}-\widetilde{X}^{n}_{t})dt}
pushes
\widetilde{X}^{n}
towards
X
.
The goal now is to show that the process
\widetilde{X}^{n}
is close to
X^{n}
in law for
\lambda
not too large, and close to
X
in distance for
\lambda
not too small. Then by picking the best
\lambda
from these two opposite requirements, we derive that
X^{n}
and
X
are close to each other in law. More precisely, by the triangle inequality and the definition of the distances
\mathbb{W}_{\|\cdot\|\wedge 1}
and
d_{TV}
in (
4.4
), (
4.5
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}_{\|\cdot\|\wedge 1}(\law(X^{n}),\law(X))

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{W}_{\|\cdot\|\wedge 1}(\law(X^{n}),\law(\widetilde{X}^{n}))+\mathbb{W}_{\|\cdot\|\wedge 1}(\law(\widetilde{X}^{n}),\law(X))
