\displaystyle\leq d_{TV}(\law(X^{n}),\law(\widetilde{X}^{n}))+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt\|\widetilde{X}^{n}-X\|_{\mathcal{C}([0,1])}.

(4.10)

To bound the first term in the right-hand side of (
4.2
), we note that SDE (
4.9
) can be rewritten as

d\widetilde{X}^{n}_{t}=b^{n}(\widetilde{X}^{n}_{t})dt+d\widetilde{W}_{t},

where we put
\widetilde{W}_{t}:=\int_{0}^{t}\lambda(X_{r}-\widetilde{X}^{n}_{r})dr+W_{t}
,
t\in[0,1]
. Girsanov theorem implies that
\widetilde{W}
is a Brownian motion under a new probability measure
\widetilde{\mathsf{P}}
(see
Lemma

4.14
for details) and therefore the pair
(\widetilde{X}^{n},\widetilde{W})
is a solution to (
4.3
) on
(\Omega,\widetilde{\mathsf{P}})
. Therefore, by Girsanov’s theorem and Pinsker’s inequality (
Proposition

4.5
) we get (see again
Lemma

4.14
for more details)

d_{TV}(\law(X^{n}),\law(\widetilde{X}^{n}))\leq d_{TV}(\mathsf{P},\widetilde{\mathsf{P}})\leq C\lambda\bigl\|\|X-\widetilde{X}^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}([0,1])}\bigr\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}.

(4.11)

Next, to compare
\widetilde{X}^{n}
and
X
, we benefit from the control term
\lambda(X-\widetilde{X}^{n})
which pushes
\widetilde{X}^{n}
towards
X
. After a short calculation, we deduce for
t\in[0,1]

|X_{t}-\widetilde{X}^{n}_{t}|\leq\Bigl|\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\lambda(t-r)}(b(X_{r})-b_{n}(\widetilde{X}_{r}^{n}))\,dr\Bigr|.

(4.12)

However, by this time the reader will probably already have become a master of stochastic sewing and will immediately recognize that this bound is of the type (
1.1
) and is very close to the bound (
3.4
). Therefore, modifying a bit the argument of
Theorem

3.4
, we obtain

\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\|X_{t}-\widetilde{X}_{t}^{n}\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}\leq C\lambda^{-\frac{3}{4}-\frac{\gamma}{2}}\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\|X_{t}-\widetilde{X}^{n}_{t}\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}^{\frac{1}{2}}+\lambda\|b-b^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma-\varepsilon}}.

Of course, similar to (
4.7
), the term
\|X_{t}-\widetilde{X}^{n}_{t}\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}
appears with an exponent less than
1
, and we cannot get rid of it directly. However, this is not a problem anymore due to the presence of the additional factor
\lambda^{-\frac{3}{4}-\frac{\gamma}{2}}
, which we can make very small. By Young’s inequality

\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\|X_{t}-\widetilde{X}_{t}^{n}\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}\leq C\lambda^{-\frac{3}{2}-\gamma}+C\lambda^{-\varepsilon}\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\|X_{t}-\widetilde{X}^{n}_{t}\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}+\lambda\|b-b^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma-\varepsilon}},

which implies

\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\|X_{t}-\widetilde{X}_{t}^{n}\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}\leq C\lambda^{-\frac{3}{2}-\gamma}+\lambda\|b-b^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma-\varepsilon}}

Now we can combine this together with (
4.11
) and substitute into (
4.2
). We get (up to the transposition of
\mathcal{C}([0,1])
and
L_{2}(\Omega)
norms which should be justified separately with Kolmogorov continuity theorem-type arguments)

\mathbb{W}_{\|\cdot\|\wedge 1}(\law(X^{n}),\law(X))\leq C\lambda^{-\frac{1}{2}-\gamma}+\lambda^{2}\|b-b^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma-\varepsilon}}.

If the power of
\lambda
is negative, that is

-\frac{1}{2}-\gamma<0,

(4.13)

then we can pick
\lambda:=\|b-b^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma-\varepsilon}}^{-1/3}
to get

\mathbb{W}_{\|\cdot\|\wedge 1}(\law(X^{n}),\law(X))\leq C\|b-b^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma-\varepsilon}}^{\rho}

for some
\rho>0
. This yields the desired weak convergence of the fixed sequence
(X^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
to any weak solution
X
, establishing weak uniqueness. The condition
(
4.13
) is exactly the condition
\gamma>-\frac{1}{2}
of
Theorem

1.2
.

Now it remains to turn this informal description of our proof strategy into a rigorous argument. In
Section

4.3
we bound the moments of the right-hand side of (
4.12
). In
Section

4.4
we establish weak existence of solutions, and in
Section

4.5
we conclude.

4.3
Key integral bounds

Let us recall that we need to bound the right-hand side of (
4.12
) and show that it is small for large
\lambda
. We split the integral into two parts:

\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\lambda(t-r)}(b(X_{r})-b_{n}(\widetilde{X}_{r}^{n}))\,dr=\Bigl(\int_{0}^{t-\lambda^{-1-\varepsilon}}+\int_{t-\lambda^{-1-\varepsilon}}^{t}\Bigr)e^{-\lambda(t-r)}(b(X_{r})-b_{n}(\widetilde{X}_{r}^{n}))\,dr.

(4.14)

Now, if
r
is far from
t
(the first integral), then the weight
e^{-\lambda(t-r)}
is tiny; it is smaller than
\exp(-\lambda^{-\varepsilon})
. On the other hand, if
r
is close to
t
(the second integral), the weight is of order
1
, but the time interval
[t-\lambda^{-1-\varepsilon},t]
is very small. Thus, in both cases, each part of the integral is small. To make this heuristics precise, we need to generalize
Theorem

3.4
in two directions: first, by allowing a deterministic time-dependent weight in front of
b
; and second, by extending the range of
\gamma
to include negative values.

We first prove a generic statement and then, as a corollary, bound (
4.14
). Recall that we measure the regularity of the perturbation in the norms
[\cdot]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{m}([S,T])}
introduced in (
3.8
).

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
m\geq 2
,
\gamma\in(-1,0)
,
\tau\in(0,1]
. Let
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,
w\colon[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
be bounded continuous functions.
Let
\varphi,\psi\colon[0,T]\times D\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be continuous measurable processes adapted to the filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
.
Assume that

\frac{\gamma}{2}+\tau>\frac{1}{2}.

(4.15)

Then there exists a constant
C=C(\gamma,\tau,d,m)
such that for any
(S,T)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
, we have

\Bigl\|\int_{S}^{T}w_{r}f(W_{r}+\varphi_{r})\,dr\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\|w\|_{L_{\infty}([S,T])}(T-S)^{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}\bigl(1+[\varphi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{m}([S,T])}(T-S)^{\tau-\frac{1}{2}}\bigr).

(4.16)

If, additionally, for some
\mu\in(0,1]
we have

\gamma>\mu-1,

(4.17)

then there exists a constant
C=C(\gamma,\mu,\tau,d,m)
such that for any
(S,T)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
we have

\displaystyle\Bigl\|\int_{S}^{T}w_{r}(f(W_{r}+\varphi_{r})-f(W_{r}+\psi_{r}))\,dr\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\quad\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\|w\|_{L_{\infty}([S,T])}(T-S)^{1+\frac{\gamma-\mu}{2}}\|\varphi-\psi\|^{\mu}_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([S,T])}

\displaystyle\qquad+C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\|w\|_{L_{\infty}([S,T])}(T-S)^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma}{2}+\tau}([\varphi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{m}([S,T])}+[\psi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{m}([S,T])}).

(4.18)

Let us note that even though we have assumed
f
to be a bounded continuous function, the right-hand sides of (
4.16
) and (
4.18
) depends only on a much weaker norm of
f
, namely
\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}
.

Proof.

First, we suppose additionally that
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
and then as in the Step 2 of the proof of
Theorem

3.4
, we remove this assumption by Fatou’s lemma. Fix
(S,T)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
.

As in the proof of
Theorem

3.4
, we rely on the stochastic sewing lemma. As before it is crucial to choose the correct germ. Inspired by our previous choice (
3.13
), we put

A^{\varphi}_{s,t}:=\int_{s}^{t}w_{r}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}f(W_{r}+\varphi_{s})\,dr;\qquad\mathcal{A}^{\varphi}_{t}:=\int_{s}^{t}w_{r}f(W_{r}+\varphi_{r})\,dr,\quad(s,t)\in\Delta_{[S,T]}.

(4.19)

We begin with the proof of (
4.16
). Verification of condition (i) is done in the same way as at the end of Step 1 in the proof of
Theorem

3.4
and is left as a technical exercise to the reader. We see that
A^{\varphi}_{s,t}
is
\mathcal{F}_{s}
-measurable and therefore condition (ii) of
Theorem

2.3
(SSL) holds. Next, let us verify condition (iii) of the SSL. Using the definition of the Besov space (
4.1
), we derive for
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[S,T]}

\displaystyle|A^{\varphi}_{s,t}|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{s}^{t}w_{r}|P_{r-s}f(W_{s}+\varphi_{s})|\,dr\leq\int_{s}^{t}w_{r}\|P_{r-s}f\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,dr

\displaystyle\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\|w\|_{L_{\infty}([S,T])}\int_{s}^{t}(r-s)^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\,dr\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\|w\|_{L_{\infty}([S,T])}(t-s)^{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}},

where
C=C(\gamma,d)
. This implies for any
m\geq 2

\|A^{\varphi}_{s,t}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\|w\|_{L_{\infty}([S,T])}(t-s)^{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}},\quad(s,t)\in\Delta_{[S,T]}.

(4.20)

Since
\gamma>-1
, condition (
2.10
) of the SSL holds.

Next, we pass to the verification of condition (
2.11
). We have for
(s,u,t)\in\Delta^{3}_{[S,T]}

\delta A^{\varphi}_{s,u,t}=\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\int_{u}^{t}w_{r}f(W_{r}+\varphi_{s})\,dr-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{u}\int_{u}^{t}w_{r}f(W_{r}+\varphi_{u})\,dr.

Therefore,

\displaystyle|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\delta A^{\varphi}_{s,u,t}|

\displaystyle=\Bigl|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\int_{u}^{t}w_{r}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{u}[f(W_{r}+\varphi_{s})-f(W_{r}+\varphi_{u})]\,dr\Bigr|

\displaystyle=\Bigl|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\int_{u}^{t}w_{r}(P_{r-u}f(W_{s}+\varphi_{s})-P_{r-u}f(W_{s}+\varphi_{u}))\,dr\Bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\int_{u}^{t}w_{r}\|P_{r-u}f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}|\varphi_{s}-\varphi_{u}|\,dr.

Using again the definition of a negative Besov space and the conditional Jensen’s nequality in the form (
2.8
) with
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{F}_{s}
, we get for any
m\geq 2

\displaystyle\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\delta A^{\varphi}_{s,u,t}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{u}^{t}w_{r}\|P_{r-u}f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|\varphi_{s}-\varphi_{u}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\,dr

\displaystyle\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\|w\|_{L_{\infty}([S,T])}[\varphi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{m}([S,T])}(t-s)^{\tau}\int_{u}^{t}(r-u)^{\frac{\gamma}{2}-\frac{1}{2}}\,dr

\displaystyle\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\|w\|_{L_{\infty}([S,T])}[\varphi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{m}([S,T])}(t-s)^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma}{2}+\tau}

(4.21)

for
C=C(\gamma,d)
. Here we used that the singularity
(r-u)^{\frac{\gamma}{2}-\frac{1}{2}}
is integrable because
\gamma>-1
. By (
4.15
) we have
\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma}{2}+\tau>1
and therefore condition (
2.11
) is satisfied.

Thus, all the conditions of
Theorem

2.3
are satisfied and (
4.16
) follows from (
2.12
) and (
4.20
), (
4.3
).

Now we move on to the proof of (
4.18
). We apply now the SSL to the processes

A_{s,t}:=A^{\varphi}_{s,t}-A^{\psi}_{s,t};\quad\mathcal{A}_{t}:=\mathcal{A}^{\varphi}_{t}-\mathcal{A}^{\psi}_{t},\qquad(s,t)\in\Delta_{[S,T]},

where the processes
A^{\varphi}
,
\mathcal{A}^{\varphi}
were introduced in (
4.19
) and
A^{\psi}
,
\mathcal{A}^{\psi}
are defined in exactly the same way with
\varphi
replaced by
\psi
.

We again leave verification of condition (i) of the SSL to the reader and note that condition (ii) obviously holds. Next, using
Lemma

4.2
, we write for any
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[S,T]}

\displaystyle|A_{s,t}|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{s}^{t}w_{r}|P_{r-s}f(W_{s}+\varphi_{s})-P_{r-s}f(W_{s}+\psi_{s})|\,dr\leq|\psi_{s}-\psi_{s}|^{\mu}\int_{s}^{t}w_{r}\|P_{r-s}f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,dr

\displaystyle\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\|w\|_{L_{\infty}([S,T])}|\psi_{s}-\psi_{s}|^{\mu}\int_{s}^{t}(r-s)^{\frac{\gamma-\mu}{2}}\,dr

\displaystyle\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\|w\|_{L_{\infty}([S,T])}|\psi_{s}-\psi_{s}|^{\mu}(t-s)^{1+\frac{\gamma-\mu}{2}}.

for
C=C(\gamma,\mu,d)
. Therefore, by Jensen’s inequality we get for
m\geq 2

\|A_{s,t}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\|w\|_{L_{\infty}([S,T])}\|\varphi-\psi\|^{\mu}_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([S,T])}(t-s)^{1+\frac{\gamma-\mu}{2}}.

(4.22)

By (
4.17
), we have
1+\frac{\gamma-\mu}{2}>1/2
. Therefore condition (
2.10
) of
Theorem

2.3
holds.

Next, using (
4.3
), we easily get for
(s,u,t)\in\Delta^{3}_{[S,T]}
,
m\geq 2

\displaystyle\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\delta A_{s,u,t}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\delta A^{\varphi}_{s,u,t}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}+\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\delta A^{\psi}_{s,u,t}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\|w\|_{L_{\infty}([S,T])}(t-s)^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma}{2}+\tau}([\varphi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{m}([S,T])}+[\psi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{m}([S,T])}),

(4.23)

where
C=C(\gamma,d)
. We see from (
4.15
)
\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma}{2}+\tau>1
and thus (
2.11
) holds.

Thus all the conditions of the SSL holds and the desired bound (
4.18
) follows from (
2.12
) and (
4.22
), (
4.3
).

To obtain (
4.16
) and (
4.18
) for general
f
, we note that for any
\varepsilon>0
the function

f_{\varepsilon}:=P_{\varepsilon}f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
. Therefore, bounds (
4.16
) and (
4.18
) hold for
f_{\varepsilon}
. Since
f_{\varepsilon}
converges pointwise to
f
as
\varepsilon\to 0
and
\|f_{\varepsilon}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\leq\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}
, by Fatou’s lemma we see that these bounds hold also for
f
. ∎

Now we can prove the corollary discussed at the beginning of the subsection. We bound the moments of the right-hand side of (
4.12
) in terms of the difference of the processes multiplied by
\lambda
to a negative power.

Corollary 4.7
.

Let
m\geq 2
,
\gamma\in(-1,0)
,
\tau\in(0,1]
,
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
. Let
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be a bounded continuous function.
Let
\varphi,\psi\colon[0,T]\times D\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be continuous measurable processes adapted to the filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
.
Assume that (
4.15
) holds. Then there exists a constant
C=C(\gamma,\varepsilon,\tau,d,m)>0
such that for any
\lambda>1
,
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
we have

\Bigl\|\int_{s}^{t}e^{-\lambda(t-r)}f(W_{r}+\varphi_{r})\,dr\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(t-s)^{\varepsilon}\lambda^{-1-\frac{\gamma}{2}+3\varepsilon}(1+[\varphi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{m}([s,t])}\lambda^{\frac{1}{2}-\tau}).

(4.24)

If, additionally, for
\mu\in(0,1]
condition (
4.17
) is satisfied,
then there exists a constant
C=C(\gamma,\varepsilon,\mu,\tau,d,m)>0
such that for any
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
,
we have

\displaystyle\Bigl\|\int_{s}^{t}e^{-\lambda(t-r)}(f(W_{r}+\varphi_{r})-f(W_{r}+\psi_{r}))\,dr\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\quad\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(t-s)^{\varepsilon}\lambda^{-1-\frac{\gamma-\mu}{2}+3\varepsilon}\|\varphi-\psi\|^{\mu}_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([s,t])}

\displaystyle\qquad+C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(t-s)^{\varepsilon}\lambda^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{2}-\tau+3\varepsilon}([\varphi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{m}([s,t])}+[\psi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{m}([s,t])}).

(4.25)

Proof.

We deduce only (
4.24
) from (
4.16
). Bound (
4.7
) is derived from (
4.18
) using exactly the same argument.

We fix
\varepsilon\in(0,1/2)
,
\lambda>1
,
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
and as discussed in (
4.14
) split the integral into two

\displaystyle\Bigl\|\int_{s}^{t}e^{-\lambda(t-r)}f(W_{r}+\varphi_{r})\,dr\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\Bigl\|\int_{s}^{(t-\lambda^{-1+\varepsilon})\vee s}e^{-\lambda(t-r)}f(W_{r}+\varphi_{r})\,dr\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq}+\Bigl\|\int_{(t-\lambda^{-1+\varepsilon})\vee s}^{t}e^{-\lambda(t-r)}f(W_{r}+\varphi_{r})\,dr\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=:I_{1}+I_{2}.

(4.26)

If
r\leq t-\lambda^{-1+\varepsilon}
, then
e^{-\lambda(t-r)}\leq e^{-\lambda^{\varepsilon}}
and
\|e^{-\lambda(t-\cdot)}\|_{L_{\infty}([s,(t-\lambda^{-1+\varepsilon})\vee s])}\leq e^{-\lambda^{\varepsilon}}
. Therefore, (
4.16
) implies

I_{1}\leq Ce^{-\lambda^{\varepsilon}}\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(t-s)^{\varepsilon}(1+[\varphi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{m}([s,t])}).

(4.27)

for
C=C(\gamma,\tau,d,m)
, where we allso used that
1+\frac{\gamma}{2}>1/2>\varepsilon
.

To bound
I_{2}
, we use the obvious inequality
\|e^{-\lambda(t-\cdot)}\|_{L_{\infty}([t-\lambda^{-1+\varepsilon},t])}\leq 1
.
Since

(t-((t-\lambda^{-1+\varepsilon})\vee s))\leq t-s\qquad\text{ and }\qquad(t-((t-\lambda^{-1+\varepsilon})\vee s))\leq\lambda^{-1+\varepsilon},

we also have for any
\rho\in[\varepsilon,2]

(t-((t-\lambda^{-1+\varepsilon})\vee s))^{\rho}\leq(t-s)^{\varepsilon}\lambda^{(-1+\varepsilon)(\rho-\varepsilon)}\leq(t-s)^{\varepsilon}\lambda^{-\rho+3\varepsilon}.

Applying this inequality with
\rho=1+\frac{\gamma}{2}
and
\gamma=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma}{2}+\tau
, we derive from (
4.16
)

I_{2}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(t-s)^{\varepsilon}\lambda^{-1-\frac{\gamma}{2}+3\varepsilon}(1+[\varphi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{m}([s,t])}\lambda^{\frac{1}{2}-\tau})

for
C=C(\gamma,\tau,d,m)
. Combining this with (
4.27
) and substituting into (
4.3
), we obtain (
4.24
); here we used that the function
\lambda\mapsto e^{-\lambda^{\varepsilon}}
decreases to
0
faster than any negative polynomial function as
\lambda\to\infty
.
∎

4.4
Weak existence

Before we proceed to the weak uniqueness of solutions to the SDE (
4.3
), let us first discuss the weak existence of solutions.
In this subsection, we mostly follow the arguments of
[
GG25
, Section 8]
.

Although the definition of a weak solution to an SDE with a distributional drift differs from the usual one (recall
Definition

4.4
), the proof strategy is similar. First, we establish an a priori regularity bound. Then we consider a sequence of solutions to approximated equations with regularized drifts and show that this sequence is tight. Finally, we show that any of its limit points is a weak solution. As always, we work with solutions to SDEs driven by a Brownian motion, but the same techniques can be easily transferred (at the cost of increased technical complexity) to SDEs driven by a non-Markov process, for example, fractional Brownian motion.

The good news is that we do not need any new technical bounds; everything follows from the already established bound (
4.16
).

Before we begin, we need to establish the following very useful technical tool. Clearly, a uniform local bound on the Hölder norm of a function over all intervals of length at most
\ell>0
yields a global bound on its Hölder norm, with a constant depending only on
\ell
. The next lemma establishes essentially the same result for the
[\cdot]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{m}([0,1])}
seminorm; recall its definition in (
3.8
).

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
m\geq 1
,
\tau\in(0,1]
,
\Gamma,\ell\geq 0
. Let
f
be a measurable function
\Omega\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

1.

Assume that for every
(S,T)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
with
T-S\leq\ell
we have

[f]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{m}([S,T])}\leq\Gamma.

(4.28)

Then

[f]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{m}([0,1])}\leq\Gamma(\ell^{\tau-1}+1).

(4.29)

2.

Let
M>0
. Suppose now that
f_{0}\equiv 0
and for every
(S,T)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
with
T-S\leq\ell
we have

[f]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{m}([S,T])}\leq M\|f_{S}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}+\Gamma.

(4.30)

Then there exists a constant
C=C(\tau,\ell,M)>0
independent of
\Gamma
such that

[f]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{m}([0,1])}\leq C\Gamma.

Proof.

(i). Let
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
. Let
N
be the smallest integer such that
(t-s)/N\leq\ell
. We partition
[s,t]
into
N
subintervals
[t_{i},t_{i+1}]
,
i=0,...,N-1
, each of length
(t-s)/N
, and sum the increments of
f
using (
4.28
):

\|f_{t}-f_{s}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\|f_{t_{i+1}}-f_{t_{i}}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq N^{1-\tau}\Gamma(t-s)^{\tau}.

Dividing this by
(t-s)^{\tau}
and using
N\leq(t-s)/\ell+1\leq 1/\ell+1
, we get

\frac{\|f_{t}-f_{s}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}}{(t-s)^{\tau}}\leq\Gamma(\ell^{\tau-1}+1),

which yields (
4.29
).

(ii). Let
N=\lceil 1/\ell\rceil
. Fix a partition
0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\dots<t_{N}=1
with
t_{i+1}-t_{i}\leq\ell

It follows from (
4.30
) and the definition of the seminorm in (
3.8
) that

\|f_{t_{i+1}}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq\|f_{t_{i}}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}+(t_{i+1}-t_{i})^{\tau}[f]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{m}([t_{i},t_{i+1}])}\leq\|f_{t_{i}}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}(1+\ell^{\tau}M)+\ell^{\tau}\Gamma,

where
i\in\{0,1,\ldots,N-1\}
. Starting from
i=0
and iterating the above bound
k
times,
k=1,\ldots,N
, we get

\|f_{t_{k}}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq N(1+\ell^{\tau}M)^{N}\ell^{\tau}\Gamma=C\Gamma,

for
C=C(\tau,\ell,M)
.
Substituting this back into (
4.30
), we deduce

\sup_{k\in\{0,1,\ldots,N-1\}}[f]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{m}([t_{k},t_{k+1}])}\leq C\Gamma

(4.31)

for
C=C(\tau,\ell,M)
.

Take now arbitrary
(S,T)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
with
T-S\leq\ell
. If for some
0\leq i\leq N-1
we have
t_{i}\leq S\leq T\leq t_{i+1}
, then (
4.31
) implies

[f]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{m}([S,T])}\leq C\Gamma.

Otherwise, there exists
i\in[1,N-1]
such that
t_{i-1}\leq S\leq t_{i}\leq T\leq t_{i+1}
. Then we get using (
4.31
)

\|f_{T}-f_{S}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq\|f_{T}-f_{t_{i}}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}+\|f_{S}-f_{t_{i}}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq C(|T-t_{i}|^{\tau}+|S-t_{i}|^{\tau})\Gamma\leq C|T-S|^{\tau}\Gamma.

Thus, in both cases, we have

[f]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{m}([S,T])}\leq C\Gamma

for
C=C(\tau,\ell,M)
. Therefore, by part (i) of the lemma, we finally derive

[f]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{m}([0,1])}\leq C\Gamma.\qed

Now we are ready to implement our weak existence proof strategy.

We begin with an a priori bound on the time regularity of the drift
\int b(X_{r})\,dr
of the solution to (
4.3
). Let us start with some heuristics, omitting as usual the arbitrarily small exponents. Clearly,
W\in\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}([0,1])
, which suggests that the solution
X
to (
4.3
) should also belong to
\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}([0,1])
. The function
b
lies in
\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for some negative
\gamma
, so, very informally, we may expect “
b(X)
” to be in
\mathcal{C}^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}([0,1])
. We gain
1
in regularity by integration, so we expect that the drift
t\mapsto\int_{0}^{t}b(X_{r})\,dr
belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}([0,1])
. This is essentially the statement of our first lemma, modulo the fact that, as usual, we measure regularity not a.s. in the
\mathcal{C}^{\tau}
scale but rather in the
\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{m}
scale; recall the corresponding definition in (
3.8
).

We derive a general a priori bound, which we will also reuse later in the uniqueness part of the proof.

Lemma 4.9

(General a priori estimate)
.

Let
m\in[2,\infty)
,
\gamma\in(-\frac{1}{2},0)
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Let
(f_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of bounded continuous functions
\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Let
\psi,g,Z\colon\Omega\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be measurable processes adapted to the filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
which satisfy

Z_{t}=x+\psi_{t}+\int_{0}^{t}g_{r}dr+W_{t},\quad t\in[0,1]

and

\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\Bigl|\psi_{t}-\int_{0}^{t}f_{k}(Z_{r})\,dr\Bigr|\to 0\quad\text{in probability as }k\to\infty.

If

[Z-W]_{\mathcal{C}^{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}L_{m}([0,1])}<\infty

(4.32)

then

[Z-W]_{\mathcal{C}^{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}L_{m}([0,1])}\leq CF(1+F)(1+\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([0,1])})

(4.33)

for
C=C(\gamma,d,m)>0
and
F:=\sup_{k}\|f_{k}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}
.

Note again that even though we assumed
f_{k}
to be a sequence of bounded continuous functions, the
\mathcal{C}^{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}L_{m}
norm of the drift does not depend on the supremum norm of
f_{k}
. Instead, it depends on the much weaker norm
\|f_{k}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}
. This will allow us to pass to the limit later.
The condition
\gamma>-\frac{1}{2}
is precisely the condition
\frac{\gamma}{2}+\tau>\frac{1}{2}
from
Lemma

4.6
, with
\tau=1+\frac{\gamma}{2}
, as motivated by the heuristics above.

Proof.

The proof relies on bound (
4.16
). Fix
m\geq 2
. First we note that for any
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
we get by Fatou’s lemma

\displaystyle\|(Z_{t}-W_{t})-(Z_{s}-W_{s})\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\|\psi_{t}-\psi_{s}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}+\int_{s}^{t}\|g_{r}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\,dr

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{k\to\infty}\Bigl\|\int_{s}^{t}f_{k}(Z_{r})\,dr\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\!\!\!\!+(t-s)\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([0,1])}.

(4.34)

For
k\in\mathbb{N}
let us apply
Lemma

4.6
with the following set of parameters:
f=f_{k}
,
w\equiv 1
,
\tau=1+\frac{\gamma}{2}
. Since
\gamma>-\frac{1}{2}
, we see that condition (
4.15
) holds. Therefore we get from (
4.16
) that there exists
C=C(\gamma,d,m)>0
such that for any
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}

\Bigl\|\int_{s}^{t}f_{k}(Z_{r})\,dr\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq CF(t-s)^{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}\bigl(1+[Z-W]_{\mathcal{C}^{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}L_{m}([s,t])}(t-s)^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}\bigr).

Substituting this into (
4.4
), we get

\|(Z_{t}-W_{t})-(Z_{s}-W_{s})\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq CF(t-s)^{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}\bigl(1+\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([0,1])}+[Z-W]_{\mathcal{C}^{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}L_{m}([s,t])}(t-s)^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}\bigr).

Fix now
(S,T)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
. By dividing both sides of the above inequality by
(t-s)^{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}
and taking
supremum over all
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[S,T]}
, we get (recall that
\gamma>-1/2
)

[Z-W]_{\mathcal{C}^{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}L_{m}([S,T])}\leq C_{0}F(1+\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([0,1])})+C_{0}F[Z-W]_{\mathcal{C}^{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}L_{m}([S,T])}(T-S)^{\frac{1}{4}}.

Choose now
\ell>0
small enough such that

C_{0}F\ell^{\frac{1}{4}}=\frac{1}{2}.

Recall that
[Z-W]_{\mathcal{C}^{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}L_{m}([S,T])}
is finite thanks to our assumption (
4.32
). Then substituting this in the above inequality we get for any
(S,T)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
with
T-S\leq\ell

[\psi]_{\mathcal{C}^{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}L_{m}([S,T])}\leq 2C_{0}F(1+\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([0,1])}).

By
Lemma

4.8
(i) this implies

[\psi]_{\mathcal{C}^{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}L_{m}([0,1])}\leq 2C_{0}F(1+\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([0,1])})(\ell^{-\frac{1}{4}}+1)\leq CF(1+F)(1+\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([0,1])})

for
C=C(\gamma,d,m)
. This implies (
4.33
).
∎

Next, let us consider a sequence of strong solutions to approximated equations

X_{t}^{n}=x+\int_{0}^{t}b^{n}(X^{n}_{t})dt+W_{t},\quad t\in[0,1],

(4.35)

where
b_{n}:=P_{1/n}b
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We see that
(b_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of smooth functions
\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
converging to
b
in
\mathcal{C}^{\gamma-\varepsilon}
for any
\varepsilon>0
, see
4.18
. Using
Lemma

4.9
, let us now show that the sequence
(X^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight.

Lemma 4.10

(Tightness)
.

Suppose that
\gamma>-\frac{1}{2}
. Then the sequence
(X^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
defined in (
4.35
) is tight in the space
\mathcal{C}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Proof.

Fix the initial data
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and denote for brevity
\varphi^{n}:=X^{n}-W
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
. For a fixed
n\in\mathbb{N}
we apply
Lemma

4.9
with
Z=X^{n}
,
\psi=\int_{0}^{t}b^{n}(X^{n})\,dr
,
g\equiv 0
and let all functions
f^{k}
to be equal to
b^{n}
. We see that for any
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
we have

|(X^{n}_{t}-W_{t})-(X^{n}_{s}-W_{s})|\leq\|b^{n}\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}|t-s|,

and therefore condition (
4.32
) is satisfied. Thus it follows from (
4.33
) that for any
m\geq 1
there exists a
constant
C=C(\gamma,d,m)>0
such that for any
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}

\|\varphi^{n}_{t}-\varphi^{n}_{s}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq C\|b^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(1+\|b^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}})|t-s|^{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}\leq C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(1+\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}})|t-s|^{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}},

where we also used that
\|b^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\leq\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}
by
4.18
.

Therefore by the Kolmogorov continuity theorem (recall
Theorem

3.12
), we have

\|[\varphi^{n}]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(1+\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}),

(4.36)

for
C=C(\gamma,d,m)>0
.

Denote now for
M>0

A_{M}:=\big\{f\in\mathcal{C}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{d}):f(0)=x\text{ and }[f]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq M\big\}.

By the Arzela–Ascoli theorem, for each
M>0
the set
A_{M}
is a compact set in the space
\mathcal{C}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Furthermore, it follows from (
4.36
) and the Chebyshev inequality that

\mathsf{P}(\varphi_{n}\notin A_{M})\leq M^{-1}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt[\psi_{n}]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(1+\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}})M^{-1}.

Thus, the sequence
(\varphi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight in
\mathcal{C}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Since the process
W
does not depend on
n
, we get that

(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is also tight in
\mathcal{C}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
∎

Now we are finally ready to show weak existence of the solutions to SDE (
4.3
). Namely, as promised in the beginning of this subsection, we will show that any of the limiting points of the sequence
(X^{n})
is a weak solution, that is, it satisfies the conditions of
Definition

4.4
.
We will rely again on the very powerful a priori estimates (
Lemma

4.9
).

Theorem 4.11
.

Let
\gamma\in(-\frac{1}{2},0)
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
b\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}
. Then equation (
4.3
) has a weak solution in the sense of
Definition

4.4
.

Proof.

Step 1
. We know from
Lemma

4.10
that sequence
(X^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight in
\mathcal{C}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Obviously, the constant sequence
(W)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight in this space as well. Hence the sequence
(X^{n},W)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight in
\mathcal{C}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{2d})
.
Since this space is separable, by the Prokhorov theorem there exists
a subsequence
(n_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
such that
(X_{n_{k}},W)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
converges weakly in the space
\mathcal{C}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{2d})
. By passing to an appropriate subsequence and applying the Skorokhod representation theorem, we see that there exists a random element
(\widehat{X},\widehat{W})
and a sequence of random elements
(\widehat{X}_{n},\widehat{W}_{n})
defined on a common probability space
(\widehat{\Omega},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},\widehat{P})

such that
\law(\widehat{X}_{n},\widehat{W}_{n})=\law(X_{n},W_{n})
and

\|(\widehat{X}_{n},\widehat{W}_{n})-(\widehat{X},\widehat{W})\|_{\mathcal{C}([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{2d})}\to 0\quad\text{as $n\to\infty$ a.s.}

(4.37)

Now let us show that
(\widehat{X},\widehat{W})
is a weak solution to (
4.3
). We will need to check two things: a) that for some filtration
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}
the process
\widehat{X}
is adapted to
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}
and
\widehat{W}
is an
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}
-Brownian motion; b) that
(\widehat{X},\widehat{W})
satisfies
Definition

4.4
.

Step 2a
.
Obviously, we have
\law(\widehat{W})=\law(\widehat{W}_{n})=\law(W)
, and thus,
\widehat{W}_{n}
,
\widehat{W}
are standard Brownian motions. For
t\in[0,1]
, put
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}:=\sigma(\widehat{W}_{s},\widehat{X}_{s};s\leq t)
. By definition,
\widehat{X}
is adapted to
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}
. We claim now that
\widehat{W}
is an
(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t})
-Brownian motion.

Indeed, for any
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
,
n,N\in\mathbb{N}
, any bounded continuous functions
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,

g\colon\mathbb{R}^{2Nd}\to\mathbb{R}
, and any time points
0\leq t_{1}\leq\ldots\leq t_{N}\leq s
one has

\displaystyle\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057ptf(\widehat{B}_{n}(t)-\widehat{B}_{n}(s))g\bigl(\widehat{B}_{n}(t_{1}),\ldots,\widehat{B}_{n}(t_{N}),\widehat{X}_{n}(t_{1}),\ldots,\widehat{X}_{n}(t_{N})\bigr)

\displaystyle\quad=\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057ptf(\widehat{B}_{n}(t)-\widehat{B}_{n}(s))\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057ptg\bigl(\widehat{B}_{n}(t_{1}),\ldots,\widehat{B}_{n}(t_{N}),\widehat{X}_{n}(t_{1}),\ldots,\widehat{X}_{n}(t_{N})\bigr),

since
\widehat{B}_{n}(t)-\widehat{B}_{n}(s)
is independent of
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{n}_{s}:=\sigma(\widehat{B}^{n}_{r},\widehat{X}_{n}(r);s\leq t)
. By passing to the limit in the above expression as
n\to\infty
using (
4.37
), we derive that the same identity holds also for
(\widehat{B},\widehat{X})
, Therefore
\widehat{B}(t)-\widehat{B}(s)
is independent of
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{s}
for any
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
. Thus,
\widehat{B}
is an
(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t})
-Brownian motion.

Step 2b
. Let us define

\psi_{t}:=\widehat{X}_{t}-x-\widehat{W}_{t},\quad t\in[0,1].

(4.38)

We see that part (i) of
Definition

4.4
holds by construction.

To check part (ii) of
Definition

4.4
, we put for
n,k\in\mathbb{N}
,
t\in[0,1]

\displaystyle\psi_{n}(t):=\int_{0}^{t}b^{n}(\widehat{X}(r))dr,\qquad\psi_{n,k}(t):=\int_{0}^{t}b^{n}(\widehat{X}^{k}(r))dr.

(4.39)

Our goal is to prove that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\Big|\int_{0}^{t}b^{n}(\widehat{X}_{r})\,dr-\psi(t)\Big|=\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\Big|\psi_{n}(t)-\psi(t)\Big|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle???}}{{=}}0\,\,\text{in probability}.

We split this expression into several parts. Let
n,k\in\mathbb{N}
. We have

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|\psi_{n}(t)-\psi(t)|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|\psi_{n}(t)-\psi_{n,k}(t)|+\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|\psi_{n,k}(t)-\psi_{k,k}(t)|+\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|\psi_{k,k}-\psi(t)|

\displaystyle=:I_{1}(n,k)+I_{2}(n,k)+I_{3}(k).

(4.40)

It is easy to deal with the first term. We have for any fixed
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}

\lim_{k\to\infty}I_{1}(n,k)\leq\lim_{k\to\infty}\|b^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|\widehat{X}-\widehat{X}^{k}\|_{\mathcal{C}([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{d})}=0

(4.41)

by (
4.37
). Similarly, it is also immediate to treat the last term.
Recall that
(\widehat{X}_{k},\widehat{W}_{k})
solves equaiton (
4.3
) with the drift
b^{k}
. Therefore,
\widehat{X}_{k}=x+\psi_{k,k}+\widehat{W}_{k}
. Hence, by definition of
\psi
in (
4.38
) and
\psi_{k,k}
in (
4.39
), we have

\lim_{k\to\infty}I_{3}(k)=\lim_{k\to\infty}\|\widehat{X}_{k}-\widehat{W}_{k}-(\widehat{X}-\widehat{W})\|_{\mathcal{C}([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{d})}=0

(4.42)

thanks to the convergence (
4.37
).

It remains to bound
I_{2}(n,k)
. This is a bit less trivial, but we will rely on the a priori bound and the Kolmogorov continuity theorem to interchange the order of taking the supremum in time and the expectation. Thus, we fix
\varepsilon>0
so that
\gamma>-\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon
.
We apply
Lemma

4.6
with
f=b^{n}-b^{k}
,
\gamma-\varepsilon
in place of
\gamma
,
\varphi=\widehat{X}^{k}-\widehat{W}^{k}
,
\tau=1+\frac{\gamma}{2}
,
w\equiv 1
. We see that condition (
4.15
) is satisfied since
\gamma>-\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon
. Therefore it follows from (
4.16
) that for any
m\geq 2
there exists a constant
C=C(\gamma,\varepsilon,d,m)>0
such that for any
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
,
n,k\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\displaystyle\|(\psi_{n,k}(t)-\psi_{k,k}(t))-(\psi_{n,k}(s)-\psi_{k,k}(s))\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\quad=\Bigl\|\int_{s}^{t}(b^{n}-b^{k})(\widehat{X}^{k}(r))\,dr\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\quad\leq C\|b^{n}-b^{k}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma-\varepsilon}}|t-s|^{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}-\varepsilon}(1+[\widehat{X}^{k}-\widehat{W}^{k}]_{\mathcal{C}^{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}L_{m}([0,1])}\bigr)

\displaystyle\quad\leq C\|b^{n}-b^{k}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma-\varepsilon}}|t-s|^{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}-\varepsilon}

for
C=C(\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}},\gamma,\varepsilon,d,m)
. Here in the last inequality we used the a priori bound in
Lemma

4.9
. Therefore,
by the Kolmogorov continuity theorem (
Theorem

3.12
) we finally get

\displaystyle\|I_{2}(n,k)\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=\|\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|\psi_{n,k}(t)-\psi_{k,k}(t)|\,\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}\leq\|[\psi_{n,k}-\psi_{k,k}]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}}\,\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C\|b^{n}-b^{k}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma-\varepsilon}}\to 0\,\,\text{as $k,n\to\infty$}.

Combining this with (
4.41
) and (
4.42
) and passing to the limit in (
4.4
) first as
k\to\infty
and then as
n\to\infty
, we finally get

\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|\psi_{n}(t)-\psi(t)|\to 0,\quad\text{in probability as $n\to\infty$},

and thus part (ii) of
Definition

4.4
holds.

Step 3
. Thus, we conclude. By Step 2a,
\widehat{X}
is adapted to the filtration
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}
and
\widehat{W}
is an
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}
-Brownian motion. By Step 2b the pair
(\widehat{X},\widehat{W})
satisfies
Definition

4.4
. Hence
(\widehat{X},\widehat{W})
is a weak solution to equation (
4.3
).
∎

Let us discuss the obtained result a bit. We have just shown that any limiting point of the sequence
(X^{n})
is a weak solution to (
4.3
). For this, we used the standard SSL to obtain the integral bound
Lemma

4.6
, which in turn implied tightness. In the next subsection, we show that all limiting points actually have the same law. Moreover, we will show that if
X
is
any
weak solution, then our fixed sequence of approximations
(X^{n})
converges weakly to
X
, which yields weak uniqueness. To establish this, the SSL alone will not suffice: we will also rely on the generalized coupling technique.

As is standard in the analysis of SDEs or SPDEs with distributional drift, we do not consider all solutions of (
4.3
), but rather restrict ourselves to those whose drifts have a certain regularity, and later establish a
conditional
weak uniqueness (that is, weak uniqueness only among such solutions). More precisely, we consider the following class of functions.

Definition 4.12
.

Let
\kappa\in(0,1]
. We say that a solution
(X,W)
to (
4.3
) belongs to the class
\mathcal{V}(\kappa)
if for any
m\geq 2
we have for
\psi:=X-W

\sup_{(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}}\frac{\|\psi_{t}-\psi_{s}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}}{|t-s|^{\kappa}}<\infty.

We see that, as usual, it is more convenient to measure the regularity of the drift in the
\mathcal{C}^{\kappa}L_{m}
norm rather than in the
\mathcal{C}^{\kappa}
norm. It is also almost immediate that any limiting point of the sequence
(X^{n})
belongs to
\mathcal{V}(1+\frac{\gamma}{2})
. Let us provide a short proof.

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
(X,B)
be a limiting point of the sequence
(X^{n},W)_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
defined in (
4.35
). Then
(X,B)\in\mathcal{V}(1+\frac{\gamma}{2})
.

Proof.

Let
m\geq 1
. We have from
Lemma

4.9
and
4.18

\sup_{(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}}\frac{\|(X^{n}_{t}-W_{t})-(X^{n}_{s}-W_{s})\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}}{|t-s|^{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}}<C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(1+\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}^{3}).

for
C=C(\gamma,d,m)
.
Therefore, by Fatou’s lemma we get

\sup_{(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}}\frac{\|(X_{t}-B_{t})-(X_{s}-B_{s})\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}}{|t-s|^{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}}<C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(1+\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}^{3}),

which implies that
X\in\mathcal{V}(1+\frac{\gamma}{2})
.
∎

4.5
Stochastic sewing with generalized couplings for weak uniqueness

We now have all the tools to rigorously implement the strategy sketched in
Section

4.2
and establish weak uniqueness of solutions to (
4.3
) that belong to the class
\mathcal{V}(1+\frac{\gamma}{2})
.

Thus, let
(X,W)
be
any
weak solution to (
4.3
) in the sense of
Definition

4.4
. Assume that
X\in\mathcal{V}(1+\frac{\gamma}{2})
. Let
X^{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, be a solution to the regularized equation (
4.8
). Introduce an auxiliary process
\widetilde{X}^{n}
which is defined on the same space as
(X,W)
and solves

\widetilde{X}^{n}_{t}=x+\int_{0}^{t}b^{n}(\widetilde{X}^{n}_{r})dr+\lambda\int_{0}^{t}(X_{r}-\widetilde{X}^{n}_{r})dt+W_{t},\quad t\in[0,1],

(4.43)

where the parameter
\lambda>1
will be fixed later. We see that since the function
b^{n}
is smooth, the process
\widetilde{X}^{n}
is well-defined. We decompose the distance between
X
and
X^{n}
as

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}_{\|\cdot\|\wedge 1}(\law(X^{n}),\law(X))

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{W}_{\|\cdot\|\wedge 1}(\law(X^{n}),\law(\widetilde{X}^{n}))+\mathbb{W}_{\|\cdot\|\wedge 1}(\law(\widetilde{X}^{n}),\law(X))

\displaystyle\leq d_{TV}(\law(X^{n}),\law(\widetilde{X}^{n}))+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt\|\widetilde{X}^{n}-X\|_{\mathcal{C}([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

(4.44)

As we explained in
Section

4.2
, to bound
d_{TV}(\law(X^{n}),\law(\widetilde{X}^{n}))
, we use the Girsanov theorem. The following general statement provides bounds on the total variation distance between the solutions of two SDEs.

Lemma 4.14
.

(
[
BKS20
, Theorem A.2]
,
[
Ü09
, Theorem 8]
)
Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Let
g\colon\Omega\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a measurable adapted function such that
\int_{0}^{1}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt|g_{r}|^{2}\,dr<\infty
.
Let
Y,Z\colon\Omega\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be strong solutions to the following SDEs:

dY_{t}=f(Y_{t})dt+dW_{t};\quad dZ_{t}=f(Z_{t})dt+g_{t}dt+dW_{t},\qquad t\in[0,1]

with the initial condition
Y_{0}=Z_{0}=x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then there exists a constant
C>0
such that

d_{TV}(\law(Y),\law(Z))\leq\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(\int_{0}^{1}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt|g_{r}|^{2}\,dr\Bigr)^{1/2}.

(4.45)

Proof.

Since the additional drift term
g
might be unbounded, we combine the Girsanov theorem with a localization argument.
For
N>0
consider a stopping time

\tau_{N}:=\inf\{t\in[0,1]:\int_{0}^{t}|g_{r}|^{2}\,dr\geq N\}

and put

\widetilde{W}_{t}^{N}:=W_{t}+\int_{0}^{t}g_{r}\mathbbm{1}(r\leq\tau_{N})\,dr.

By definition, the process
t\mapsto g_{t}\mathbbm{1}(t\leq\tau_{N})
satisfies the Novikov condition and therefore
by the Girsanov theorem, there exists an equivalent probability measure
\widetilde{\mathsf{P}}^{N}
such that the process
\widetilde{W}^{N}
is an
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
- Brownian motion under this measure and

\frac{d\widetilde{\mathsf{P}}^{N}}{d\mathsf{P}}=\exp\Bigl(-\int_{0}^{1}g_{r}\mathbbm{1}(r\leq\tau_{N})\,dW_{r}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}|g_{r}|^{2}\mathbbm{1}(r\leq\tau_{N})\,ds\Bigr),

(4.46)

Let
Z^{N}
be a strong solution to SDE

Z^{N}(t)=x+\int_{0}^{t}f(Z^{N}(r))\,dr+\widetilde{W}_{t}^{N},\quad t\in[0,1].

(4.47)

on the space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\widetilde{\mathsf{P}}^{N})
.
Z^{N}
is well-defined since the function
f
is Lipschitz by assumption. We clearly have

d_{TV}(\law_{\mathsf{P}}(Y),\law_{\mathsf{P}}(Z))\leq d_{TV}(\law_{\mathsf{P}}(Y),\law_{\mathsf{P}}(Z^{N}))+d_{TV}(\law_{\mathsf{P}}(Z^{N}),\law_{\mathsf{P}}(Z)).

(4.48)

We see that
(Z^{N},\widetilde{W}^{N})
is a strong solution to SDE (
4.47
) on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\widetilde{\mathsf{P}}^{N})
and
(Y,W)
is a strong solution to exactly the same equation on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathsf{P})
. Since weak uniqueness holds for SDE (
4.47
) (recall that the function
f
is Lipschitz) we have
\law_{\mathsf{P}}(Y)=\law_{\widetilde{\mathsf{P}}^{N}}(Z_{N})
.
Then, using Pinsker’s inequality (
Proposition

4.5
) and the explicit formula for the density (
4.46
) we derive

\displaystyle d_{TV}(\law_{\mathsf{P}}(Y),\law_{\mathsf{P}}(Z_{N})

\displaystyle=d_{TV}(\law_{\widetilde{\mathsf{P}}^{N}}(Z_{N}),\law_{\mathsf{P}}(Z_{N}))\leq d_{TV}(\mathsf{P},\widetilde{\mathsf{P}}^{N})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Bigl(\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\mathsf{P}}\log\frac{d\mathsf{P}}{d\widetilde{\mathsf{P}}^{N}}\Bigr)^{1/2}=\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(\int_{0}^{1}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\mathsf{P}}|g_{r}|^{2}\mathbbm{1}(r\leq\tau_{N})\,dr\Bigr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(\int_{0}^{1}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\mathsf{P}}|g_{r}|^{2}\,dr\Bigr)^{1/2}.

(4.49)

Here we used that
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt\int_{0}^{1}g_{r}\mathbbm{1}(r\leq\tau_{N})\,dW_{r}=0
since
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt\int_{0}^{1}|g_{r}|^{2}\mathbbm{1}(r\leq\tau_{N})\,dr
is finite by definition.

To bound the second term in the right-hand side of (
4.48
), we note that on the set
\{\tau_{N}=\infty\}
the processes
Z^{N}
and
Z
coincide. Therefore,

d_{TV}(\law_{\mathsf{P}}(Z_{N}),\law_{\mathsf{P}}(Z))\leq\mathsf{P}(\tau_{N}<\infty).

Now we combine this with (
4.5
) and substitute into (
4.48
). We get

d_{TV}(\law_{\mathsf{P}}(Y),\law_{\mathsf{P}}(Z))\leq\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(\int_{0}^{1}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\mathsf{P}}|g_{r}|^{2}\,dr\Bigr)^{1/2}+\mathsf{P}(\tau_{N}<\infty).

Since
N
was arbitrary, by passing to the limit as
N\to\infty
, we get (
4.45
).
∎

Using
Lemma

4.14
, it is immediate to bound the first term on the right-hand side of (
4.5
).

Corollary 4.15
.

We have

d_{TV}(\law(X^{n}),\law(\widetilde{X}^{n}))\leq\frac{1}{2}\lambda\|\|X-\widetilde{X}^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}.

Proof.

Follows immediately from
Lemma

4.14
by taking
Y=X^{n}
,
Z=\widetilde{X}^{n}
,
f=b^{n}
,
g=\lambda(X-\widetilde{X}^{n})
.
∎

Now we move on to bounding the second term on the right-hand side of (
4.5
). Fortunately, we are completely prepared by now and can rely on the integral bounds in
Lemma

4.6
, which were established using the SSL.

Lemma 4.16
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
such that

5\varepsilon<\frac{1}{2}+\gamma.

(4.50)

there exist constants
C=C(\varepsilon)
,
\lambda_{0}=\lambda_{0}(\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}},\gamma,\varepsilon,d,m)>1
such that for any

\lambda>\lambda_{0}

one has

\bigl\|\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|X_{t}-\widetilde{X}^{n}_{t}|\bigr\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}\leq C\lambda^{-\frac{3}{2}-\gamma+11\varepsilon}+C\|b^{n}-b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma-\varepsilon}}\lambda^{-1-\frac{\gamma}{2}+7\varepsilon}.

(4.51)

Before we begin the proof, let us just recall that the process
\widetilde{X}^{n}
depends on
\lambda
, so we cannot simply take
\lambda\to\infty
in (
4.51
) to show that
\bigl\|\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|X_{t}-\widetilde{X}^{n}_{t}|\bigr\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}
is small. Nevertheless, recalling our heuristics in
Section

4.2
, we see that the key thing is that in the first term
\lambda
appears with a power smaller than
-1
, so we can “pay the price” of
\lambda
when we plug this bound into
Corollary

4.15
.

Proof.

To bound the
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt\|\widetilde{X}^{n}-X\|_{\mathcal{C}([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{d})}
, we rely, as usual, on the Kolmogorov continuity theorem. That is, we first bound
\|\widetilde{X}^{n}-X\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\varepsilon}L_{m}([0,1])}
for some very small
\varepsilon>0
and sufficiently large
m
, and then interchange the order of taking the expectation and the
\mathcal{C}^{\varepsilon}([0,1])
norm.

We take
m:=2/\varepsilon\geq 2
and our goal is to show that
\|\widetilde{X}^{n}-X\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\varepsilon}L_{m}([0,1])}
can be bounded by a half of itself plus
\lambda^{\text{(some negative power smaller than $-1$)}}
. To achieve this we will follow the strategy of
Section

4.2
with the following technical modification: since
b
is a distribution, we cannot even define the right-hand side of (
4.12
). To make everything rigorous, we introduce yet another layer of approximations. Thus, for
k\in\mathbb{N}
we put

Z^{k}_{t}:=x+\int_{0}^{t}b^{k}(X_{t})\,dt+W_{t},\quad t\in[0,1].

By definition of a solution to SDE (
4.3
), (recall
Definition

4.3
) and by passing to a subsequence if necessary, we have

\|Z^{k}-X\|_{\mathcal{C}([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{d})}\to 0\quad a.s.,\quad\text{as $k\to\infty$}.

(4.52)

We should think of
k
as being much larger than
n
which is fixed; in the proof we will pass to the limit as
k\to\infty
. Recall that the process
\widetilde{X}^{n}
is defined in (
4.43
).

Step 1
. By definition of
Z^{k}
and
\widetilde{X}^{n}
, we have

d(Z^{k}_{t}-\widetilde{X}^{n}_{t})=-\lambda(X_{t}-\widetilde{X}^{n}_{t})dt+(b^{k}(X_{t})-b^{n}(\widetilde{X}^{n}_{t}))\,dt.

Hence, by the chain rule

\displaystyle d[e^{\lambda t}(Z^{k}_{t}-\widetilde{X}^{n}_{t})]

\displaystyle=e^{\lambda t}(b^{k}(X_{t})-b^{n}(\widetilde{X}^{n}_{t}))\,dt+e^{\lambda t}\lambda(\widetilde{X}_{t}^{n}-X_{t}+Z_{t}^{k}-\widetilde{X}_{t}^{n})\,dt

\displaystyle=e^{\lambda t}(b^{k}(X_{t})-b^{n}(\widetilde{X}^{n}_{t}))\,dt+e^{\lambda t}\lambda(Z_{t}^{k}-X_{t}).

Let
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
. Integrating the above identity in time from
s
to
t
and dividing both sides of the equation by
\exp(\lambda t)
, we derive

\displaystyle|(Z^{k}_{t}-\widetilde{X}^{n}_{t})-e^{-\lambda(t-s)}(Z^{k}_{s}-\widetilde{X}^{n}_{s})|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\Bigl|\int^{t}_{s}e^{-\lambda(t-r)}(b^{k}(X_{r})-b^{n}(\widetilde{X}^{n}_{r}))\,dr\Bigr|+\lambda\int^{t}_{s}|Z_{r}^{k}-X_{r}|\,dr

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\Bigl|\int^{t}_{s}e^{-\lambda(t-r)}(b^{n}(X_{r})-b^{n}(\widetilde{X}^{n}_{r}))\,dr\Bigr|+\Bigl|\int_{s}^{t}e^{-\lambda(t-r)}(b^{n}(X_{r})-b^{k}(X_{r}))\,dr\Bigr|

\displaystyle\qquad\phantom{\leq}+\lambda\|Z^{k}-X\|_{\mathcal{C}[0,1]}

\displaystyle\qquad=:I_{1,n}(s,t)+I_{2,n,k}(s,t)+\lambda\|Z^{k}-X\|_{\mathcal{C}([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Let us pass to the limit in this inequality as
k\to\infty
. Recalling (
4.52
), we get

|(X_{t}-\widetilde{X}^{n}_{t})-e^{-\lambda(t-s)}(X_{s}-\widetilde{X}^{n}_{s})|\leq I_{1,n}(s,t)+\liminf_{k\to\infty}I_{2,n,k}(s,t).

Therefore,

\displaystyle|(X_{t}-\widetilde{X}^{n}_{t})-(X_{s}-\widetilde{X}^{n}_{s})|

\displaystyle\leq|(X_{t}-\widetilde{X}^{n}_{t})-e^{-\lambda(t-s)}(X_{s}-\widetilde{X}^{n}_{s})|+(1-e^{-\lambda(t-s)})|X_{s}-\widetilde{X}^{n}_{s}|

\displaystyle\leq I_{1,n}(s,t)+\liminf_{k\to\infty}I_{2,n,k}(s,t)+\lambda^{\varepsilon}|t-s|^{\varepsilon}|X_{s}-\widetilde{X}^{n}_{s}|,

(4.53)

where we used the elementary inequality
1-e^{-a}\leq a^{\rho}
valid for any
a\geq 0
,
\rho\in[0,1]
.

Step 2a
. Let us now analyze each of the terms in the above inequality. We begin with
I_{1}
. This is the term for which we have tailored
Corollary

4.7
, and we apply it now with
\tau=1+\frac{\gamma}{2}
,
\mu=\frac{1}{2}
,
f=b^{n}
. We see that conditions (
4.15
) and
(
4.17
) hold thanks to our choice of parameters and the standing assumption
\gamma>-\frac{1}{2}
. Therefore we get by bound (
4.7
), using as usual that
\|b^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\leq\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}
:

\displaystyle\|I_{1,n}(s,t)\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(t-s)^{\varepsilon}\lambda^{-\frac{3}{4}-\frac{\gamma}{2}+3\varepsilon}\|X-\widetilde{X}^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([0,1])}^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(t-s)^{\varepsilon}\lambda^{-\frac{3}{2}-\gamma+3\varepsilon}\bigl([X-W]_{\mathcal{C}^{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}L_{m}([0,1])}+[\widetilde{X}^{n}-W]_{\mathcal{C}^{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}L_{m}([0,1])}\bigr)

(4.54)

for
C=C(\gamma,\varepsilon,d,m)
independent of
\lambda
.

To bound
[X-W]_{\mathcal{C}^{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}L_{m}([0,1])}

we apply an a priori bound from
Lemma

4.9
. We take there
X=Z
,
f_{k}=b^{k}
,
g\equiv 0
and we see that condition (
4.32
) is precisely our assumption
X\in\mathcal{V}(1+\frac{\gamma}{2})
. Therefore we get for any
m\geq 2

[X-W]_{\mathcal{C}^{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}L_{m}([0,1])}\leq C(1+\|b\|^{2}_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}).

(4.55)

for
C=C(\gamma,d,m)>0
.

To bound
[\widetilde{X}^{n}-W]_{\mathcal{C}^{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}L_{m}([0,1])}
we also apply
Lemma

4.9
. We take now
Z=\widetilde{X}^{n}
,
f_{1}=f_{2}=...=b^{n}
,
g=\lambda(X-\widetilde{X}^{n})
. It is easy see that
[\widetilde{X}^{n}-W]_{\mathcal{C}^{1}L_{m}([0,1])}<\infty
and thus condition (
4.32
) holds. Hence, it follows from (
4.33
) that

[\widetilde{X}^{n}-W]_{\mathcal{C}^{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}L_{m}([0,1])}\leq C(1+\|b\|^{2}_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}})(1+\lambda\|X-\widetilde{X}^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([0,1])})

(4.56)

for
C=C(\gamma,d,m)>0
.

Now we substitute (
4.55
) and (
4.56
) back into (
4.5
). Recall that we are allowed to bound
I_{1}
only by terms of the form
\lambda
to a negative power or
\|X-\widetilde{X}^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([0,1])}
to the power
1
. Since the first term there is not of that form, we bound it using the inequality
xy\leq x^{2}+y^{2}
for
x=\lambda^{-\frac{3}{4}-\frac{\gamma}{2}+4\varepsilon}
,
y=\lambda^{-\varepsilon}\|X-\widetilde{X}^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([0,1])}^{\frac{1}{2}}
. We derive

\displaystyle\|I_{1,n}(s,t)\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C(t-s)^{\varepsilon}\lambda^{-\frac{3}{2}-\gamma+8\varepsilon}+C(t-s)^{\varepsilon}\lambda^{-2\varepsilon}\|X-\widetilde{X}^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([0,1])}

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq}+C(t-s)^{\varepsilon}\lambda^{-\frac{1}{2}-\gamma+3\varepsilon}\|X-\widetilde{X}^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([0,1])}

\displaystyle\leq C(t-s)^{\varepsilon}\lambda^{-\frac{3}{2}-\gamma+8\varepsilon}+C(t-s)^{\varepsilon}\lambda^{-2\varepsilon}\|X-\widetilde{X}^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([0,1])}

(4.57)

for
C=C(\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}},\gamma,\varepsilon,d,m)
and in the last step, we used the inequality (
4.50
) for our choice of
\varepsilon
. We see that all the terms are now in the desired form.

Step 2b
. Next, we move to the term
I_{2,n,k}
from (
4.5
). We apply
Corollary

4.7
with
\gamma-\varepsilon
in place of
\gamma
,
\tau=1+\frac{\gamma}{2}
,
f=b^{n}-b^{k}
. We see that condition (
4.15
) holds and we get
from (
4.24
) and (
4.55
)

\displaystyle\|I_{2,n,k}(s,t)\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C\|b^{n}-b^{k}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma-\varepsilon}}(t-s)^{\varepsilon}\lambda^{-1-\frac{\gamma}{2}+4\varepsilon}\Bigl(1+[X-W]_{\mathcal{C}^{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}L_{m}([0,1])}\lambda^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{2}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq C\|b^{n}-b^{k}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma-\varepsilon}}(t-s)^{\varepsilon}\lambda^{-1-\frac{\gamma}{2}+4\varepsilon}

(4.58)

for
C=C(\gamma,d,m)
independent of
\lambda
,
n
,
k
.

Step 2c
. Now we substitute (
4.5
) and (
4.5
) into (
4.5
). We note that
\|b^{n}-b^{k}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma-\varepsilon}}\to\|b^{n}-b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma-\varepsilon}}
as
k\to\infty
. Therefore, by Fatou’s lemma we derive
for any
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}

\displaystyle\|(X_{t}-\widetilde{X}^{n}_{t})-(X_{s}-\widetilde{X}^{n}_{s})\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}(t-s)^{\varepsilon}\lambda^{-\frac{3}{2}-\gamma+8\varepsilon}+C_{0}(t-s)^{\varepsilon}\lambda^{-2\varepsilon}\|X-\widetilde{X}^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([0,1])}

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq}+C_{0}(t-s)^{\varepsilon}\|b^{n}-b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma-\varepsilon}}\lambda^{-1-\frac{\gamma}{2}+4\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq}+\lambda^{\varepsilon}|t-s|^{\varepsilon}\|X_{s}-\widetilde{X}^{n}_{s}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)},

(4.59)

where
C_{0}=C_{0}(\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}},\gamma,\varepsilon,d,m)
.

Step 3: buckling for supremum norm
.
Choose now any
\lambda\geq 1
such that

C_{0}\lambda^{-2\varepsilon}\leq\frac{1}{2},

(4.60)

where
C_{0}
is as in (
4.5
).
By choosing now
s=0
in (
4.5
) and taking supremum over all
t\in[0,1]
we deduce with the help of (
4.60
)

\|X-\widetilde{X}^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([0,1])}\leq\frac{1}{2}\|X-\widetilde{X}^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([0,1])}+\lambda^{-\frac{3}{2}-\gamma+10\varepsilon}+\|b^{n}-b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma-\varepsilon}}\lambda^{-1-\frac{\gamma}{2}+6\varepsilon}.

(4.61)

Note that
\|X\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([0,1])}<\infty
because
X
belongs to the class
\mathcal{V}(1+\frac{\gamma}{2})
. Furthermore, it is also easy to see that

\|\widetilde{X}^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([0,1])}<\infty
. Hence
\|X-\widetilde{X}^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([0,1])}<\infty
. Thus we can put the term
\frac{1}{2}\|X-\widetilde{X}^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([0,1])}
in the left-hand side of (
4.61
) and derive

\|X-\widetilde{X}^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([0,1])}\leq 2\lambda^{-\frac{3}{2}-\gamma+10\varepsilon}+2\|b^{n}-b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma-\varepsilon}}\lambda^{-1-\frac{\gamma}{2}+6\varepsilon}.

(4.62)

Step 4: bounding the Hölder norm
.
Now we substitute (
4.62
) into (
4.5
). We note that
\|X_{s}-\widetilde{X}^{n}_{s}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq\|X-\widetilde{X}^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([0,1])}
. We derive
for any
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}

\|(X_{t}-\widetilde{X}^{n}_{t})-(X_{s}-\widetilde{X}^{n}_{s})\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq C(t-s)^{\varepsilon}\lambda^{-\frac{3}{2}-\gamma+11\varepsilon}+C(t-s)^{\varepsilon}\|b^{n}-b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma-\varepsilon}}\lambda^{-1-\frac{\gamma}{2}+7\varepsilon}

for some
C>0
.
Dividing both sides of the above inequality by
(t-s)^{\varepsilon}
and taking the supremum over all
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
, we finally obtain

[X-\widetilde{X}^{n}]_{\mathcal{C}^{\varepsilon}L_{m}([0,1])}\leq C\lambda^{-\frac{3}{2}-\gamma+11\varepsilon}+C\|b^{n}-b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma-\varepsilon}}\lambda^{-1-\frac{\gamma}{2}+7\varepsilon}

Since
m\varepsilon>1
and the processes
X
and
\widetilde{X}^{n}
are continuous, we can apply the Kolmogorov continuity theorem (
Theorem

3.12
) to derive

\|\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|X_{t}-\widetilde{X}^{n}_{t}|\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq C\lambda^{-\frac{3}{2}-\gamma+11\varepsilon}+C\|b^{n}-b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma-\varepsilon}}\lambda^{-1-\frac{\gamma}{2}+7\varepsilon}.

for
C=C(\varepsilon)
, which is the desired bound (
4.51
).
∎

Now, to show weak uniqueness, it just remains to collect the bounds from
Corollary

4.15
and
Lemma

4.16
and plug them into (
4.5
). Let us now provide the precise statement, formalizing
Theorem

1.2
.

Theorem 4.17
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
b\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\gamma>-\frac{1}{2}
. Then SDE (
4.3
) has a unique weak solution in the class
\mathcal{V}(1+\frac{\gamma}{2})
.

Proof.

Fix
b\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and let
(b^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
be a sequence of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
functions converging to
b
in
\mathcal{C}^{\gamma-}
.
Let
(X^{n},W)
be the strong solution to the regularized equation (
4.8
). Let
(X,W)
be a weak solution to (
4.3
) in the class
\mathcal{V}(1+\frac{\gamma}{2})
.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
such that condition (
4.50
) holds. Let
\lambda_{0}
be as in
Lemma

4.16
. Then for any
\lambda>\lambda_{0}
we have by (
4.5
),
Corollary

4.15
and
Lemma

4.16

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}_{\|\cdot\|\wedge 1}(\law(X^{n}),\law(X))

\displaystyle\leq d_{TV}(\law(X^{n}),\law(\widetilde{X}^{n}))+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt\|\widetilde{X}^{n}-X\|_{\mathcal{C}([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq C\lambda\|\|\widetilde{X}^{n}-X\|_{\mathcal{C}([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C\lambda^{-\frac{1}{2}-\gamma+11\varepsilon}+C\|b^{n}-b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma-\varepsilon}}\lambda^{-\frac{\gamma}{2}+7\varepsilon}

(4.63)

for
C=C(\varepsilon)
. Note that since
\gamma<0
, the factor
\lambda^{-\frac{\gamma}{2}+7\varepsilon}
is actually a positive power of
\lambda
. Therefore, we cannot simply take
\lambda\to\infty
in the above inequality in order to show that the left-hand side is zero. Nevertheless we can balance the two terms by taking

\lambda:=\|b^{n}-b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma-\varepsilon}}^{-\frac{2}{1+\gamma}}+\lambda_{0}.

Substituting this
\lambda
into (
4.5
) we get

\mathbb{W}_{\|\cdot\|\wedge 1}(\law(X^{n}),\law(X))\leq C\|b^{n}-b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma-\varepsilon}}+C\|b^{n}-b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma-\varepsilon}}^{\frac{1+2\gamma-22\varepsilon}{1+\gamma}}\leq C\|b^{n}-b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma-\varepsilon}}^{\frac{1+2\gamma-22\varepsilon}{1+\gamma}}

for
C=C(\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}},\gamma,\varepsilon,d,m)
. Now we can choose
\varepsilon>0
even smaller so that

\frac{1+2\gamma-22\varepsilon}{1+\gamma}>0

(this is possible thanks to the main assumption
\gamma>-\frac{1}{2}
and finally pass to the limit as
n\to\infty
. We get

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{W}_{\|\cdot\|\wedge 1}(\law(X^{n}),\law(X))=0.

Now if
(\overline{X},\overline{W})
is another weak solution to (
4.3
) and
\overline{X}\in\mathcal{V}(1+\frac{\gamma}{2})
, then by above

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{W}_{\|\cdot\|\wedge 1}(\law(X^{n}),\law(\overline{X}))=0,

which implies that
\law(X)=\law(\overline{X})
and thus weak uniqueness holds.
∎

4.6
Exercises

Exercise 4.18
.

In this exercise we prove that, as claimed in
Section

4.1
, if
\beta<0
and
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\beta}
, then the sequence
P_{s}f
converges to
f
in
\mathcal{C}^{\beta-}
as
s\to 0
.

1.

Show that for any
s>0
we have
\|P_{s}f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\beta}}\leq\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\beta}}
.

Hint
: Use
Definition

4.1
and a trivial bound
\|P_{s}g\|_{L_{\infty}}\leq\|g\|_{L_{\infty}}
for a bounded measurable
g
.

2.

Let
\gamma<0
. Show that for some constant
C=C(\gamma,d)
we have for any
t>0

\|P_{t}f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq Ct^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}.

Hint
: Write
P_{t}f=P_{\frac{t}{2}}P_{\frac{t}{2}}f
and apply consequently (
3.2
) and
Definition

4.1
.

3.

Show that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
t>0
we have

|P_{t+s}f(x)-P_{t}f(x)|\leq\int_{t}^{t+s}|\Delta P_{r}f(x)|\,dr.

4.

Let
\varepsilon>0
,
t>0
. Deduce from parts
2
and
3
that for
C=C(\beta,\varepsilon,d)

\|P_{t+s}f-P_{t}f\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\beta-\varepsilon}}t^{\frac{\beta}{2}-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}s^{\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}.

5.

Conclude that
P_{s}f
converges to
f
in
\mathcal{C}^{\beta-}
as
s\to 0
.

5
Analysis of numerical methods and further applications of stochastic sewing

In this section, we see how stochastic sewing can be very efficiently applied in stochastic numerics.
As always, we present the main idea in the basic setting of Brownian noise (
Section

5.1
) and leave the extension to the fractional Brownian case to the reader. However, when the driving noise is an
\alpha
-stable process, certain fundamental difficulties arise that require new tools: the John–Nirenberg inequality (
Section

5.2
), new norms (
Section

5.4
), and the shifted stochastic sewing lemma (
Section

5.5
). When the drift is a function in a Sobolev space rather than a Hölder continuous function, we need an additional tool: the taming singularities lemma (
Section

5.8
). We will cover all of these in this section.

5.1
Numerics for SDEs driven by Brownian noise

We begin with SDEs driven by standard Brownian motion, with the goal of establishing
Theorem

1.4
. In this subsection, we mainly follow
[
BDG21
]
.

Recall the setup of
Section

1.2
and the definition of
\kappa_{n}(r)
in (
1.11
). Fix
\gamma\in(0,1]
,
b\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}
, Let
X
be a solution to (
1.1
), and for
n\in\mathbb{N}
let
X^{n}
be the Euler scheme for this equation as defined in (
1.12
). Similar to
Section

3.3
, we denote

\varphi:=X-W,\quad\varphi^{n}=X^{n}-W.

We would like to bound the approximation error

\|X-X^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{2}([0,1])}=\|\varphi-\varphi^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{2}([0,1])}

by half of itself plus terms of the form
Cn^{-rate}
. However, this is not possible to achieve directly, because when we apply
Theorem

3.4
, a term with a higher seminorm, namely
[\varphi-\varphi^{n}]_{\mathcal{C}^{1/2}L_{2}([0,1])}
, appears. Therefore, instead we aim to bound

[\varphi-\varphi^{n}]_{\mathcal{C}^{1/2}L_{2}([S,T])}

by half of itself plus
Cn^{-rate}
. This is possible whenever the interval
[S,T]
is small enough, and then we apply a special lemma that transforms this local bound into a bound on the entire interval.

Thus, for
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
we need to bound

\displaystyle(\varphi_{t}-\varphi^{n}_{t})-(\varphi_{s}-\varphi^{n}_{s})

\displaystyle=\int_{s}^{t}(b(X_{r})-b(X^{n}_{r}))\,dr+\int_{s}^{t}(b(X^{n}_{r})-b(X^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r)}))\,dr

\displaystyle=\int_{s}^{t}(b(W_{r}+\varphi_{r})-b(W_{r}+\varphi^{n}_{r}))\,dr

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\int_{s}^{t}(b(W_{r}+\varphi^{n}_{r})-b(W_{\kappa_{n}(r)}+\varphi^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r)}))\,dr.

(5.1)

We already know how to deal with the first integral, see
Lemma

3.5
. Therefore, we focus on the second one. As usual, to warm up, we begin with a simplified result, where
\varphi^{n}\equiv 0
.

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
\gamma\in(0,1]
,
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
,
m\geq 2
,
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
. Then there exists a constant
C=C(\gamma,\varepsilon,d,m)>0
such that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
(S,T)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
one has

\Bigl\|\int_{S}^{T}(f(W_{r})-f(W_{\kappa_{n}(r)}))\,dr\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(T-S)^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon}n^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{2}+\varepsilon}.

(5.2)

Remark 5.2
.

Theorem

5.1
is also of independent interest. It provides the rate of convergence for an approximation of an additive functional of Brownian motion based on high-frequency observations. It extends the corresponding results of
[
Alt21
]
.

To prove
Theorem

5.1
, we need the following technical lemma on heat kernel bounds.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
,
\gamma\in[0,1]
,
0<s\leq t
. Then

\|P_{t}f-P_{s}f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}s^{\frac{\gamma}{2}-1}(t-s)

for
C=C(\gamma,d)>0
.

Proof.

Fix
0<s\leq t
. For any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, using
Lemma

3.1
we have

\displaystyle|P_{t}f(x)-P_{s}f(x)|

\displaystyle=\Bigl|\int_{s}^{t}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}P_{r}f(x)\,dr\Bigr|=\Bigl|\int_{s}^{t}\Delta P_{r}f(x)\,dr\Bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{s}^{t}\|\nabla P_{r}f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}\,dr\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\int_{s}^{t}r^{\frac{\gamma}{2}-1}\,dr\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}s^{\frac{\gamma}{2}-1}(t-s).\qed

Proof of
Theorem

5.1
.

As before, we rely on the stochastic sewing lemma. Fix
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
. Consider the germ

A_{s,t}:=\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\int_{s}^{t}(f(W_{r})-f(W_{\kappa_{n}(r)}))\,dr,\quad(s,t)\in\Delta_{[S,T]}.

(5.3)

Then, for any
(s,u,t)\in\Delta^{3}_{[S,T]}

\delta A_{s,u,t}=\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\int_{u}^{t}(f(W_{r})-f(W_{\kappa_{n}(r)}))\,dr-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{u}\int_{u}^{t}(f(W_{r})-f(W_{\kappa_{n}(r)}))\,dr.

and as in the proof of
Theorem

3.2
, we have

\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\delta A_{s,u,t}=0.

Thus, condition (
2.11
) trivially holds.

Now let us verify (
2.10
). Note the following potential danger in the germ
A_{s,t}
in (
5.3
): if
r
is very close to
s
, it may happen that
\kappa_{n}(r)<s
. In this case,
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}f(W_{\kappa_{n}(r)})
is just
f(W_{\kappa_{n}(r)})
, and we do not gain any additional smoothing from the heat kernel (in contrast, the first term in the integral
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}f(W_{r})=P_{r-s}f(W_{s})
always contains smoothing).

Therefore, we distinguish between the cases where
t-s
is small or large relative to
\frac{1}{n}
. In the latter case, we split the integral in (
5.3
) into two parts: when
r
is far from
s
(i.e.,
r\geq\kappa_{n}(s)+\frac{2}{n}
), we obtain sufficient smoothing and proceed as before; when
r
is close to
s
, the entire integral will be very small.

Thus, take any
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[S,T]}
. First, suppose that
t-s\leq\frac{2}{n}
. Then, we immediately get

\displaystyle\|A_{s,t}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{s}^{t}\|f(W_{r})-f(W_{\kappa_{n}(r)})\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\,dr\leq\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\int_{s}^{t}\bigl(\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt|W_{r}-W_{\kappa_{n}(r)}|^{m\gamma}\bigr)^{\frac{1}{m}}\,dr

\displaystyle\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(t-s)n^{-\frac{\gamma}{2}}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}n^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{2}+\varepsilon}(t-s)^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon},

(5.4)

for
C=C(\gamma,d,m)>0
.
Here, in the penultimate inequality, we used that
r-\kappa_{n}(r)\leq\frac{1}{n}
. In the final inequality, we used that
(t-s)\leq\frac{2}{n}
, which allowed us to transfer some powers from
(t-s)
to
\frac{1}{n}
. Note that we kept
(t-s)^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon}
, which is the bare minimum required for the stochastic sewing lemma to be applicable.

If
t-s\geq\frac{2}{n}
, let
s^{\prime}:=\kappa_{n}(s)+\frac{2}{n}
be the second gridpoint to the right of
s
. We write

\displaystyle\|A_{s,t}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\Bigl\|\int_{s}^{s^{\prime}}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\big(f(W_{r})-f(W_{\kappa_{n}(r)})\big)dr\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\!\!\!+\Bigl\|\int_{s^{\prime}}^{t}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\big(f(W_{r})-f(W_{\kappa_{n}(r)})\big)dr\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=:I_{1}+I_{2}

(5.5)

We treat
I_{1}
exactly as in (
5.1
) and get

I_{1}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\int_{s}^{s^{\prime}}\bigl(\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt|W_{r}-W_{\kappa_{n}(r)}|^{m\gamma}\bigr)^{\frac{1}{m}}\,dr\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}n^{-1-\frac{\gamma}{2}}.

(5.6)

for
C=C(\gamma,d,m)>0
. To bound
I_{2}
, we use
Lemma

5.3
and derive

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle=\Bigl\|\int_{s^{\prime}}^{t}|P_{r-s}f(W_{s})-P_{\kappa_{n}(r)-s}f(W_{s})|\,dr\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq\int_{s^{\prime}}^{t}\|P_{r-s}f-P_{\kappa_{n}(r)-s}f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}\,dr

(5.7)

\displaystyle\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\int_{s^{\prime}}^{t}(\kappa_{n}(r)-s)^{\frac{\gamma}{2}-1}n^{-1}\,dr

\displaystyle\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\int_{s^{\prime}}^{t}(r-s^{\prime})^{\frac{\gamma}{2}-1}n^{-1}\,dr\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(t-s)^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}n^{-1},

for
C=C(\gamma,d)>0
. Here in the penultimate inequality we used that
r-\kappa_{n}(r)\leq\frac{1}{n}\leq s^{\prime}-s
and hence
\kappa_{n}(r)-s\geq r-s^{\prime}
. Substituting this and (
5.6
) into (
5.1
) and using that
t-s\geq\frac{1}{n}
, we get

\|A_{s,t}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}n^{-1-\frac{\gamma}{2}}+C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(t-s)^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}n^{-1}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(t-s)^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon}n^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{2}+\varepsilon}.

(5.8)

Note how carefully we traded powers of
n^{-1}
for powers of
t-s
to ensure that condition (
2.10
) is satisfied.

Condition (ii) of
Theorem

2.3
is satisfied by the definition of
A_{s,t}
in (
5.3
), and condition (
2.9
) follows from the BDG inequality by exactly the same argument as in the proof of
Theorem

3.2
. Thus, all conditions of the SSL are satisfied, and the bound (
2.12
) together with (
5.1
) and (
5.8
) yields precisely (
5.2
).
∎

Next, an easy application of the Girsanov theorem allows to insert the drift into inequality (
5.2
). Recall the definition of the process
X^{n}
in (
1.12
).

Corollary 5.4
.

Let
\gamma\in(0,1]
,
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
,
m\geq 2
,
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
. Let
b\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then there exists a constant
C=C(\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}},\gamma,\varepsilon,d,m)
such that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
(S,T)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
one has

\Bigl\|\int_{S}^{T}(f(X^{n}_{r})-f(X^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r)}))\,dr\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(T-S)^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon}n^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{2}+\varepsilon}.

(5.9)

Proof.

In the proof we write for brevity
h_{t}:=b(X^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(t)})
. Consider a probability measure
\mathsf{P}^{n}
on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F})
defined by

\frac{d\mathsf{P}^{n}}{d\mathsf{P}}=\exp\bigl(-\int_{0}^{1}h_{r}dW_{r}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}|h_{r}|^{2}dr\bigr).

Since the function
b
is bounded, we see that
\mathsf{P}^{n}
is a probability measure and Girsanov’s theorem implies that the process

W^{n}:=X^{n}_{t}-x=\int_{0}^{t}b(X^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r)})dr+W_{t},\quad t\in[0,1]

is a Brownian motion under the measure
\mathsf{P}^{n}
. Hence for any
(S,T)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
, we get

\displaystyle\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\mathsf{P}}\Bigl|\int_{S}^{T}(f(X^{n}_{r})-f(X^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r)}))\,dr\Bigr|^{m}

\displaystyle\quad=\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\mathsf{P}^{n}}\Bigl|\int_{S}^{T}(f(X^{n}_{r})-f(X^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r)}))\,dr\Bigr|^{m}\frac{d\mathsf{P}}{d\mathsf{P}^{n}}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\Bigl(\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\mathsf{P}^{n}}\Bigl|\int_{S}^{T}(f(X^{n}_{r})-f(X^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r)}))\,dr\Bigr|^{2m}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Bigl(\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\mathsf{P}^{n}}\Bigl(\frac{d\mathsf{P}}{d\mathsf{P}^{n}}\Bigr)^{2}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\quad=\Bigl(\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\mathsf{P}^{n}}\Bigl|\int_{S}^{T}(f(W^{n}_{r}+x)-f(W^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r)}+x)\,dr\Bigr|^{2m}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Bigl(\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\mathsf{P}}\frac{d\mathsf{P}}{d\mathsf{P}^{n}}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\quad=\Bigl(\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\mathsf{P}}\Bigl|\int_{S}^{T}(f(W_{r}+x)-f(W_{\kappa_{n}(r)}+x)\,dr\Bigr|^{2m}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Bigl(\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\mathsf{P}}\frac{d\mathsf{P}}{d\mathsf{P}^{n}}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}

(5.10)

Using the standard martingale calculations, we get

\displaystyle\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\mathsf{P}}\frac{d\mathsf{P}}{d\mathsf{P}^{n}}

\displaystyle=\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\mathsf{P}}e^{\int_{0}^{1}h_{r}dW_{r}-\int_{0}^{1}|h_{r}|^{2}\,dr+\frac{3}{2}\int_{0}^{1}|h_{r}|^{2}dr}

\displaystyle\leq\Bigl(\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\mathsf{P}}e^{\int_{0}^{1}2h_{r}dW_{r}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}|2h_{r}|^{2}\,dr}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Bigl(\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\mathsf{P}}e^{3\int_{0}^{1}|h_{r}|^{2}\,dr}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq e^{\frac{3}{2}\|h\|^{2}_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}},

where in the last inequality we used that the process
t\mapsto e^{\int_{0}^{t}2h_{r}dW_{r}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}|2h_{r}|^{2}\,dr}
is a martingale, because
h
is bounded. Substituting this into (
5.10
) and applying
Lemma

5.3
to treat the first factor in the right-hand side of (
5.10
), we get (
5.9
).
∎

Now we have all the ingredients to prove the rate of convergence of the Euler scheme (
1.10
) to the solution of SDE (
1.3
). The first part of the proof is similar to the proof of strong uniqueness in
Theorem

1.1
in
Section

3.3
: for small
T-S
, we bound
[\varphi-\varphi^{n}]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{m}([S,T])}
by half of the same quantity. However, this time the bound also involves an additional term, which will eventually determine the convergence rate. Therefore, we pass from small
T-S
to arbitrary
T-S
using
Lemma

4.8
. Finally, we use the Kolmogorov continuity theorem to bound the supremum of the error between the Euler scheme and the solution.

Proof of
Theorem

1.4
.

Step 1: Estimate on short time intervals
. We will use decomposition (
5.1
). Fix
\varepsilon>0
. Let
(S,T)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
. Using
Corollary

5.4
and
Theorem

3.4
with
\tau=\frac{1}{2}
,
\Gamma_{0}=\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}
, we get for any
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[S,T]}

\displaystyle\|(X_{t}-X^{n}_{t})-(X_{s}-X^{n}_{s})\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=\|(\varphi_{t}-\varphi^{n}_{t})-(\varphi_{s}-\varphi^{n}_{s})\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\Bigl\|\int_{s}^{t}(b(W_{r}+\varphi_{r})-b(W_{r}+\varphi^{n}_{r}))\,dr\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\Bigl\|\int_{s}^{t}(b(W_{r}+\varphi^{n}_{r})-b(W_{\kappa_{n}(r)}+\varphi^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r)}))\,dr\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C(t-s)^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}\|\varphi-\varphi^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([S,T])}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+C(t-s)^{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}[\varphi-\varphi^{n}]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{m}([S,T])}+C(t-s)^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon}n^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{2}+\varepsilon}.

for
C=C(\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}},\gamma,\varepsilon,d,m)>0
.
Dividing both sides by
(t-s)^{1/2}
and taking supremum over all
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[S,T]}
we get

\displaystyle[\varphi-\varphi^{n}]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{m}([S,T])}

\displaystyle\,\,\leq C(T-S)^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\|\varphi-\varphi^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([S,T])}+C(T-S)^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}[\varphi-\varphi^{n}]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{m}([S,T])}+Cn^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{2}+\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\,\,\leq C_{0}(T-S)^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\|\varphi_{S}-\varphi^{n}_{S}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}+C_{0}(T-S)^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}[\varphi-\varphi^{n}]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{m}([S,T])}+C_{0}n^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{2}+\varepsilon}

(5.11)

for
C_{0}=C_{0}(\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}},\gamma,\varepsilon,d,m)>0
, where in the last inequality we used (
3.9
).
Fix now
\ell>0
such that

C_{0}\ell^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}\leq\frac{1}{2}.

(we stress that
\ell
does not depend on
n
). Then we get from (
5.11
) for any
(S,T)\in\Delta_{[S,T]}
with
T-S\leq\ell

[\varphi-\varphi^{n}]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{m}([S,T])}\leq 2C_{0}\|\varphi_{S}-\varphi^{n}_{S}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}+2C_{0}n^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{2}+\varepsilon}.

(5.12)

Step 2: Passage to arbitrary time intervals
.
We apply
Lemma

4.8
(ii) to
f:=\varphi-\varphi^{n}
.
Bound (
5.12
) implies that there exists
C=C(\ell,C_{0})
independent of
n
such that

[\varphi-\varphi^{n}]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{m}([0,1])}\leq CC_{0}n^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{2}+\varepsilon}\leq Cn^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{2}+\varepsilon},

(5.13)

for
C=C(\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}},\gamma,\varepsilon,d,m)>0
.

Step 3: Supremum bound via Kolmogorov’s theorem
.
By the Kolmogorov continuity theorem (
Theorem

3.12
), inequality (
5.13
) implies
for any
m\geq 1

\|[X-X^{n}]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{4}}([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}=\|[\varphi-\varphi^{n}]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{4}}([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq Cn^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{2}+\varepsilon},

for
C=C(\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}},\gamma,\varepsilon,d,m)>0
. Recalling that
X_{0}-X^{n}_{0}=0
, we get the desired bound (
1.13
).
∎

We note that the right-hand side of (
1.13
) does not deteriorate for large
m
. This allows to get a bound on a.s. convergence rate by the Borel–Cantelli arguments.

Corollary 5.5
.

Under the conditions of
Theorem

1.4
, there exists a random variable
\eta
such that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}

\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|X_{t}-X^{n}_{t}|\leq\eta n^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{2}+\varepsilon}.

Proof.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
. Denote
Z_{n}:=\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|X_{t}-X^{n}_{t}|
,
\rho:=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma}{2}
. Then by
Theorem

1.4
for any
m\geq 2
we have

\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathsf{P}(|Z_{n}|>n^{-\rho+\varepsilon})\leq\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt|Z_{n}|^{m}}{n^{m(-\rho+\varepsilon)}}\leq\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}Cn^{-\frac{m\varepsilon}{2}}

for
C=C(\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}},\gamma,\varepsilon,d,m)>0
.
Choosing
m=4/\varepsilon
, the above sum is finite, so by the Borel-Cantelli lemma we have
|Z_{n}|\leq n^{-\rho+\varepsilon}
for all
n>n_{0}(\omega)
for some
n_{0}(\omega)
. This yields the claim by setting

\eta:=1\vee\max_{n\leq n_{0}}(|Z_{n}|n^{\rho-\varepsilon}).\qed

5.2
John–Nirenberg inequality

Let us move on to the next problem and study well-posedness for SDEs driven by a symmetric
\alpha
-stable process and the strong rate of convergence of the Euler scheme,
{\alpha\in(0,2)}
. It turns out that certain parts of the proof in the Gaussian case do not work in this setting at all and must be replaced by new arguments. This highlights a common situation: while the original stochastic sewing lemma of Lê, in the formulation of
[
Lê20
]
, might not be applicable to a given problem, one can often modify it or complement it with different ideas to make things work. In the upcoming subsections, we will see in detail how the proof strategy of
Theorems

1.1
and
1.4
must be reworked in the setting of Lévy processes.

Thus, we fix
\alpha\in(1,2)
and recall the definition of a symmetric
\alpha
-stable process
L
in (
1.7
). We fix a filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
and suppose that
L
is adapted to
\mathcal{F}
and
L_{t}-L_{s}
is independent of
\mathcal{F}_{s}
whenever
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
.
It follows from the definition that, in contrast to Brownian motion,
L
has finite moments only of order less than
\alpha

\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt|L_{t}|^{m}\leq Ct^{\frac{m}{\alpha}},\quad t\in[0,1]

for
C=C(\alpha,d,m)>0
whenever
m<\alpha
. Actually, some of the results remain valid in greater generality for
\alpha\leq 1
. However again, to simplify the presentation and highlight the main ideas, we focus here only on the case
\alpha>1
.

If we try to prove an analogue of
Theorem

5.1
for an
\alpha
-stable process (in order to establish
Theorem

1.5
and obtain strong convergence of the Euler scheme), we immediately encounter the following difficulty. The proof of
Theorem

5.1
relies heavily on the fact that for
r\in[0,1]
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\geq 2

\|f(W_{r})-f(W_{\kappa_{n}(r)})\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\||W_{r}-W_{\kappa_{n}(r)}|^{\gamma}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}n^{-\frac{\gamma}{2}},

see (
5.1
) and (
5.6
).
However, for an
\alpha
-stable Lévy process, this is no longer true, as
L
does not have moments of order
\alpha
and higher. Thus, the best we can obtain is just
