\|f(L_{r})-f(L_{\kappa_{n}(r)})\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\||L_{r}-L_{\kappa_{n}(r)}|^{\gamma}\wedge 1\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}n^{-(\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}\wedge\frac{1}{m})+\varepsilon},

(5.14)

so the bound deteriorates for large
m
. Consequently, we would get an
L_{m}(\Omega)
convergence rate of order
n^{-(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}\wedge\frac{1}{m})}
and a.s. rate of order
n^{-\frac{1}{2}}
. The correct rate, however, is of order
n^{-(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma}{\alpha})\wedge 1}
.

Note that for
m=2
, the bound (
5.14
) is good enough, as it yields the correct convergence rate
n^{-(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}\wedge\frac{1}{2})}
. Therefore, we need a tool that can transfer an
L_{2}
-moment bound to an arbitrary
L_{m}
-moment bound without any loss. This is indeed possible and is achieved via the John–Nirenberg inequality.

Theorem 5.6

(John–Nirenberg inequality,
[
SV06
, Exercise A.3.2]
,
[
Lê22b
, Theorem 2.3]
,

[
BDG22
, Appendix B]
)
.

Let

0\leq S\leq T
and let
\mathcal{A}\colon\Omega\times[S,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a continuous process adapted to the filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
. Assume that there exist a constant
\Gamma>0
such that
for any
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[S,T]}
one has

\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}|\mathcal{A}_{t}-\mathcal{A}_{s}|\leq\Gamma,\quad\text{a.s.}

(5.15)

Then for any
m\in[1,\infty)
, there exists a constant
C>0
independent of
d,m,S,T
such that for any

s\in[S,T]
one has

\|\sup_{r\in[s,T]}|\mathcal{A}_{r}-\mathcal{A}_{s}|\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq Cm\Gamma.

(5.16)

Remark 5.7
.

We emphasize that the continuity of
\mathcal{A}
is crucial in
Theorem

5.6
. Indeed, if
\mathcal{A}
is a standard Poisson process, then clearly
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}|\mathcal{A}_{t}-\mathcal{A}_{s}|\leq t-s
, but of course it is not true that
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt|\mathcal{A}_{t}-\mathcal{A}_{s}|^{m}\leq C(t-s)^{m}
for
m>1
. Similarly, if
\mathcal{A}
is an
\alpha
-stable process,
\alpha\in(1,2)
, then
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}|\mathcal{A}_{t}-\mathcal{A}_{s}|\leq C(t-s)^{1/\alpha}
, but
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt|\mathcal{A}_{t}-\mathcal{A}_{s}|^{m}=\infty
for
m\geq\alpha
.

We prove
Theorem

5.6
by arguing similarly to
[
Lê22a
]
. First, we establish
the so-called “good
\lambda
inequality” of Burkholder
[
Bur73
]
.

Proposition 5.8

(Good
\lambda
inequality)
.

Let
X
,
Y
be nonnegative random variables. Assume that for any
\lambda>0
,
\theta\in(0,1)
,
h>0

one has

\mathsf{P}(X\geq(1+h)\lambda)\leq\theta\mathsf{P}(X\geq\lambda)+\mathsf{P}(Y\geq\theta h\lambda).

(5.17)

Then for any
m\geq 1
there exists a universal constant
C>0
independent of
m
such that

\|X\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq Cm\|Y\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}.

(5.18)

Proof.

Using the identity
x^{m}=m\int_{0}^{x}\lambda^{m-1}\,d\lambda
valid for any
x\geq 0
,
m\geq 1
, we derive for any
\theta\in(0,1)
,
M>0
,
h>0

\displaystyle\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt(X\wedge M)^{m}

\displaystyle=m\int_{0}^{M}\mathsf{P}(X\geq\lambda)\lambda^{m-1}\,d\lambda=(1+h)^{m}m\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{1+h}}\mathsf{P}\bigl(X\geq(1+h)\lambda\bigr)\lambda^{m-1}\,d\lambda

\displaystyle\leq(1+h)^{m}m\theta\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{1+h}}\mathsf{P}(X\geq\lambda)\lambda^{m-1}\,d\lambda+(1+h)^{m}m\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{1+h}}\mathsf{P}(Y\geq\theta h\lambda)\lambda^{m-1}\,d\lambda

\displaystyle\leq(1+h)^{m}\theta\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt(X\wedge M)^{m}+(1+h)^{m}\theta^{-m}h^{-m}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057ptY^{m},

where in the penultimate inequality we use our main assumption (
5.17
). Take now
\theta:=\frac{1}{2}(1+h)^{-m}
. Then, by above

\frac{1}{2}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt(X\wedge M)^{m}\leq(1+h)^{m^{2}+m}2^{m}h^{-m}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057ptY^{m}.

Passing to the limit as
M\to\infty
and using the monotone convergence theorem, we obtain

\frac{1}{2}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057ptX^{m}\leq(1+h)^{m^{2}+m}2^{m}h^{-m}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057ptY^{m}.

By taking now
h=\frac{1}{m}
and using that
(1+\frac{1}{m})^{m}\leq e
, we get (
5.18
).
∎

Now we are ready to prove the John-Nirenberg inequality.

Proof of
Theorem

5.6
.

Fix
0\leq S\leq T
. Put

V^{*}(\omega):=\sup_{r\in[S,T]}|\mathcal{A}_{r}(\omega)-A_{S}(\omega)|,\qquad\omega\in\Omega.

We would like to apply
Proposition

5.8
with
X=V^{*}
,
Y=2\Gamma
.

Step 1
. We claim that condition (
5.15
) can be extended and for any stopping times
\tau\leq\eta
taking values in
[S,T]
we have a.s.

\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\tau}|\mathcal{A}_{\eta}-\mathcal{A}_{\tau}|\leq 2\Gamma.

(5.19)

First, we prove (
5.19
) for the case when
\eta=T
and
\tau
takes finitely many values
S\leq t_{1}<\ldots<t_{n}=T
.
Recall the identity
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\tau}(X\mathbbm{1}_{\tau=t})=\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{t}(X\mathbbm{1}_{\tau=t})
valid for any integrable random variable
X
, see, e.g.,
[
KS91
, Problem 1.2.17(i)]
.
Then, using (
5.15
), we deduce

\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\tau}|\mathcal{A}_{T}-\mathcal{A}_{\tau}|=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{t_{i}}\bigl[|\mathcal{A}_{T}-\mathcal{A}_{t_{i}}|\mathbbm{1}(\tau=t_{i})\bigr]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbbm{1}(\tau=t_{i})\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{t_{i}}|\mathcal{A}_{T}-\mathcal{A}_{t_{i}}|\leq\Gamma.

(5.20)

Next, for a general stopping time
\tau\in[S,T]
, we consider a sequence of stopping times
\tau_{n}
taking finitely many values in
[S,T]
and converging to
\tau
from above. Then, using the continuity of
\mathcal{A}
and (
5.20
), we deduce (note that the continuity is crucial here)

\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\tau}|\mathcal{A}_{T}-\mathcal{A}_{\tau}|\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\tau}|\mathcal{A}_{T}-\mathcal{A}_{\tau_{n}}|=\liminf_{n\to\infty}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\tau}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\tau_{n}}|\mathcal{A}_{T}-\mathcal{A}_{\tau_{n}}|\leq\Gamma.

(5.21)

Finally, let
\tau\leq\eta
be arbitrarily stopping times taking values in
[S,T]
. Then
(
5.21
) yields

\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\tau}|\mathcal{A}_{\eta}-\mathcal{A}_{\tau}|\leq\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\tau}|\mathcal{A}_{T}-\mathcal{A}_{\tau}|+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\tau}|\mathcal{A}_{T}-\mathcal{A}_{\eta}|\leq\Gamma+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\tau}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\eta}|\mathcal{A}_{T}-\mathcal{A}_{\eta}|\leq 2\Gamma,

which is (
5.19
).

Step 2
.
Let us verify that condition (
5.17
) holds for
X=V^{*}
,
Y=2\Gamma
. Take arbitrary
\lambda,h>0
and consider two stopping times:

\tau:=T\wedge\inf\{r\in[S,T]:|\mathcal{A}_{r}-\mathcal{A}_{S}|\geq\lambda\};\qquad\eta:=T\wedge\inf\{r\in[S,T]:|\mathcal{A}_{r}-\mathcal{A}_{S}|\geq(1+h)\lambda\}.

Then, by continuity of
\mathcal{A}
, we get

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}\bigl(V^{*}\geq(1+h)\lambda\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{P}\bigl(|\mathcal{A}_{\eta}-\mathcal{A}_{s}|=(1+h)\lambda\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{P}\bigl(|\mathcal{A}_{\tau}-\mathcal{A}_{s}|=\lambda,|\mathcal{A}_{\eta}-\mathcal{A}_{s}|-|\mathcal{A}_{\tau}-\mathcal{A}_{s}|=h\lambda)

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{P}\bigl(|\mathcal{A}_{\tau}-\mathcal{A}_{s}|=\lambda,|\mathcal{A}_{\eta}-\mathcal{A}_{\tau}|\geq h\lambda)

\displaystyle=\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt\mathbbm{1}_{\{|\mathcal{A}_{\tau}-\mathcal{A}_{s}|=\lambda\}}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\tau}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|\mathcal{A}_{\eta}-\mathcal{A}_{\tau}|\geq h\lambda\}}

\displaystyle\leq\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt\mathbbm{1}_{\{|\mathcal{A}_{\tau}-\mathcal{A}_{s}|=\lambda\}}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\tau}\frac{|\mathcal{A}_{\eta}-\mathcal{A}_{\tau}|}{h\lambda}

\displaystyle\leq 2\Gamma h^{-1}\lambda^{-1}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt\mathbbm{1}_{\{|\mathcal{A}_{\tau}-\mathcal{A}_{s}|=\lambda\}}=2\Gamma h^{-1}\lambda^{-1}\mathsf{P}(V^{*}\geq\lambda),

(5.22)

where in the last inequality we used (
5.19
).

Now let
\theta\in(0,1)
. If
\theta\geq\frac{2\Gamma}{h\lambda}
, then (
5.2
) implies

\mathsf{P}\bigl(V^{*}\geq(1+h)\lambda\bigr)\leq\theta\mathsf{P}(V^{*}\geq\lambda),

If
\theta\leq\frac{2\Gamma}{h\lambda}
, then
\mathsf{P}(2\Gamma\geq\theta h\lambda)=1
and we trivially have

\mathsf{P}\bigl(V^{*}\geq(1+h)\lambda\bigr)\leq 1=\mathsf{P}(2\Gamma\geq\theta h\lambda).

Since
h,\lambda>0
and
\theta\in(0,1)
were arbitrary, we see that in both cases condition (
5.17
) holds. Hence, by good
\lambda
inequality (
Proposition

5.8
), we get

\|V^{*}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq Cm\Gamma,

for
C>0
independent of
m,d
, which is the desired bound (
5.16
).
∎

5.3
Integral functionals of a Lévy process

Now, equipped with the John–Nirenberg inequality, we can extend the bound (
5.1
) to the
\alpha
-stable case and overcome the issue that the rate deteriorates for large
m
. In this and the next subsections, we follow
[
BDG22
]
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
,
L
be a
d
-dimensional symmetric
\alpha
-stable process with the characteristic function (
1.7
),
\alpha\in(1,2)
. We denote its density by
p^{\alpha}_{t}
and let
P^{\alpha}_{t}
,
t\geq 0
, be the corresponding semigroup.

The following extension of
Theorem

5.1
holds.

Theorem 5.9
.

Let
\alpha\in(1,2)
,
\gamma\in(0,1]
,
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
,
m\geq 2
,
\varepsilon>0
. Then there exists a constant
C=C(\alpha,\gamma,\varepsilon,d,m)>0
such that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
one has

\Bigl\|\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\Bigl|\int_{0}^{t}(f(L_{r})-f(L_{\kappa_{n}(r)}))\,dr\Bigr|\,\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}n^{-((\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma}{\alpha})\wedge 1)+\varepsilon}.

(5.23)

Note that the convergence rate in the right-hand side of (
5.23
) does not decrease for large
m
.
To obtain this result, we need an analogues of Gaussian bounds provided in
Lemmas

3.1
and
5.3
for the Lévy case.

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
,
\gamma\in[0,1]
,
\varepsilon>0
. Then there exists
{C=C(\alpha,\gamma,\varepsilon,d)>0}
such that for any
0<s\leq t
we have

\displaystyle\|P^{\alpha}_{t}f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}\leq Ct^{\frac{\gamma-1}{\alpha}}\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}};\quad\|\nabla P^{\alpha}_{t}f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}\leq Ct^{\frac{\gamma-2}{\alpha}}\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}};

(5.24)

\displaystyle\|P^{\alpha}_{t}f-P^{\alpha}_{s}f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}s^{\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}-1-\varepsilon}(t-s).

(5.25)

The proof of
Lemma

5.10
follows from a scaling argument and is left to the reader, see
5.32
.

It is also easy to see that the following analogue of (
3.4
) holds: for any bounded measurable
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,
0\leq s<t
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}f(L_{t}+x)=P^{\alpha}_{t-s}f(L_{s}+x)

(5.26)

Proof of
Theorem

5.9
.

Let us see how the John–Nirenberg inequality helps us to overcome the obstacle described at the beginning of
Section

5.2
. The idea is to obtain (
5.23
) first for
m=2
using a similar strategy as before (stochastic sewing), and then extend it to the case of arbitrary
m>2
using the John–Nirenberg inequality.
Fix
\varepsilon>0
.

Step 0
. Without loss of generality, and to simplify the notation, we additionally assume that

\gamma<\frac{\alpha}{2}.

(5.27)

Indeed, if
\gamma\geq\frac{\alpha}{2}
, then
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}\subset\mathcal{C}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-\alpha\varepsilon}
, and we may prove the theorem with
\frac{\alpha}{2}-\alpha\varepsilon
in place of
\gamma
. Since in this case the right-hand side of (
5.23
) remains unchanged when
\gamma
is replaced by
\frac{\alpha}{2}-\alpha\varepsilon
, this implies the desired estimate also for
\gamma
.

Step 1: Case
m=2
. We apply the stochastic sewing lemma to the germ

A_{s,t}:=\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\int_{s}^{t}(f(L_{r})-f(L_{\kappa_{n}(r)}))\,dr,\quad(s,t)\in\Delta_{[S,T]}.

It is easy to see that
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\delta A_{s,u,t}=0
for any
(s,u,t)\in\Delta^{3}_{[S,T]}
and therefore condition (
2.11
) holds.

To verify (
2.10
), as in the proof of
Theorem

5.1
, for
s\in[S,T]
we define
s^{\prime}:=\kappa_{n}(s)+\frac{2}{n}
; that is
s^{\prime}
is the second gridpoint to the right of
s
. Then we have for any
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[S,T]}

\displaystyle\|A_{s,t}\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\Bigl\|\int_{s}^{s^{\prime}\wedge t}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\big(f(L_{r})-f(L_{\kappa_{n}(r)})\big)dr\Bigr\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}\!\!\!+\Bigl\|\int_{s^{\prime}\wedge t}^{t}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\big(f(L_{r})-f(L_{\kappa_{n}(r)})\big)dr\Bigr\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=:I_{1}+I_{2}

(5.28)

As we explained in
Section

5.2
, the first term on the right-hand side of (
5.3
) is dangerous in the Lévy case: for large
m
, it reduces the convergence rate because
L
has moments only up to order
\alpha
, and we need the moment of order
m\gamma
. However, for
m=2
, we are safe, recall assumption (
5.27
). We derive

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{s}^{s^{\prime}\wedge t}\|f(L_{r})-f(L_{\kappa_{n}(r)})\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}\,dr\leq\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\int_{s}^{s^{\prime}\wedge t}\bigl(\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt|L_{r}-L_{\kappa_{n}(r)}|^{2\gamma}\bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}\,dr

\displaystyle\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}((s^{\prime}\wedge t)-s)n^{-\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}n^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}+\varepsilon}(t-s)^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon},

(5.29)

for
C=C(\alpha,\gamma,d)>0
. Here in the last inequality we used that
(s^{\prime}\wedge t)-s\leq t-s
and

(s^{\prime}\wedge t)-s\leq 2n^{-1}
by definition of
s^{\prime}
.

Next, we treat
I_{2}
using
Lemma

5.10
. If
s^{\prime}\wedge t=t
, then there is nothing to prove,
I_{2}=0
. Otherwise, if
s^{\prime}\wedge t=s^{\prime}
, then
t\geq s^{\prime}\geq s+\frac{1}{n}
and we get

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle=\Bigl\|\int_{s^{\prime}}^{t}(P^{\alpha}_{r-s}f(L_{s})-P^{\alpha}_{\kappa_{n}(r)-s}f(L_{s}))\,dr\Bigr\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}\leq\int_{s^{\prime}}^{t}\|P^{\alpha}_{r-s}f-P^{\alpha}_{\kappa_{n}(r)-s}f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}\,dr

\displaystyle\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\int_{s^{\prime}}^{t}(\kappa_{n}(r)-s)^{\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}-1-\varepsilon}n^{-1}\,dr

\displaystyle\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\int_{s^{\prime}}^{t}(r-s^{\prime})^{\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}-1-\varepsilon}n^{-1}\,dr\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(t-s^{\prime})^{\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}-\varepsilon}n^{-1}

(5.30)

\displaystyle\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(t-s)^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon}n^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}+2\varepsilon}

for
C=C(\alpha,\gamma,\varepsilon,d)>0
. Here in (
5.30
) we used that
r-\kappa_{n}(r)\leq\frac{1}{n}\leq s^{\prime}-s
and hence
\kappa_{n}(r)-s\geq r-s^{\prime}
and in the last inequality we used that
t-s\geq\frac{1}{n}
. Now, combining this with (
5.3
) and (
5.3
), we get

\|A_{s,t}\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(t-s)^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon}n^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{2}+2\varepsilon}

and thus condition (
2.10
) is satisfied.

It is easy to see that both of the remaining conditions (condition (i) and (ii)) of
Theorem

2.3
hold, and
we get for any
(S,T)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}

\Bigl\|\int_{S}^{T}(f(L_{r})-f(L_{\kappa_{n}(r)}))\,dr\Bigr\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(T-S)^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon}n^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}+2\varepsilon},

(5.31)

for
C=C(\alpha,\gamma,\varepsilon,d)>0
.

Step 2: Case
m>2
. Now we extend
L_{2}(\Omega)
bound to
L_{m}(\Omega)
bound.
We apply
Theorem

5.6
(John–Nirenberg inequality) with
S=0
,
T=1
to the process

\mathcal{A}_{t}:=\int_{0}^{t}(f(L_{r})-f(L_{\kappa_{n}(r)}))\,dr,\quad t\in[0,1].

Obviously, the process
\mathcal{A}
is continuous because
f
is bounded.

Now let us verify condition (
5.15
). As before, we run into an issue with conditioning: since the expression for
\mathcal{A}_{t}
includes
L_{\kappa_{n}(r)}
, it may happen that
s\leq r
but
s>\kappa_{n}(r)
, in which case no smoothing occurs. To overcome this, we use a similar trick as before, distinguishing two cases. If
s
is a grid point, then
s\leq r
automatically implies
s\leq\kappa_{n}(r)
. Otherwise, we split the integral into two parts: a small part, where we apply a rough bound using
\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}
, and a larger part, where we can exploit the conditioning.

Thus, we take arbitrary
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
. If
s
is a gridpoint (so that
\kappa_{n}(s)=s
), we get

\displaystyle\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}|\mathcal{A}_{t}-\mathcal{A}_{s}|

\displaystyle=\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\Bigl|\int_{s}^{t}(f(L_{r})-f(L_{\kappa_{n}(r)}))\,dr\Bigr|

\displaystyle=\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt\Bigl|\int_{s}^{t}(f(L_{r}-L_{s}+x)-f(L_{\kappa_{n}(r)}-L_{s}+x))\,dr\Bigr|\bigg\rvert_{x=L_{s}}.

(5.32)

It follows from (
5.31
) and stationarity of the increments of
L
that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt\Bigl|\int_{s}^{t}(f(L_{r}-L_{s}+x)-f(L_{\kappa_{n}(r)}-L_{s}+x))\,dr\Bigr|

\displaystyle=\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt\Bigl|\int_{0}^{t-s}(f(L_{r}+x)-f(L_{\kappa_{n}(r)}+x))\,dr\Bigr|

\displaystyle\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}n^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}+2\varepsilon},

for
C=C(\alpha,\gamma,\varepsilon,d)>0
, and we used again that
s
is a gridpoint. Substituting this into (
5.3
), we get

\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}|\mathcal{A}_{t}-\mathcal{A}_{s}|\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}n^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}+2\varepsilon}.

(5.33)

If
s
is not a gridpoint, we denote as before
s^{\prime}:=\kappa_{n}(s)+\frac{2}{n}
— the second gridpoint to the right of
s
. We get

\displaystyle\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}|\mathcal{A}_{t}-\mathcal{A}_{s}|

\displaystyle\leq\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}|\mathcal{A}_{s^{\prime}}-\mathcal{A}_{s}|+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}|\mathcal{A}_{t}-\mathcal{A}_{s^{\prime}}|\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}|s^{\prime}-s|+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s^{\prime}}|\mathcal{A}_{t}-\mathcal{A}_{s^{\prime}}|

\displaystyle\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}n^{-1}+C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}n^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}+2\varepsilon}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}n^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}+2\varepsilon},

(5.34)

for
C=C(\alpha,\gamma,\varepsilon,d)>0

where the penultimate inequality follows from (
5.33
). Thus, all conditions of
Theorem

5.6
are satisfied and we get for any
m\geq 2

\Bigl\|\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\Bigl|\int_{0}^{t}(f(L_{r})-f(L_{\kappa_{n}(r)}))\,dr\Bigr|\,\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}=\|\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|\mathcal{A}_{t}-\mathcal{A}_{0}|\,\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}n^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}+2\varepsilon}

for
C=C(\alpha,\gamma,\varepsilon,d,m)>0
.
This implies the statement of the lemma.
∎

5.4
Estimates in conditional Hölder norms

The next milestone on our way toward establishing
Theorem

1.5
is extending the well-posedness results of
Theorem

1.1
to SDEs driven by
\alpha
-stable noise. Let
X
be a solution to SDE

X_{t}=x+\int_{0}^{t}b(X_{r})dr+L_{t},

where
L
is a symmetric
d
-dimensional
\alpha
-stable process,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
b\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\gamma>1-\frac{\alpha}{2}
,
\alpha\in(1,2)
.
However, if we apply the same strategy as in
Section

3
, two important new obstacles appear.

The first problem is related to
Remark

3.6
. When proving
Theorem

3.4
, we had to impose the following regularity condition on the drift
\varphi
:

|\varphi_{t}-\varphi_{s}|\leq\Gamma_{0}|t-s|\quad a.s.

(5.35)

for some
\Gamma_{0}>0
.
In the setting of
Theorem

1.1
, this condition was optimal: indeed, if
\varphi_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}b(X_{r})\,dr
, where the function
b
is bounded, then we do not expect
\varphi
to have better a.s. regularity. However, in our new setting, we work with much nicer drifts belonging to a certain Hölder space. Therefore, while condition (
5.35
) still obviously holds, it does not capture the full information about
\varphi
, and would therefore inevitably lead to suboptimal results.

The second problem was described in
Remark

3.7
. If we perform exactly the same calculations as in the proof of
Theorem

3.4
but now for
\alpha<2
, then the first term on the right-hand side of (
3.17
) becomes

C\Gamma_{0}\|\varphi-\psi\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([S,T])}|u-s|\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\int_{u}^{t}(r-u)^{\frac{\gamma-2}{\alpha}}\,dr

(5.36)

For the integral to be finite, we need to require
\frac{\gamma-2}{\alpha}>-1
, that is

\gamma>2-\alpha
, which is much worse than our desired condition
\gamma>1-\frac{\alpha}{2}
, see (
1.8
).

In this subsection, we will deal with the first problem and postpone the solution of the second problem to the next subsection.

To solve the first problem, let us look at the regularity of

\varphi_{t}:=x+\int_{0}^{t}b(X_{r})dr
. Since
X
is not continuous, we do not expect that
\varphi\in\mathcal{C}^{\theta}
a.s. for any
\theta>1
. Note however, that
X
is stochastically continuous (that is,
X_{t+\varepsilon}\to X_{t}
in probability as
\varepsilon\to 0
) and it is not difficult to see that

\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt|\varphi_{t}-\varphi_{s}-(t-s)\varphi^{\prime}_{s}|\leq\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}|t-s|^{1+\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}},

so “stochastic regularity” of
\varphi
is
1+\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}
.

Next, observe that there was an additional smoothing when we passed from (
3.2
) to (
3.17
), and what we actually need is an a.s. bound on
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}|\varphi_{t}-\varphi_{s}|
. Unfortunately, this still does not give us the desired exponent
1+\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}
, as the best we get is only

\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}|\varphi_{t}-\varphi_{s}|\leq C(t-s).

However, if we change the approximation of
L_{t}+\varphi_{t}
in the germ (
3.13
) from
L_{r}+\varphi_{s}
to
L_{r}+\varphi_{s}+(t-s)\varphi^{\prime}_{s}
, then when passing from (
3.2
) to (
3.17
) we would need to bound
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}|\varphi_{t}-\varphi_{s}-(t-s)\varphi^{\prime}_{s}|
, which is much smaller.

We now take one step further and observe that the best possible approximation of a random variable by an
\mathcal{F}_{s}
-measurable random variable in
L_{2}(\Omega)
is its conditional expectation. Therefore, motivated by the above discussion we introduce the following seminorm. For
m\geq 1
,
(S,T)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
, a measurable function
f\colon\Omega\times[S,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\tau>0
put

[f]_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\tau}{L_{m}}([S,T])}:=\sup_{(s,t)\in\Delta_{[S,T]}}\frac{\|f(t)-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}f(t)\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}}{|t-s|^{\tau}}.,

(5.37)

The following useful lemma shows that the conditional expectation is the best approximation (up to a constant) not only in the
L_{2}(\Omega)
sense, but in any
L_{m}(\Omega)
,
m\geq 1
. It implies that the seminorm
[\cdot]_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\tau}{L_{m}}([S,T])}
is weaker than
[\cdot]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{m}([S,T])}
used in
Section

3
.

To simplify the notation we write here
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\mathcal{G}}[\cdot]:=\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt[\cdot|\mathcal{G}]
.

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
m\geq 1
. Let
\mathcal{G}\subset\mathcal{F}
be a
\sigma
-algebra. Let random variables
Y,Z\in L_{m}(\Omega)
and suppose that
Z
in
\mathcal{G}
–measurable. Then

\displaystyle\|Y-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\mathcal{G}}Y\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq 2\|Y-Z\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)};

(5.38)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\mathcal{G}}|Y-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\mathcal{G}}Y|\leq 2\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\mathcal{G}}|Y-Z|;

(5.39)

Proof.

Fix
m\geq 1
. Using that
Z
is
\mathcal{G}
-measurable, we derive

\displaystyle\|Y-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\mathcal{G}}Y\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\|Y-Z\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}+\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\mathcal{G}}Y-Z\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=\|Y-Z\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}+\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\mathcal{G}}(Y-Z)\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq 2\|Y-Z\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)},

where the last inequality follows from Jensen’s inequality (
2.8
).

Very similarly,

\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\mathcal{G}}|Y-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\mathcal{G}}Y|\leq\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\mathcal{G}}|Y-Z|+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\mathcal{G}}|Z-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\mathcal{G}}Y|=\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\mathcal{G}}|Y-Z|+|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\mathcal{G}}(Y-Z)|\leq 2\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{\mathcal{G}}|Y-Z|.\qed

Corollary 5.12
.

For any
(S,T)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
,
m\geq 1
, measurable function
f\colon\Omega\times[S,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
adapted to the filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{t})
,
\tau>0
one has

[f]_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\tau}{L_{m}}([S,T])}\leq 2[f]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{m}([S,T])}.

Proof.

We just note that for any
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[S,T]}
we have thanks to
Lemma

5.11

\|f(t)-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}f(t)\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq 2\|f(t)-f(s)\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)},

because
f_{s}
is
\mathcal{F}_{s}
-measurable by assumption. Thus

[f]_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\tau}{L_{m}}([S,T])}\leq 2\sup_{(s,t)\in\Delta_{[S,T]}}\frac{\|f(t)-f(s)\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}}{|t-s|^{\tau}}=2[f]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{m}([S,T])}\qed

Finally, let us also bound the conditional seminorm of a solution to our SDE (
1.9
).

Corollary 5.13
.

Let
\alpha\in(1,2)
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
b\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\gamma\in[0,1]
.
Let
X
be a solution to (
1.9
) and denote
\varphi:=X-L
. Then
there exists a constant
C=C(\alpha,d)>0
such that for any
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}

\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}|\varphi_{t}-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\varphi_{t}|\leq C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(t-s)^{1+\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}}.

(5.40)

Proof.

Let
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
. Since the random variable
\varphi_{s}+(t-s)b(X_{s})
is
\mathcal{F}_{s}
measurable, by
Lemma

5.11
, we get

\displaystyle\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}|\varphi_{t}-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\varphi_{t}|

\displaystyle\leq 2\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}|\varphi_{t}-\varphi_{s}-(t-s)b(X_{s})|=2\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\Bigl|\int_{s}^{t}(b(X_{r})-b(X_{s}))\,dr\Bigr|

\displaystyle\leq 2\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\int_{s}^{t}(\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}|L_{r}-L_{s}|^{\gamma}+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}|\varphi_{r}-\varphi_{s}|^{\gamma})\,dr\leq C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}|t-s|^{1+\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}},

for
C=C(\alpha,d)>0
and we used that
|\varphi_{r}-\varphi_{s}|\leq\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}|r-s|
. This proves (
5.40
).
∎

5.5
Shifted stochastic sewing lemma

To overcome the second problem discussed in
Section

5.4
(see also
Remark

3.7
), we use Gerencsér’s idea to replace the stochastic sewing lemma (
Theorem

2.3
) with a
shifted
stochastic sewing lemma. Essentially, this allows us to change the factor
(r-u)
in the integrand of (
5.36
) to

r-(u-(t-s))
. As a result, the integral becomes finite and of order
(t-s)^{\frac{\gamma-2}{\alpha}+1}
as desired, even when
\frac{\gamma-2}{\alpha}<-1
.

This is also the first example where we see a general principle in action: SSL is not a one-size-fits-all method. Its components can be adjusted to overcome a specific obstacle without losing the core structure. For further extensions of the shifted SSL, we refer to
[
MP24
]
and
[
AGRT25
, Lemma 2.7]
.

For
0\leq S\leq T
we introduce a modified simplex

\Delta^{shift}_{[S,T]}:=\{(s,t)\in[S,T]^{2}:s<t,\,\,s-(t-s)\geq S\}.

Lemma 5.14

(Shifted stochastic sewing lemma,
[
Ger22
, Lemma 2.2]
)
.

Let
m\in[2,\infty)
,
0\leq S\leq T
.
Suppose that there exist measurable functions
{\mathcal{A}\colon\Omega\times[S,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}}
,
A\colon\Omega\times\Delta^{shift}_{[S,T]}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and a complete filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[S,T]}
such that the following holds:

1.

one has

\mathcal{A}_{T}-\mathcal{A}_{S}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}A_{S+i\frac{T-S}{n},S+(i+1)\frac{T-S}{n}}\,\,\text{in probability.}

(5.41)

2.

for any
(s,t)\in\Delta^{shift}_{[S,T]}
, the random variable
A_{s,t}
is
\mathcal{F}_{t}
–measurable;

3.

there exist
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}\geq 0
,
\gamma_{1}>\frac{1}{2}
,
\gamma_{2}>1
such that for every
(s,t)\in\Delta^{shift}_{[S,T]}
and
u=(s+t)/2
we have

\displaystyle\|A_{s,t}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq\Gamma_{1}|t-s|^{\gamma_{1}},

(5.42)

\displaystyle\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s-(t-s)}\delta A_{s,u,t}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq\Gamma_{2}|t-s|^{\gamma_{2}}.

(5.43)

Then there exists a constant
C=C(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},d,m)>0
independent of
S
,
T
such that

\|\mathcal{A}_{T}-\mathcal{A}_{S}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq C\Gamma_{1}|T-S|^{\gamma_{1}}+C\Gamma_{2}|T-S|^{\gamma_{2}}.

(5.44)

Remark 5.15
.

The key difference between the shifted SSL and the usual SSL is condition (
5.43
), which allows for additional smoothing by replacing the conditional expectation
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}
with
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s-(t-s)}
. We will see the benefits of this later in
Section

5.6
.

The proof relies on the following simple bound, which is a corollary of the BDG inequality.

Proposition 5.16
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
(X_{i})_{i=2,\ldots,n}
be a sequence of random vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
adapted to
the filtration
(\mathcal{G}_{i})_{i=0,\ldots,n}
. Then for any
m\geq 2
there exists a constant
C=C(m)>0
independent of
n
such that

\Bigl\|\sum_{i=2}^{n}X_{i}\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq C\sum_{i=2}^{n}\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt[X_{i}|\mathcal{F}_{i-2}]\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}+C\Bigl(\sum_{i=2}^{n}\|X_{i}\|^{2}_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\Bigr)^{1/2}.

(5.45)

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\Bigl\|\sum_{i=2}^{n}X_{i}\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\Bigl\|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[2,n]\\
\text{ $i$ is even}\end{subarray}}(X_{i}-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt[X_{i}|\mathcal{G}_{i-2})\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}+\Bigl\|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[2,n]\\
\text{ $i$ is odd}\end{subarray}}(X_{i}-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt[X_{i}|\mathcal{G}_{i-2})\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq}+\sum_{i=2}^{n}\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt[X_{i}|\mathcal{F}_{i-2}]\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}.

(5.46)

The sequence
(X_{i}-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt[X_{i}|\mathcal{G}_{i-2})_{i\in[2,n],\text{ $i$ is even}}
is a martingale difference sequence. Therefore, using (
2.13
) and Jensen’s inequality, we derive

\displaystyle\Bigl\|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[2,n]\\
\text{ $i$ is even}\end{subarray}}(X_{i}-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt[X_{i}|\mathcal{G}_{i-2}])\Bigr\|^{2}_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[2,n]\\
\text{ $i$ is even}\end{subarray}}\|X_{i}-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt[X_{i}|\mathcal{G}_{i-2}]\|^{2}_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\ C\sum_{i\in[2,n]}(\|X_{i}\|^{2}_{L_{m}(\Omega)}+\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt[X_{i}|\mathcal{G}_{i-2}]\|^{2}_{L_{m}(\Omega)})

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{i\in[2,n]}\|X_{i}\|^{2}_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

for
C=C(m)
. Clearly, the same bound holds for the second term in the right-hand side of (
5.46
). Substituting this into (
5.46
), we get the desired inequality (
5.45
).
∎

Proof of
Lemma

5.14
.

Let us modify appropriately the proof of the SSL. Let
m\geq 2
. Fix
0\leq S\leq T
and consider the dyadic partition
t^{n}_{i}=S+\frac{i}{2^{n}}(T-S)
,
i=0,1,\ldots,2^{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, of the interval
[S,T]
. Put

A^{(n)}:=\sum_{i=1}^{2^{n}-1}A_{t^{n}_{i},t^{n}_{i+1}},\,n\in\mathbb{N}.

Note that the very first element,
A_{t_{0}^{n},t_{1}^{n}}
is not included in the sum as the pair
(t_{0}^{n},t_{1}^{n})=(S,S+2^{-n})
does not belong to
\Delta^{shift}_{[S,T]}
. On the other hand, if
i\in[1,2^{n}-1]
an
i
is an integer, that
(t_{i}^{n},t_{i+1}^{n})\in\Delta^{shift}_{[S,T]}
and thus
A^{(n)}
is well-defined.

As in the proof of
Theorem

2.3
we deduce that by Fatou’s lemma and condition (
5.41
)

\|\mathcal{A}_{T}-\mathcal{A}_{S}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\|A^{(n)}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq\|A_{S,T}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\|A^{(n+1)}-A^{(n)}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}.

(5.47)

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
we apply bound (
5.45
) with
2^{n}
in place of
n
,
X_{i}:=\delta A_{t^{n}_{i-1},t^{n+1}_{2i-1},t^{n}_{i}}
,
\mathcal{G}_{i}:=\mathcal{F}_{t^{n}_{i}}
. Using assumptions (
5.42
) and (
5.43
), we derive

\displaystyle\|A^{(n+1)}-A^{(n)}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\Bigl\|\sum_{i=2}^{2^{n}}\delta A_{t^{n}_{i-1},t^{n+1}_{2i-1},t^{n}_{i}}\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}+\|A_{t^{n+1}_{1},t^{n+1}_{2}}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\quad\leq C\sum_{i=2}^{2^{n}}\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{t^{n}_{i-2}}\delta A_{t^{n}_{i-1},t^{n+1}_{2i-1},t^{n}_{i}}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}+C\Bigl(\sum_{i=2}^{2^{n}}\|\delta A_{t^{n}_{i-1},t^{n+1}_{2i-1},t^{n}_{i}}\|^{2}_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\Bigr)^{1/2}+\|A_{t^{n+1}_{1},t^{n+1}_{2}}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\quad\leq C\Gamma_{2}2^{-n(\gamma_{2}-1)}|t-s|^{\gamma_{2}}+C\Gamma_{1}2^{-n(\gamma_{1}-\frac{1}{2})}|t-s|^{\gamma_{1}}+\Gamma_{1}2^{-n\gamma_{1}}|t-s|^{\gamma_{1}},

(5.48)

where
C=C(d,m)>0
. To get the first term in the last inequality we used that, by construction,

{t^{n}_{i-1}-(t^{n}_{i}-t^{n}_{i-1})}=t^{n}_{i-2}

and therefore bound (
5.43
) is applicable. To get the second term in the last inequality, we used (
5.42
) and a simple inequality

\|\delta A_{s,u,t}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq\|A_{s,t}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}+\|A_{s,u}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}+\|A_{u,t}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq 3\Gamma_{1}|t-s|^{\gamma_{1}},\,\,(s,u,t)\in\Delta^{3}_{[S,T]}.

Inequality (
5.5
) is where the key improvement of the shifted SSL over the usual SSL occurs. The updated BDG-type bound (
5.45
) allows us to insert additional smoothing and replace
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{t^{n}_{i-1}}[\dots]
, which appeared in the proof of the SSL, with
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{t^{n}_{i-2}}[\dots]
at no cost.

Summing up inequalities (
5.5
) over
n
and substituting them back into (
5.47
), we get

\|\mathcal{A}_{T}-\mathcal{A}_{S}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq C\Gamma_{2}|T-S|^{\gamma_{2}}+C\Gamma_{1}|T-S|^{\gamma_{1}}

for
C=C(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},d,m)>0
. Here we used once again using (
5.42
) to bound
\|A_{S,T}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}
in (
5.47
). This shows (
5.44
).
∎

5.6
Strong well-posedness for SDEs with Lévy noise

Now, equipped with the tools developed in the previous two subsections, we are ready to establish the well-posedness of SDEs with irregular drift driven by an
\alpha
-stable process, that is,
Theorem

1.3
.
The idea is for
0\leq S\leq T
to bound moments of

\mathcal{A}_{t}:=\int_{0}^{t}b(L_{r}+\varphi_{r})-b(L_{r}+\psi_{r})\,dr,\quad t\in[S,T],

where
\varphi
,
\psi
are relatively regular perturbations, in terms of
\|\varphi-\psi\|
, that is, to extend
Theorem

3.4
to the Lévy case. To achieve this, we change the germ, replace some of the seminorms used in
Theorem

3.4
by the conditional seminorms introduced in
Section

5.4
, and we replace SSL by a shifted SSL. This refined approach yields
Theorem

1.3
in the full regime
b\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}
,
\gamma>1-\frac{\alpha}{2}
, recall condition (
1.8
).

Let us now discuss what to take as the germ in the shifted SSL to approximate the process
\mathcal{A}
introduced above. A natural first idea is to proceed as in the proof of
Theorem

3.4
and set

A_{s.t}:=\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\int_{s}^{t}b(L_{r}+\varphi_{s})-b(L_{r}+\psi_{s})\,dr,\quad(s,t)\in\Delta^{shift}_{[S,T]}.

However, if we now recall the discussion in
Section

5.4
, in order to capture the fact that the stochastic regularity of
\varphi
exceeds
1
(see
Corollary

5.13
), we need a better approximation of
\varphi_{r}
than simply
\varphi_{s}
. At the same time, this approximation must be
\mathcal{F}_{s}
-measurable, since we require access to the conditional law
\law(L_{r}+\text{approximation}|\mathcal{F}_{s})
. The best candidate is
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\varphi_{r}
. Thus, the second idea would be to set

A_{s.t}:=\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\int_{s}^{t}b(L_{r}+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\varphi_{r})-b(L_{r}+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\psi_{r})\,dr,\quad(s,t)\in\Delta^{shift}_{[S,T]}.

The problem now is that, to make all the integrals in the calculation of
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\delta A_{s,u,t}
finite, we need some extra smoothing, see the discussion in
Remark

3.7
and at the beginning of
Section

5.4
. Therefore, we introduce our final choice of the germ:

A_{s.t}:=\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s-(t-s)}\int_{s}^{t}b(L_{r}+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s-(t-s)}\varphi_{r})-b(L_{r}+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s-(t-s)}\psi_{r})\,dr,\quad(s,t)\in\Delta^{shift}_{[S,T]}.

(5.49)

Note that we have also changed our approximation to
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s-(t-s)}\varphi_{r}
in order to keep access to

\law(L_{r}+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s-(t-s)}\varphi_{r}|\mathcal{F}_{s-(t-s)})
.

Now we are ready to prove the following key bound. Recall the definition of the seminorm
[\cdot]_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\tau}{L_{m}}([S,T])}
in (
5.37
).

Lemma 5.17
.

Let
m\geq 2
,
\alpha\in(1,2)
,
\gamma\in(1-\frac{\alpha}{2},1]
,
\tau\in(0,1]
,
\theta>0
. Let
b\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
. Let
\psi,\psi\colon\Omega\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
be bounded measurable functions adapted to
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
. Assume that

\tau+\frac{\gamma-1}{\alpha}>0,\quad\theta+\frac{\gamma-2}{\alpha}>0.

(5.50)

Suppose further that
there exists a constant
\Gamma_{0}\geq 0
such that for any
0\leq s\leq t\leq 1

\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}|\varphi_{t}-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\varphi_{t}|+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}|\psi_{t}-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\psi_{t}|\leq\Gamma_{0}|t-s|^{\theta}.

(5.51)

Then there exists a constant
C=C(\alpha,\gamma,\tau,d,m)>0
such that
for any
(S,T)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
one has

\displaystyle\Bigl\|\int_{S}^{T}\bigl(b(L_{r}+\varphi_{r})-b(L_{r}+\psi_{r})\bigr)\,dr\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\,\,\leq C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(T-S)^{1+\frac{\gamma-1}{\alpha}}\bigl((1+\Gamma_{0})\|\varphi-\psi\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([S,T])}+[\varphi-\psi]_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\tau}{L_{m}}([S,T])}(T-S)^{\tau}\bigr).

(5.52)

Remark 5.18
.

We see that compared with
Theorem

3.4
, regularity condition (
3.11
) is now replaced by stochastic regularity condition (
5.51
).

Proof.

Fix
m\geq 2
.
0\leq S\leq T\leq 1
. Let us verify that all the conditions of the shifted SSL (
Lemma

5.14
) are satisfied for the processes

\mathcal{A}_{t}:=\int_{0}^{t}b(L_{r}+\varphi_{r})-b(L_{r}+\psi_{r})\,dr,\qquad t\in[S,T].

and the germ
A_{s,t}
defined in (
5.49
).

Let
(s,t)\in\Delta^{shift}_{[S,T]}
. Recall identity (
5.26
) and the definition of the semigroup
P^{\alpha}
in
Section

5.3
. We get

\displaystyle|A_{s,t}|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{s}^{t}|P^{\alpha}_{r-(s-(t-s))}b(L_{s-(t-s)}+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s-(t-s)}\varphi_{r})-P^{\alpha}_{r-(s-(t-s))}b(L_{s-(t-s)}+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s-(t-s)}\psi_{r})|\,dr

\displaystyle\leq\int_{s}^{t}\|P^{\alpha}_{r-(s-(t-s))}b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s-(t-s)}(\varphi_{r}-\psi_{r})|\,dr.

Thus, by (
5.24
) and Jensen’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\|A_{s,t}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\int_{s}^{t}(r-s)^{\frac{\gamma-1}{\alpha}}\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s-(t-s)}(\varphi_{r}-\psi_{r})\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\,dr

\displaystyle\leq C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(t-s)^{1+\frac{\gamma-1}{\alpha}}\sup_{r\in[S,T]}\|\varphi_{r}-\psi_{r}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\|\varphi-\psi\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([S,T])}(t-s)^{1+\frac{\gamma-1}{\alpha}}

(5.53)

for
C=C(\alpha,\gamma,d)>0
. Note that by the assumption
\gamma>1-\alpha/2
, we have
1+\frac{\gamma-1}{\alpha}>1/2
and therefore, condition (
5.42
) is satisfied.

Now let us verify condition (
5.43
). As required, we take
(s,t)\in\Delta^{shift}_{[S,T]}
, and
u:=(t+s)/2
. It will be convenient to denote
s_{1}:=s-(t-s)
,
s_{2}:=s-(u-s)
,
s_{3}:=s
,
s_{4}:=u
,
s_{5}:=t
. One has
s_{1}\leq s_{2}\leq s_{3}\leq s_{4}\leq s_{5}
, see
Fig.

5.1
. We have

Figure 5.1:
Time points for bounding
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s-(t-s)}\delta A_{s,u,t}

\displaystyle\delta A_{s,u,t}

\displaystyle=\delta A_{s_{3},s_{4},s_{5}}

\displaystyle=\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{1}}\int_{s_{3}}^{s_{5}}(b(L_{r}+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{1}}\varphi_{r})-b(L_{r}+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{1}}\psi_{r})\,dr

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{2}}\int_{s_{3}}^{s_{4}}(b(L_{r}+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{2}}\varphi_{r})-b(L_{r}+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{2}}\psi_{r})

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{3}}\int_{s_{4}}^{s_{5}}(b(L_{r}+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{3}}\varphi_{r})-b(L_{r}+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{3}}\psi_{r})\,dr,

where we used the identity
u-(t-u)=s=s_{3}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s-(t-s)}\delta A_{s,u,t}

\displaystyle\quad=\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{1}}\delta A_{s_{3},s_{4},s_{5}}

\displaystyle\quad=\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{1}}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{2}}\int_{s_{3}}^{s_{4}}(b(L_{r}+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{1}}\varphi_{r})-b(L_{r}+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{1}}\psi_{r})-b(L_{r}+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{2}}\varphi_{r})+b(L_{r}+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{2}}\psi_{r}))\,dr

\displaystyle\qquad+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{1}}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{3}}\int_{s_{4}}^{s_{5}}(b(L_{r}+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{1}}\varphi_{r})-b(L_{r}+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{1}}\psi_{r})-b(L_{r}+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{3}}\varphi_{r})+b(L_{r}+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{3}}\psi_{r}))\,dr

\displaystyle\quad=:I_{1}+I_{2}.

(5.54)

We begin with the analysis of
I_{1}
. This is where the benefits of the shifted sewing and conditional norms become apparent. Please compare it with the similar analysis we carried out in the Gaussian case in (
3.2
)–(
3.18
). Using the identity (
5.26
) and
Lemma

3.5
, we derive

\displaystyle|I_{1}|\leq\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{1}}\int_{s_{3}}^{s_{4}}\bigl|P^{\alpha}_{r-s_{2}}b(L_{s_{2}}+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{1}}\varphi_{r})-P^{\alpha}_{r-s_{2}}b(L_{s_{2}}+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{1}}\psi_{r})

\displaystyle\hskip 55.97205pt-P^{\alpha}_{r-s_{2}}b(L_{s_{2}}+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{2}}\varphi_{r})-b(P^{\alpha}_{r-s_{2}}L_{s_{2}}+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{2}}\psi_{r})\bigr|\,dr

\displaystyle\quad\leq\int_{s_{3}}^{s_{4}}\|\nabla P^{\alpha}_{r-s_{2}}b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}\bigl|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{1}}(\varphi_{r}-\psi_{r})\bigr|\,\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{1}}[|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{1}}\varphi_{r}-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{2}}\varphi_{r}|]\,dr

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{s_{3}}^{s_{4}}\|P^{\alpha}_{r-s_{2}}b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{1}}|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{1}}(\varphi_{r}-\psi_{r})-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{2}}(\varphi_{r}-\psi_{r})|\,dr.

Now we can use stochastic regularity of
\varphi
and Jensen’s inequality to get

\displaystyle\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{1}}|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{1}}\varphi_{r}-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{2}}\varphi_{r}|

\displaystyle=\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{1}}|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{2}}(\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{1}}\varphi_{r}-\varphi_{r})|\leq\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{1}}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{2}}|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{1}}\varphi_{r}-\varphi_{r}|=\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{1}}|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{1}}\varphi_{r}-\varphi_{r}|

\displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{0}|r-s_{1}|^{\theta},

recall assumption (
5.51
). Very similarly,

\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{1}}|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{1}}(\varphi_{r}-\psi_{r})-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{2}}(\varphi_{r}-\psi_{r})|\leq\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{1}}|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{1}}(\varphi_{r}-\psi_{r})-(\varphi_{r}-\psi_{r})|.

Therefore, using heat kernel bounds (
5.24
), we get

\displaystyle\|I_{1}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C\Gamma_{0}\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\int_{s_{3}}^{s_{4}}(r-s_{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{\alpha}}\bigl\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{1}}(\varphi_{r}-\psi_{r})\bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}(r-s_{1})^{\theta}\,dr

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq}+C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\int_{s_{3}}^{s_{4}}(r-s_{2})^{\frac{\gamma-1}{\alpha}}\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{1}}(\varphi_{r}-\psi_{r})-(\varphi_{r}-\psi_{r})\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\,dr

\displaystyle\leq C\Gamma_{0}\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\|\varphi-\psi\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([S,T])}(s_{3}-s_{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{\alpha}}(s_{4}-s_{3})(s_{4}-s_{1})^{\theta}

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq}+C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}[\varphi-\psi]_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\tau}{L_{m}}([S,T])}(s_{3}-s_{2})^{\frac{\gamma-1}{\alpha}}(s_{4}-s_{3})(s_{4}-s_{1})^{\tau}

\displaystyle\leq C\Gamma_{0}\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\|\varphi-\psi\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([S,T])}(t-s)^{\frac{\gamma-2}{\alpha}+1+\theta}

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq}+C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}[\varphi-\psi]_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\tau}{L_{m}}([S,T])}(t-s)^{\tau+\frac{\gamma-1}{\alpha}+1},

(5.55)

for
C=C(\alpha,\gamma,d,m)>0
, where the last inequality follows from the fact
s_{3}-s_{2}=s_{4}-s_{3}=(t-s)/2
and
s_{4}-s_{1}=(u-s)+(t-s)=\frac{3}{2}(t-s)
. Note that the integral in (
5.6
) is finite even though
\frac{\gamma-2}{\alpha}
may be less than
-1
. This is where the shifted stochastic sewing played a crucial role; otherwise, the integrand would be
(r-s_{3})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{\alpha}}
, and the integral would diverge.

By exactly the same argument (we just need to take
s_{3}
in place of
s_{2}
,
s_{4}
in place of
s_{3}
and
s_{5}
in place of
s_{4}
), we get
the exact same bound for
I_{2}
, and then by (
5.6
), for
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s-(t-s)}\delta A_{s,u,t}
as well:

\displaystyle\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s-(t-s)}\delta A_{s,u,t}\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C\Gamma_{0}\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\|\varphi-\psi\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{m}([S,T])}(t-s)^{\frac{\gamma-2}{\alpha}+1+\theta}

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq}+C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}[\varphi-\psi]_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\tau}{L_{m}}([S,T])}(t-s)^{\tau+\frac{\gamma-1}{\alpha}+1}.

(5.56)

Since by the assumptions of the lemma
\frac{\gamma-2}{\alpha}+1+\theta>1
and
\tau+\frac{\gamma-1}{\alpha}+1>1
, we see that condition (
5.43
) is satisfied.

Next, it is immediate that condition (ii) of the shifted SSL is satisfied and verification of (
5.41
) is done along the same lines as in the proof of
Theorem

3.4
and is left as an exercise to the reader.

Thus, all the conditions of
Lemma

5.14
are satisfied. Now bound (
5.17
) follows from (
5.6
) and (
5.6
).
∎

Now we can transform
Lemma

5.17
into a stability bound for solutions of the SDE (
1.9
). The idea is to bound the difference between the two solutions in the
[\cdot]_{\mathcal{C}^{1/2}L_{2}([S,T])}
seminorm by half of itself plus the difference between the two drifts. First, we do this for the case when the interval
[S,T]
is small enough, and then we use
Lemma

4.8
to extend the bound to the whole interval.

Corollary 5.19
.

Let
\alpha\in(1,2)
,
\gamma\in(1-\frac{\alpha}{2},1]
.
Let
b^{1},b^{2}\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Let
X^{1}
,
X^{2}
be two solutions adapted to the same filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
to the following SDEs:

X_{t}^{1}=x+\int_{0}^{t}b^{1}(X_{r}^{1})\,dr+L_{t},\quad X_{t}^{2}=x+\int_{0}^{t}b^{2}(X_{r}^{2})\,dr+L_{t}.

Then there exists a constant
C=C(\alpha,\gamma,d,\max(\|b^{1}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}},\|b^{2}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}))>0

such that

\|X^{1}-X^{2}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{2}([0,1])}\leq C\|b^{1}-b^{2}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}.

(5.57)

Proof.

Denote
\varphi:=X^{1}-L
,
\psi:=X^{2}-L
,
M:=\max(\|b^{1}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}},\|b^{2}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}})
.

Step 1: Estimate on short time intervals
.
It follows from
Corollary

5.13
, that there exists a constant
C=C(\alpha,d)>0
such that

\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}|\varphi_{t}-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\varphi_{t}|+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}|\psi_{t}-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\psi_{t}|\leq CM(t-s)^{1+\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}},\quad(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}.

(5.58)

We apply now
Lemma

5.17
with
\theta=1+\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}
,
\tau=\frac{1}{2}
,
m=2
. It is easy to see, that condition (
5.50
) holds by assumption
\gamma>1-\alpha/2
and condition (
5.51
) holds thanks to the stochastic regularity result (
5.58
). Therefore, it follows from (
5.17
) that there exists
C=C(\alpha,\gamma,d,M)>0
such that for any
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
one has

\displaystyle\|(\varphi_{t}-\varphi_{s})-(\psi_{t}-\psi_{s})\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\Bigl\|\int_{s}^{t}\bigl(b^{1}(L_{r}+\varphi_{r})-b^{1}(L_{r}+\psi_{r})\bigr)\,dr\Bigr\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}+\Bigl\|\int_{s}^{t}(b^{1}-b^{2})(L_{r}+\psi_{r})\,dr\Bigr\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\quad\leq C(t-s)^{1+\frac{\gamma-1}{\alpha}}\bigl(\|\varphi-\psi\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{2}([s,t])}+[\varphi-\psi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{2}([s,t])}(t-s)^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigr)+(t-s)\|b^{1}-b^{2}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}.

Note that here we just used the rough bound
[\cdot]_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\frac{1}{2}}{L_{2}}([s,t])}\leq 2[\cdot]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{2}([s,t])}
from
Corollary

5.12
. However, later in
Section

5.7
, when deriving bounds for the convergence rate of the Euler scheme, we will need a more precise bound involving
[\cdot]_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\frac{1}{2}}{L_{2}}([s,t])}
.

Let
(S,T)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
. We divide both sides of the above inequality by
(t-s)^{1/2}
and take supremum over all
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[S,T]}
. Note that the condition
\gamma>1-\alpha/2
implies
1+\frac{\gamma-1}{\alpha}>\frac{1}{2}
. Therefore, we get

\displaystyle[\varphi-\psi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{2}([S,T])}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\varphi-\psi\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{2}([S,T])}+C(T-S)^{\frac{1}{2}}[\varphi-\psi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{2}([S,T])}+\|b^{1}-b^{2}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}\|\varphi_{S}\!-\!\psi_{S}\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}+C_{0}(T\!-\!S)^{\frac{1}{2}}[\varphi-\psi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{2}([S,T])}+\|b^{1}\!-b^{2}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}.

(5.59)

where
C_{0}=C_{0}(\alpha,\gamma,d,M)>0
and in the last inequality we used (
3.9
).
Take now
\ell\in(0,1]
small enough such that

C_{0}\ell^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\frac{1}{2}.

Then we derive from (
5.6
) that for any
(S,T)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
with
T-S\leq\ell
we have

[\varphi-\psi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{2}([S,T])}\leq 2C_{0}\|\varphi_{S}-\psi_{S}\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}+2\|b^{1}-b^{2}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}.

(5.60)

Step 2: Passage to arbitrary time intervals
.
We apply
Lemma

4.8
(ii) to
f:=\varphi-\psi
.
It follows from (
5.60
) that condition (
4.30
) holds and therefore there exists
C=C(\ell,C_{0})
such that

[\varphi-\psi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{2}([0,1])}\leq C\|b^{1}-b^{2}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}.

Since
\varphi_{0}=\psi_{0}
and
\varphi-\psi=X^{1}-X^{2}
, we get the desired bound (
5.57
).
∎

The obtained stability bound implies, almost immediately, existence and uniqueness of solutions to the SDE (
1.9
). We note that, in contrast to the proof of the existence part of
Theorem

1.1
, we can no longer use Girsanov’s theorem to obtain weak existence “for free”, so we have to establish strong existence directly.

Proof of
Theorem

1.3
.

Strong uniqueness
.
Let
X,Y
be two solutions to SDE (
1.9
) adapted to the filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
. By
Corollary

5.19
, we have

\|X-Y\|_{L_{2}([0,1])}=0.

Therefore,
X_{t}=Y_{t}
a.s. for all
t\in[0,1]
.

Strong existence
.
Let
(b^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of smooth functions
\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
converging to
b
in supremum norm, such that
\|b^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\leq\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}
(for example, one can take
b_{n}:=P_{1/n}b
). Consider an SDE

X^{n}_{t}=x+\int_{0}^{t}b^{n}(X^{n}_{r})\,dr+L_{t},\quad t\in[0,1].

(5.61)

Since
b^{n}
is smooth, this equation has a strong solution. Thus,
X^{n}
is adapted to the completed natural filtration of the Lévy process,
\mathcal{F}^{L}_{t}:=\sigma(L_{s},\mathcal{N};s\leq t)
, where
\mathcal{N}
denotes all
\mathsf{P}
-null sets.

Let
\varphi^{n}:=X^{n}-L
. By
Corollary

5.19
, there exists a constant
C=C(\alpha,\gamma,d,\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}})>0
such that for any
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
,
t\in[0,1]
we have

\|\varphi^{n}_{t}-\varphi^{m}_{t}\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}\leq C\|b^{n}-b^{m}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}.

Since
\|b^{n}-b^{m}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}\to 0
as
n,m\to\infty
, we see that there exists a process
\varphi\colon\Omega\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
adapted to the filtration
(\mathcal{F}^{L}_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
such that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\varphi_{t}-\varphi^{n}_{t}\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}=0.

(5.62)

Furthermore, by Fatou’s lemma, for any
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
we have

\|\varphi_{t}-\varphi_{s}\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\varphi^{n}_{t}-\varphi^{n}_{s}\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}\leq|t-s|\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}.

Therefore, by the Kolmogorov continuity theorem (recall
Theorem

3.12
), we see that the process
\varphi
has a continuous modification, which we will denote
\widetilde{\varphi}
. We claim now that
X:=\widetilde{\varphi}+L
is a strong solution to SDE (
1.9
).

Indeed,
X
is càdlàg by construction and
X
is adapted to
(\mathcal{F}^{L}_{t})
. Note also that for any
t\in[0,1]

\displaystyle\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt\Bigl|\int_{0}^{t}(b(X_{r})-b^{n}(X_{r}^{n}))dr\Bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt\Bigl|\int_{0}^{t}(b-b^{n})(X^{n}_{r})dr\Bigr|+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt\Bigl|\int_{0}^{t}(b(X_{r})-b(X_{r}^{n}))dr\Bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\|b-b^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}+\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\int_{0}^{t}((\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt|X_{r}-X_{r}^{n}|^{\gamma})\wedge 1)dr\to 0,

as
n\to\infty
by the dominated convergence theorem (we used that
X-X^{n}=\widetilde{\varphi}-\varphi^{n}
and (
5.62
)). Thus, we can pass to the limit in (
5.61
) as
n\to\infty
to deduce that for any
t\in[0,1]
we have a.s.

X_{t}=x+\int_{0}^{t}b(X_{r})\,dr+L_{t}.

Hence,
X
is indeed a strong solution to (
1.9
).
∎

5.7
Numerics for SDEs with Lévy noise

Now, after lots of hard work, we can finally harvest the results and obtain the rate of convergence of the Euler scheme to the solution of an SDE driven by a Lévy process.
We fix
\alpha\in(1,2)
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
b\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\gamma\in[0,1]
.

Let
X
be a solution to (
1.9
) and for
n\in\mathbb{N}
we denote by
X^{n}
the corresponding Euler approximation:

X^{n}_{t}:=x+\int_{0}^{t}b(X^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r)})\,dr+L_{t}.

Denote

\varphi:=X-L,\quad\varphi^{n}=X^{n}-L.

As in
Section

5.1
, we aim to bound the approximation error

[\varphi-\varphi^{n}]_{\mathcal{C}^{1/2}L_{2}([S,T])}

(5.63)

by half of itself plus terms of the form
Cn^{-rate}
. It is sufficient to do this only for small intervals
[S,T]
, since the global bound then follows from
Lemma

4.8
. As a first attempt, we use the same decomposition for the error (
5.63
) as in the Brownian case, see
Section

5.1
. We write for
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}

\displaystyle(\varphi_{t}-\varphi^{n}_{t})-(\varphi_{s}-\varphi^{n}_{s})

\displaystyle=\int_{s}^{t}(b(X_{r})-b(X^{n}_{r}))\,dr+\int_{s}^{t}(b(X^{n}_{r})-b(X^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r)}))\,dr

\displaystyle=\int_{s}^{t}(b(L_{r}+\varphi_{r})-b(L_{r}+\varphi^{n}_{r}))\,dr

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\int_{s}^{t}(b(L_{r}+\varphi^{n}_{r})-b(L_{\kappa_{n}(r)}+\varphi^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r)}))\,dr

\displaystyle=:Err_{n,1}(s,t)+Err_{n,2}(s,t).

(5.64)

Note however, that in the Brownian case a bound on
\int(b(W_{r})-b(W_{\kappa_{n}(r)}))\,dr
implied almost immediately a bound on
\int(b(W_{r}+\varphi^{n}_{r})-b(W_{\kappa_{n}(r)}+\varphi^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r)}))\,dr
by Girsanov’s theorem. This tool is clearly not available here. Therefore, we have to change our error decomposition and split the second error term,
Err_{n,2}
, into two. We have

\displaystyle Err_{n,2}(s,t)

\displaystyle=\int_{s}^{t}(b(L_{r}+\varphi^{n}_{r})-b(L_{r}+\varphi^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r)}))\,dr+\int_{s}^{t}(b(L_{r}+\varphi^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r)})-b(L_{\kappa_{n}(r)}+\varphi^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r)}))\,dr

\displaystyle=:Err_{n,21}(s,t)+Err_{n,22}(s,t).

(5.65)

Now we observe that our main integral bound for Lévy processes,
Lemma

5.17
, can handle the first two error terms:
Err_{n,1}
and
Err_{n,21}
. We only need to check that the drifts
\varphi^{n}
and
t\mapsto\varphi^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(t)}
have enough stochastic regularity, so that conditions of
Lemma

5.17
are satisfied. This is not very difficult to check. We argue as in
Corollary

5.13
, but with one important modification: it is no longer true that
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}|L_{\kappa_{n}(r)}-L_{s}|
can be bounded by
C|r-s|^{1/\alpha}
whenever
s\leq r
. Indeed, it may happen that
\kappa_{n}(r)<s\leq r
, in which case
L_{\kappa_{n}(r)}-L_{s}
is no longer independent of
\mathcal{F}_{s}
. Therefore, we have to treat this case separately.

Lemma 5.20
.

There exists a constant
C=C(\alpha,d)>0
such that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}

\displaystyle\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}|\varphi^{n}_{t}-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\varphi^{n}_{t}|\leq C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(t-s)^{1+\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}};

(5.66)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}|\varphi^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(t)}-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\varphi^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(t)}|\leq C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(t-s)^{1+\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}}.

(5.67)

Proof.

Let
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
. Denote by
s^{\prime}
the smallest grid point which is bigger or equal to s, that is,
s^{\prime}:=\frac{1}{n}\lceil ns\rceil
. If
s\leq t\leq s^{\prime}
, then by construction
\varphi_{t}^{n}
is
\mathcal{F}_{s}
-measurable and thus the left-hand side of (
5.66
) is zero. Otherwise, if
s\leq s^{\prime}<t
, we rely again on
Lemma

5.11
. We note that the random variable
\varphi^{n}_{s^{\prime}}+(t-s^{\prime})b(X^{n}_{s})
is
\mathcal{F}_{s}
measurable and therefore

\displaystyle\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}|\varphi^{n}_{t}-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\varphi_{t}|

\displaystyle\leq 2\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}|\varphi^{n}_{t}-\varphi^{n}_{s^{\prime}}-(t-s^{\prime})b(X^{n}_{s})|=2\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\Bigl|\int_{s^{\prime}}^{t}(b(X^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r)})-b(X^{n}_{s}))\,dr\Bigr|

\displaystyle\leq 2\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\int_{s^{\prime}}^{t}(\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}|L_{\kappa_{n}(r)}-L_{s}|^{\gamma}+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}|\varphi^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r)}-\varphi^{n}_{s}|^{\gamma})\,dr\leq C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}|t-s|^{1+\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}},

for
C=C(\alpha,d)>0
and we used that
|\varphi^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r)}-\varphi^{n}_{s}|\leq\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}|\kappa_{n}(r)-s|\leq|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}|r-s|
and
L_{\kappa_{n}(r)}-L_{s}
is independent of
\mathcal{F}_{s}
. This proves (
5.66
).

To show (
5.67
), we just note that if
\kappa_{n}(t)\leq s
, then the left-hand side of this inequality is
0
and the bound is trivial. Otherwise, we just apply (
5.66
) with
\kappa_{n}(t)
in place of
t
. We get

\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}|\varphi^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(t)}-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\varphi^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(t)}|\leq C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(\kappa_{n}(t)-s)^{1+\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}}\leq C\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(t-s)^{1+\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}}.\qed

Now we have all the tools to bound
Err_{n,1}
and
Err_{n,21}
, defined in (
5.64
) and (
5.65
) by applying
Lemma

5.17
.

Corollary 5.21
.

Let
\gamma\in(1-\frac{\alpha}{2},1]
,
\varepsilon>0
.
There exists a constant
C={C(\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}},\alpha,\gamma,\varepsilon,d)>0}
such that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}

\displaystyle\|Err_{n,1}(s,t)\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}\leq C\|\varphi-\varphi^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{2}([s,t])}(t-s)^{1+\frac{\gamma-1}{\alpha}}+C[\varphi-\varphi^{n}]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{2}([s,t])}(t-s)^{\frac{3}{2}+\frac{\gamma-1}{\alpha}};

(5.68)

\displaystyle\|Err_{n,21}(s,t)\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}\leq Cn^{-1}(t-s)^{1+\frac{\gamma-1}{\alpha}}+Cn^{-\frac{1}{2}-(\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}\wedge\frac{1}{2})+\varepsilon}(t-s)^{\frac{3}{2}+\frac{\gamma-1}{\alpha}}.

(5.69)

Proof.

We apply
Lemma

5.17
with
m=2
,
\tau=\frac{1}{2}
,
\theta=1+\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}
. We see that condition (
5.50
) is satisfied by our standing assumption
\gamma>1-\frac{\alpha}{2}
.

First we take
\varphi
as before and
\psi:=\varphi^{n}
. Then by
Lemma

5.20
the stochastic regularity condition (
5.51
) is satisfied and we derive from (
5.17
) for any
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}

\|Err_{n,1}(s,t)\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}\leq C\|\varphi-\varphi^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{2}([s,t])}(t-s)^{1+\frac{\gamma-1}{\alpha}}+C[\varphi-\varphi^{n}]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{2}([s,t])}(t-s)^{\frac{3}{2}+\frac{\gamma-1}{\alpha}}

for
C=C(\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}},\alpha,\gamma,d)>0
, which is (
5.68
).

Next, we take now in
Lemma

5.17

\varphi^{n}
in place of
\varphi
and
\psi(t):=\varphi^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(t)}
and the same parameters
m,\tau,\theta
as above.
Lemma

5.20
guarantees that condition (
5.51
) holds and, very similarly, we get from (
5.17
) that for any
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}

\|Err_{n,21}(s,t)\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}\leq C\|\varphi^{n}-\varphi^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(\cdot)}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{2}([s,t])}(t-s)^{1+\frac{\gamma-1}{\alpha}}+C[\varphi^{n}-\varphi^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(\cdot)}]_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\frac{1}{2}}{L_{2}}([s,t])}(t-s)^{\frac{3}{2}+\frac{\gamma-1}{\alpha}}

(5.70)

for
C=C(\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}},\alpha,\gamma,d)>0
. Now let us work with the norms and seminorms on the right-hand side of the above inequality. By definition, we have

|\varphi^{n}_{r}-\varphi^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r)}|=(r-\kappa_{n}(r))|b(X^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r)})|\leq\frac{1}{n}\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}},\quad r\in[0,1].

Therefore

\|\varphi^{n}-\varphi^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(\cdot)}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{2}([s,t])}\leq\frac{1}{n}\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}.

(5.71)

Let us now bound
[\varphi^{n}-\varphi^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(\cdot)}]_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\frac{1}{2}}{L_{2}}([s,t])}
. This is exactly the point where it becomes clear why in
Lemma

5.17
we had to bound the left-hand side of (
5.17
) by a term involving the conditional seminorm
[\cdot]_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\tau}{L_{2}}([s,t])}
rather than the cruder Hölder seminorm
[\cdot]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{2}([s,t])}
, recall
Corollary

5.12
. Indeed, if

(r^{\prime},r)\in\Delta_{[s,t]}
are such that
\kappa_{n}(r)-\frac{1}{n}<r^{\prime}<\kappa_{n}(r)=r
, then

|(\varphi^{n}_{r}-\varphi^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r)})-(\varphi^{n}_{r^{\prime}}-\varphi^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r^{\prime})})|=(r^{\prime}-\kappa_{n}(r^{\prime}))|b(X^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r^{\prime})})|=(r^{\prime}-r+\frac{1}{n})|b(X^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r^{\prime})})|

and therefore

[\varphi^{n}-\varphi^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(\cdot)}]_{\mathcal{C}^{\tau}L_{2}([s,t])}=\infty

(5.72)

for any
\tau>0
. On the other hand, we have for any
(r^{\prime},r)\in\Delta_{[s,t]}

\|(\varphi^{n}_{r}-\varphi^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r)})-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{r^{\prime}}(\varphi^{n}_{r}-\varphi^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r)})\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}=(r-\kappa_{n}(r))\|b(X^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r)})-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{r^{\prime}}b(X^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r)})\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}.

(5.73)

If
\kappa_{n}(r)\leq r^{\prime}
then the left-hand side of the above identity is zero. Otherwise, if

r^{\prime}<\kappa_{n}(r)\leq r
, then by
Lemma

5.11
,

\displaystyle\|b(X^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r)})-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{r^{\prime}}b(X^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r)})\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq 2\|b(X^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r)})-b(X^{n}_{r^{\prime}})\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq 2\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}\||L_{\kappa_{n}(r)}-L_{r^{\prime}}|^{\gamma\wedge(\frac{\alpha}{2}-\varepsilon\alpha)}+|\varphi^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r)}-\varphi^{n}_{r^{\prime}}|^{\gamma}\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq 4\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}|\kappa_{n}(r)-r^{\prime}|^{(\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}\wedge\frac{1}{2})-\varepsilon}\leq 4\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}|r-r^{\prime}|^{(\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}\wedge\frac{1}{2})-\varepsilon}.

Here we used again that the function
\varphi^{n}
is Lipschitz. Substituting this into (
5.73
) and using that in this regime we have
r-\kappa_{n}(r)\leq(r-r^{\prime})\wedge\frac{1}{n}
, we get that for any
(r^{\prime},r)\in\Delta_{[s,t]}

\|(\varphi^{n}_{r}-\varphi^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r)})-\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{r^{\prime}}(\varphi^{n}_{r}-\varphi^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r)})\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}\leq 4\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(r-r^{\prime})^{\frac{1}{2}}n^{-\frac{1}{2}-(\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}\wedge\frac{1}{2})+\varepsilon},

which implies by the definition of the seminorm
[\cdot]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{2}([s,t])}
in (
5.37
)

[\varphi^{n}-\varphi^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(\cdot)}]_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\frac{1}{2}}{L_{2}}([s,t])}\leq 4\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}n^{-\frac{1}{2}-(\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}\wedge\frac{1}{2})+\varepsilon}.

This bound is much better than (
5.72
) and justifies the introduction of the conditional seminorm. Now we substitute this bound together with (
5.71
) into (
5.70
) and obtain the bound for the second error term (
5.69
).
∎

It remains to bound the term
Err_{n,22}
defined in (
5.65
). Recall that we already obtained a simpler bound in the case
\varphi\equiv 0
in
Theorem

5.9
. In fact, exactly the same bound remains valid in the general case. The idea is to combine the techniques used in the proof of
Theorem

5.9
with a shifted stochastic lemma. We present here only the final result and leave its proof as
5.33
.

Lemma 5.22
.

Let
\gamma\in(1-\frac{\alpha}{2},1]
,
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
.
There exists a constant
C={C(\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}},\alpha,\gamma,\varepsilon,d)>0}
such that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
we have

\|Err_{n,22}(s,t)\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}\leq Cn^{-\frac{1}{2}-(\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}\wedge\frac{1}{2})+2\varepsilon}(t-s)^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon}.

(5.74)

Now we can finally prove
Theorem

1.5
. We just need to combine the error bounds, substitute them into the error decompositions (
5.64
) and (
5.65
), and apply the tactics discussed before: first we bound
[\varphi-\varphi^{n}]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{2}([s,t])}
by half of itself and terms of the form
Cn^{-rate}
for small time intervals
t-s
, and then we use
Lemma

4.8
to pass to arbitrary time intervals.

Proof of
Theorem

1.5
.

We prove the theorem only in the case
m=2
and without the supremum over time inside the expectation. In the general case, one must consider more sophisticated norms and use the John–Nirenberg inequality, in the spirit of the proof of
Theorem

5.9
. To keep the proof relatively simple, we do not treat this extension here and refer the interested reader to
[
BDG22
]
for the full proof.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
such that
\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma-1}{\alpha}>\varepsilon
. For
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
, we combine (
5.64
), (
5.65
) and the error bounds (
5.68
), (
5.69
), (
5.74
) to derive

\displaystyle\|(\varphi_{t}-\varphi^{n}_{t})-(\varphi_{s}-\varphi^{n}_{s})\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\quad\leq C_{0}(t-s)^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}-(\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}\wedge\frac{1}{2})+2\varepsilon}+\|\varphi-\varphi^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{2}([s,t])}+[\varphi-\varphi^{n}]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{2}([s,t])})

for
C_{0}=C_{0}(\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}},\alpha,\gamma,\varepsilon,d)
.
Let now
0\leq S\leq T\leq 1
. Then dividing the above inequality by
(t-s)^{\frac{1}{2}}
and taking the supremum over all
s,t\in\Delta_{[S,T]}
we get

\displaystyle[\varphi-\varphi^{n}]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{2}([S,T])}

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}(T-S)^{\varepsilon}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}-(\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}\wedge\frac{1}{2})+2\varepsilon}+\|\varphi-\varphi^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{2}([S,T])}+[\varphi-\varphi^{n}]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{2}([S,T])})

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}(T-S)^{\varepsilon}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}-(\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}\wedge\frac{1}{2})+2\varepsilon}+\|\varphi_{S}-\varphi^{n}_{S}\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}+2[\varphi-\varphi^{n}]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{2}([S,T])}).

where in the last inequality we also used (
3.9
). Take now
\ell\in(0,1]
small enough so that

2C_{0}\ell^{\varepsilon}\leq\frac{1}{2}.

Then we derive for any
(S,T)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
such that
T-S\leq\ell

[\varphi-\varphi^{n}]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{2}([S,T])}\leq n^{-\frac{1}{2}-(\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}\wedge\frac{1}{2})+2\varepsilon}+\|\varphi_{S}-\varphi^{n}_{S}\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}.

By
Lemma

4.8
(ii), there exists a constant
C=C(\|b\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}},\alpha,\gamma,\varepsilon,d)
such that

[\varphi-\varphi^{n}]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{2}([0,1])}\leq Cn^{-\frac{1}{2}-(\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}\wedge\frac{1}{2})+2\varepsilon}.

Recalling that
\varphi_{0}=\varphi^{n}_{0}
, we get

\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\|X_{t}-X^{n}_{t}\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}=\|\varphi-\varphi^{n}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}L_{2}([0,1])}\leq[\varphi-\varphi^{n}]_{\mathcal{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{2}([0,1])}\leq Cn^{-\frac{1}{2}-(\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}\wedge\frac{1}{2})+2\varepsilon},

which is the desired bound.
∎

5.8
Exercises

Theorem

5.1
and
Theorem

1.4
provide convergence rates of the Euler scheme for SDEs with Hölder-continuous drifts. While in general these rates are known to be optimal
[
EMGY25
]
, finer properties of the drift, such as Besov regularity, can lead to better rates. The goal of the first group of exercises is to show that for an indicator drift in
d=1
, the correct rate is
n^{-3/4}
rather than
n^{-1/2}
as suggested by
Theorem

5.1
.
To establish this, we need a number of new tools, which are also of independent interest.

The idea is to establish an analogue of
Theorem

5.1
for Besov drifts in three steps; step 1 and step 2 are a modification of
[
DGL23
]
. First, we prove an analogue of (
5.2
) when
S
is large enough compared with
T-S
and
m=2
. Next, we extend the result to general
S
and
T
using the taming singularities technique. Finally, we apply the John-Nirenberg inequality to pass to arbitrary
m\geq 2
.

Exercise 5.23

(Taming singularities lemma,
[
BFG21
,
LL21
]
)
.

Let
(E,d)
be a metric space,
T>0
. Suppose there exist constants
\tau,\eta\geq 0
with
\tau>\eta
, and
\Gamma>0
, such that a function
Y\colon(0,T]\to E
satisfies

d(Y_{s},Y_{t})\leq\Gamma s^{-\eta}(t-s)^{\tau}\quad\text{for any $0<s\leq t\leq T$ such that $t-s\leq s$.}

Show that
Y
is can be continuously extended at
0
and there exists a constant
C=C(\eta,\tau,T)>0
such that

d(Y_{s},Y_{t})\leq C\Gamma(t-s)^{\tau-\eta}\quad\text{for any $0\leq s\leq t\leq T$.}

(5.75)

Hint
: First, prove (
5.75
) for
s=0
by splitting the interval
[0,t]
into subintervals
[t_{i+1},t_{i}]
, where
t_{0}=t
and
t_{i}\searrow 0
is a very carefully chosen sequence with
t_{n+1}\geq t_{n}-t_{n+1}
, and apply the triangle inequality.

We need to develop basic tools to deal with Besov functions of positive regularity. We start with the definition.

Definition 5.24

(see, e.g.,
[
BKP17
]
)
.

Let
p\in[1,\infty)
,
\gamma\in(0,1]
. We say that a function
f\in L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
belongs to the Besov (called also Nikolskii–Besov) space
\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
if

\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}:=\|f\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\sup_{h\in(0,1]}\frac{\|f(h+\cdot)-f\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}}{h^{\gamma}}.

Thus, Besov spaces extend the notion of Hölder spaces to the case
p<\infty
. Let us see in which Besov spaces the indicator function lies.

Exercise 5.25
.

Show that the indicator function
f(x):=\mathbbm{1}_{\{x\in[0,1]\}}
,
x\in\mathbb{R}
, belongs to
\mathcal{B}^{1/p}_{p}(\mathbb{R})
for any
p\in[1,\infty)
.

Next, we need to provide heat kernel estimates for Besov functions, extending
Lemma

5.3
.

Exercise 5.26
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
,
\gamma\in(0,1]
,
p\in[1,\infty)
,
0<s\leq t
. Show that

\|P_{t}f-P_{s}f\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}}s^{\frac{\gamma}{2}-1}(t-s),

for
C=C(\gamma,d,p)>0
.

Hint
: Argue as in the proofs of
Lemmas

3.1
and
5.3
. Use that by the definition of
\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}

\Bigl\|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Delta p_{t}(y)(f(\cdot-y)-f(\cdot))\,dy\Bigr\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\Delta p_{t}(y)||y|^{\gamma}\,dy.

5.26
allows us to improve an important estimate in the proof of
Theorem

5.1
. We used a very crude bound there involving
\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}
:

\|P_{t_{2}}f(W_{s})-P_{t_{1}}f(W_{s})\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq\|P_{t_{2}}f-P_{t_{1}}f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}(t_{2}-t_{1})t_{1}^{\frac{\gamma}{2}-1}.

for
0\leq t_{1}\leq t_{2}
,
s\in[0,1]
, see (
5.7
). As the next exercise shows,
\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}
can be replaced by
\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}}
at the cost of introducing a singularity (of which we should not be afraid!; recall the taming singularities lemma,
5.23
).

Exercise 5.27
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
,
\gamma\in(0,1]
,
p\in[1,\infty)
,
0<t_{1}\leq t_{2}
,
s\in(0,1]
. Show that

\|P_{t_{2}}f(W_{s})-P_{t_{1}}f(W_{s})\|_{L_{p}(\Omega)}\leq\|P_{t_{2}}f-P_{t_{1}}f\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|p_{s}\|_{L_{\infty}}^{1/p}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}}(t_{2}-t_{1})t_{1}^{\frac{\gamma}{2}-1}s^{-\frac{d}{2p}}.

for
C=C(\gamma,d,p)>0
.

Exercise 5.28
.

In this exercise we perform Step 1 of the proof strategy discussed above and establish an analogue of (
5.2
) for Besov
f
though for restricted values of
S,T
. Thus, let
\gamma\in(0,1]
,
p\in[2,\infty)
. Let
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be a bounded measurable function,
f\in\mathcal{B}_{p}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\varepsilon>0
. Suppose that
0<S<T\leq 1
,
T-S\leq S
. We would like to apply the SSL to the process

\mathcal{A}_{t}:=\int_{0}^{t}(f(W_{r})-f(W_{\kappa_{n}(r)}))\,dr,\quad t\in[S,T]

and the germ

A_{s.t}:=\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\int_{s}^{t}f(W_{r})-f(W_{\kappa_{n}(r)})\,dr,\quad(s,t)\in\Delta_{[S,T]}.

1.

Show that for any
(s,u,t)\in\Delta^{3}_{[S,T]}
, we have
\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\delta A_{s,u,t}=0
.

2.

First, assume that
S\in(0;\frac{2}{n}]
. Then
T-S\leq S\leq\frac{2}{n}
.

(iia)

Suppose that
\gamma\leq\frac{d}{p}
. Show that

|A_{s,t}|\leq 2\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}|t-s|\leq 8\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}|t-s|^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon}n^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{2}+\varepsilon}S^{-\frac{d}{2p}},\quad(s,t)\in\Delta_{[S,T]}.

(iib)

Suppose that
\gamma>\frac{d}{p}
. Argue exactly as in (
5.1
) to derive for
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[S,T]}

\|A_{s,t}\|_{L_{p}(\Omega)}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma-\frac{d}{p}}}n^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{2}+\frac{d}{2p}+\varepsilon}(t-s)^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}}n^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{2}+\varepsilon}(t-s)^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon}S^{-\frac{d}{2p}}.

Hint
: Use without the proof that by the Besov embedding theorem (see, e.g.,
[
BCD11
, Proposition 2.39]
) we have
\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma-\frac{d}{p}}}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}_{p}^{\gamma}}
for
C=C(\gamma,d,p)
.

3.

Now we consider the case
S\geq\frac{2}{n}
. Denote
s^{\prime}:=\kappa_{n}(s)+\frac{2}{n}
, that is
s^{\prime}
is the second gridpoint to the right of
s
. For
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[S,T]}
we split the term
A_{s,t}

\displaystyle\|A_{s,t}\|_{L_{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\int_{s}^{s^{\prime}\wedge t}\|f(W_{r})-f(W_{\kappa_{n}(r)})\|_{L_{p}(\Omega)}\,dr+\Bigl\|\int_{s^{\prime}\wedge t}^{t}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\big(f(W_{r})-f(W_{\kappa_{n}(r)})\big)dr\Bigr\|_{L_{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\quad=:I_{1}+I_{2}

(iiia)

Show that for any
r\in(0,1)

\|f(W_{r})-f(W_{\kappa_{n}(r)})\|_{L_{p}(\Omega)}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}}({\kappa_{n}(r)})^{-\frac{d}{2p}}(r-\kappa_{n}(r))^{\frac{\gamma}{2}},

for
C=C(\gamma,d,p)>0
.

Hint
: Use the definition of the space
\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}
and independence of
W_{\kappa_{n}(r)}
and
W_{r}-W_{\kappa_{n}(r)}
.

(iiib)

Deduce from
3(iiia)
that for
C=C(\gamma,d,p)>0

I_{1}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}}((s^{\prime}\wedge t)-s)n^{-\frac{\gamma}{2}}S^{-\frac{d}{2p}}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}}n^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{2}+\varepsilon}(t-s)^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon}S^{-\frac{d}{2p}}.

Hint
: Use that
\kappa_{n}(r)\geq S-\frac{1}{n}\geq S/2
.

(iiic)

Show that either
s^{\prime}\wedge t=t
and then
I_{2}\equiv 0
, or
s^{\prime}\wedge t=s^{\prime}
and then
t\geq s^{\prime}\geq s+\frac{1}{n}
and for
C=C(\gamma,\varepsilon,d,p)>0

I_{2}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}}|t-s|^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon}n^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{2}+2\varepsilon}S^{-\frac{d}{2p}}.

Hint
: Argue as in (
5.30
) and use
5.27
when needed.

(iiid)

Conclude that for
C=C(\gamma,\varepsilon,d,p)>0

\|A_{s,t}\|_{L_{p}(\Omega)}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{p}}|t-s|^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon}n^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{2}+2\varepsilon}S^{-\frac{d}{2p}},\quad(s,t)\in\Delta_{[S,T]}.

4.

Deduce from parts (i)–(iii) that all the conditions of the SSL are satisfied and therefore for any
0<S<T\leq 1
,
T-S\leq S
one has

\Bigl\|\int_{S}^{T}(f(W_{r})-f(W_{\kappa_{n}(r)}))\,dr\Bigr\|_{L_{p}(\Omega)}\leq C(\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}+\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}_{p}^{\gamma}})(T-S)^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon}n^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{2}+2\varepsilon}S^{-\frac{d}{2p}}.

for
C=C(\gamma,\varepsilon,d,p)>0
independent of
S
,
T
,
n
.

Exercise 5.29
.

We proceed to Step 2 of the proof. Let
\gamma\in(0,1]
,
p\in[2,\infty)
,
\varepsilon>0
,
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be a bounded measurable function. Assume additionally that
d/p\leq 1
. Use the taming singularities lemma (
5.23
), to derive from
5.28
4
that there exists a constant
C=C(\gamma,\varepsilon,d,p)>0
such that for any
(S,T)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\Bigl\|\int_{S}^{T}(f(W_{r})-f(W_{\kappa_{n}(r)}))\,dr\Bigr\|_{L_{p}(\Omega)}\leq C(\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}+\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}_{p}^{\gamma}})(T-S)^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{d}{2p}+\varepsilon}n^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{2}+2\varepsilon}.

Exercise 5.30
.

Finally, we can now get the main estimate: bound on convergence rate of integral functionals of Brownian motion for Besov functions. Let
\gamma\in(0,1]
,
p\in[2,\infty)
,
\varepsilon>0
,
d/p\leq 1
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be a bounded measurable function.

1.

Let
s\in[0,1]
be a gridpoint, that is,
\kappa_{n}(s)=s
. Deduce from
5.29
that for any
t\in[s,1]
one has

\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s}\Bigl|\int_{s}^{t}(f(W_{r})-f(W_{\kappa_{n}(r)}))\,dr\Bigr|\leq C(\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}+\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}_{p}^{\gamma}})(t-s)^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{d}{2p}+\varepsilon}n^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{2}+2\varepsilon}

(5.76)

for
C=C(\gamma,\varepsilon,d,p)>0
independent of
n
.

Hint
: Use the independence of the increments of
W
and that
\kappa_{n}(r)-s=\kappa_{n}(r-s)
since
s
is a gridpoint.

2.

Use part
1
to show that (
5.76
) holds for arbitrary
(s,t)\in\Delta_{[0,1]}
.

Hint
: If
s
is not a gridpoint, split the integral
\int_{s}^{t}=\int_{s}^{s^{\prime}}+\int_{s^{\prime}}^{t}
, where
s^{\prime}=\kappa_{n}(s)+\frac{1}{n}
and argue as in (
5.34
).

3.

Use the John-Nirenberg inequality (
Theorem

5.6
) to deduce from part
2
that for any
m\geq 1

there exists a constant
C=C(\gamma,\varepsilon,d,p,m)>0
such that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
one has

\Bigl\|\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\Bigl|\int_{0}^{t}(f(W_{r})-f(W_{\kappa_{n}(r)}))\,dr\Bigr|\,\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq C(\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}+\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}_{p}^{\gamma}})n^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{2}+2\varepsilon}

Exercise 5.31
.

Finally, let us give an illustration of the just-obtained result.
Let
d=1
. Show that for any
m\geq 1
,
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a constant
C=C(\varepsilon,m)>0
such that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
one has

\Bigl\|\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\Bigl|\int_{0}^{t}(\mathbbm{1}(W_{r}\in[0,1])-\mathbbm{1}(W_{\kappa_{n}(r)}\in[0,1]))\,dr\Bigr|\,\Bigr\|_{L_{m}(\Omega)}\leq Cn^{-\frac{3}{4}+\varepsilon}.

Hint
: Use
5.25
.

Thus, while
Theorem

5.1
provided the rate of convergence for the indicator functions of order
Cn^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, we see that more sophisticated considerations allow us to improve the rate to
Cn^{-3/4}
, which is known to be optimal
[
ALG22
]
.

Exercise 5.32
.

In this exercise we prove

. Let

Show that for any

|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt(f(L_{1}+x)-f(L_{1}+y))|\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}|x-y|\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}|x-y|.

You can use without proof that

,

).

,

,

one has

. Deduce from part (iii) that there exists

) up to the loss of arbitrary

.

Exercise 5.33
.

In this exercise, we prove
Lemma

5.22
. The main idea is to apply the shifted stochastic sewing lemma (

) to the process

\mathcal{A}_{t}:=\int_{0}^{t}(b(L_{r}+\varphi^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r)})-b(L_{\kappa_{n}(r)}+\varphi^{n}_{\kappa_{n}(r)}))\,dr,\quad t\in[S,T].

Motivated by the discussion in

To simplify the notation, denote

,

Show that the germ satisfies

.

and use (

).

,

denote

,

, recall

. Show that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s-(t-s)}\delta A_{s,u,t}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt-b(L_{r}+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{2}}\psi_{r})+b(L_{\kappa_{n}(r)}+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{2}}\psi_{r})\,dr

\displaystyle\quad+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{1}}\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{3}}\int_{s_{4}}^{s_{5}}b(L_{r}+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{1}}\psi_{r})-b(L_{\kappa_{n}(r)}+\hskip 0.6458pt\mathsf{E}\hskip 0.43057pt^{s_{1}}\psi_{r})
